# Structure note: "8 Apple Varieties Sold in Canada To NEVER EAT Again! (Only 3 Are ACTUALLY Worth Buying)"

Prepared 29 Sep 2026 from five transcripts and the primary sources listed in section 0. All word counts are measured by script (beat-boundary anchors on the cleaned transcript, `[music]`, `[clears throat]` and `>>` stripped), not estimated. Every fact in sections 3 and 4 carries the URL that was actually opened and the date opened (29 Sep 2026 unless stated). Anything that could not be opened at tier (a)/(b)/(d) is in section 5, UNVERIFIED / DO-NOT-USE, and must not appear in the script.

House rules applied throughout (hard): no health, nutrition, antioxidant, sugar, glycemic, pesticide-residue, "healthier", vitamin or food-safety claims of any kind; tiers (a) government/regulator/court/company-or-association-own-site/breeder-or-licensor-own-page, (b) named outlet with byline and date, (c) aggregator/SEO/Wikipedia/review/Reddit/Trustpilot/menu-price sites = flag only, (d) named consumer survey with publisher, year, methodology; verbatim quotes in quotation marks with URL; corporate and association claims attributed; prices only from a retailer's own site/app/flyer or a dated (b) report with date, banner, region, $/kg and $/lb, and origin as displayed; never state or imply wrongdoing; no U.S. data presented as Canadian; taste is opinion.

## 1. Measured skeletons

### 1a. Channel, "8 Canadian Apple Varieties To NEVER EAT Again" (Feb 2026, 61.5K, 6.8x median), 3,198 w

Cold open, 161 w (5.0%), plus a 14-word like/subscribe ask at 5.0%. Six moves: (1) a 15-word clipped news-audio hook that is not the channel's voice ("Well, it is one of Minnesota's most popular inventions. We're talking about the honey crisp apple."); (2) the "not as healthy as you think" reversal; (3) the thesis, entirely nutrition ("bred for sweetness over nutrition, engineered to last months in controlled atmosphere storage, and selected for appearance rather than health benefits"); (4) the mechanism sentence ("Because apples are eaten with the skin, where most beneficial compounds concentrate..."); (5) the count and promise ("eight apple varieties you should think twice about buying in Canada and two varieties that are genuinely better choices"); (6) the ask ("Before we start, make sure to like this video and subscribe to the channel."). No dated fact anywhere in the open. No stated basis for the order.

Count and order: 8 to 1 as a countdown, then 2 worth-it counted 2 to 1. The basis is never stated; #1 is "the most disappointing nutritional value of any apple on this list," which rests on one 2003 study. The audience can and did ask why Honeycrisp, which the script itself calls the premium apple, sits at #8 (mildest) while Empire, which it calls "reasonable" in price, is #1 (worst).

Beats (words, share, start position):

| Beat | Words | Share | Starts at |
|---|---|---|---|
| Cold open | 161 | 5.0% | 0.0% |
| Open ask (like + subscribe) | 14 | 0.4% | 5.0% |
| #8 Honeycrisp | 330 | 10.3% | 5.5% |
| #7 Red Delicious | 195 | 6.1% | 15.8% |
| #6 Spartan | 187 | 5.8% | 21.9% |
| #5 Cortland | 200 | 6.3% | 27.7% |
| #4 Granny Smith | 164 | 5.1% | 34.0% |
| #3 Fuji | 312 | 9.8% | 39.1% |
| #2 Ambrosia | 335 | 10.5% | 48.9% |
| #1 Empire | 335 | 10.5% | 59.3% |
| Turn | 22 | 0.7% | 69.8% |
| Worth-it #2 Gala | 334 | 10.4% | 70.5% |
| Worth-it #1 McIntosh | 419 | 13.1% | 81.0% |
| Close, moral | 119 | 3.7% | 94.1% |
| Close ask (like, subscribe, share) | 71 | 2.2% | 97.8% |

There is no mid-list ask. The only asks are at 5.0% and 97.8%. The middle four beats (#7 to #4) are short, 164–200 words, and the script is bottom-heavy: the two worth-it beats total 753 words (23.5%), more than any two avoid beats.

Per-beat anatomy, measured inside the three longest beats:

Honeycrisp, #8 (330 w): bridge 21 ("We are starting at number eight with a variety that dominates Canadian supermarket shelves, but comes with hidden concerns. Honey Crisp."); origin and breeder 34 ("developed at the University of Minnesota and released in 1991... described as explosively crisp by its creators"); market growth 37 (a North American per-capita figure, unsourced); price 17 ("organic Honey Crisp receiving approximately 60% higher prices," unsourced); accusation 91 (disease, "frequent applications," thin skin, residues, all rule-(a) breaches); nutrition 51 (phenolics, the AAFC study); storage and vitamins 40; closer 39 ("that premium reflects growing difficulty and consumer demand, not nutritional superiority"). Of 330 words, 182 (55%) are nutrition or residue commentary.

Empire, #1 (335 w): bridge 32; origin and breeder 48 ("a cross, made by orchardist Lester Anderson in the Hudson Valley with the seedlings sent to Cornell's Geneva Station for evaluation in 1945 and released in 1966"); Canadian market 37 ("popular in Ontario and Quebec, where they are harvested in September and October and stored through winter"); accusation 109 (the Tsao study, "ranked dead last," epicatechin, procyanidin B2, "60% of antioxidant activity"); price framing 41 ("often marketed as a premium choice... Canadian consumers pay reasonable prices"); closer 68 ("When you pick up an Empire apple at Loblaws, Metro or Sobeys, you are choosing the variety that ranked at the absolute bottom of government antioxidant testing... the worst value proposition on the market"). 218 of 335 words (65%) are nutrition.

McIntosh, worth-it #1 (419 w): bridge 17; origin 51 ("discovered as a chance seedling by John McIntosh in 1811 on his farm in Dundela, Ontario"); cultural recognition 27 (silver dollar, "over 40% of the commercial Canadian apple market," both unsourced); nutrition block 59; season and freshness 55; taste description 58 ("white, tender, and juicy with a sweet tart flavor that hints at spice"); concession 49 ("Yes, Macintosh apples are softer than modern engineered varieties and can bruise... This is actually a sign of authenticity"); genetics 24; residue and safety 52 (CFIA, Health Canada, "100% of apple products tested"); closer 27. The concession-then-reframe move (softer = authentic) is the one structural device in this beat worth keeping; everything else in it is either nutrition, taste or unsourced.

Asks: open 14 w at 5.0% ("make sure to like this video and subscribe"); close 71 w at 97.8% ("make sure to like the video, subscribe for more food investigations and ingredient breakdowns, and share this with someone who eats apples every day thinking they are all the same"). No comment ask anywhere.

The turn, 22 w at 69.8%: "All right, enough about what to avoid. Let us talk about two apple varieties that actually deserve your attention and your dollars." No disclosure, no basis, no bridge logic.

Close (190 w): moral, 119 w, restates the nutrition thesis ("the prettiest apple on the shelf is not necessarily the healthiest one in your hand") and gives three pieces of advice ("Prioritize varieties with balanced flavor profiles. Seek out Canadian grown options when available. and understand that..."); then the ask, 71 w; sign-off "Small choices add up when it comes to food, and the apple you reach for tomorrow could make a real difference to your health over time. Thanks for watching." No "How to protect yourself," no tagline, no disclaimer, no comment prompt.

What February did that the new script keeps: an origin line with a breeder and a date in every beat; the Ontario/Quebec/BC growing-region sentence in most beats; the concession-then-reframe in the McIntosh beat; the "Seek out Canadian grown options when available" instruction (now the whole thesis rather than an afterthought); 8 avoid then a numbered worth-it list.

### 1b. Tasted & Tested, "8 Apple Varieties RANKED Worst to Best" (124K), 2,467 w

Cold open 138 w (5.6%): accusation first ("Here's what the apple industry doesn't want you to know. Most of the apples you're buying are bred for profit, not flavor."), one named villain in the open ("The red delicious apple, once America's sweetheart, has been systematically bred into mediocrity"), the promise ("rank them worst to best because life's too short to waste money on mealy, flavorless fruit"), then a 16-word subscribe ask at 5.6%. No dated fact.

Count and order: 8 to 1, "worst to best," and unlike February the basis is stated (flavour, an opinion) and the list runs past "avoid" into "best," so #1 is a recommendation, not a villain. Beats grow toward the end.

| Beat | Words | Share | Starts at |
|---|---|---|---|
| Cold open | 138 | 5.6% | 0.0% |
| Open ask | 16 | 0.6% | 5.6% |
| #8 Red Delicious | 239 | 9.7% | 6.2% |
| #7 Golden Delicious | 216 | 8.8% | 15.9% |
| #6 McIntosh | 230 | 9.3% | 24.7% |
| #5 Granny Smith | 305 | 12.4% | 34.0% |
| #4 Gala | 238 | 9.6% | 46.4% |
| Ask 1 (subscribe) | 25 | 1.0% | 56.0% |
| #3 Fuji | 267 | 10.8% | 57.0% |
| Ask 2 (like) | 20 | 0.8% | 67.9% |
| #2 Honeycrisp | 278 | 11.3% | 68.7% |
| #1 SweeTango | 338 | 13.7% | 79.9% |
| Close | 125 | 5.1% | 93.6% |
| Close ask (subscribe + comment) | 32 | 1.3% | 98.7% |

Per-beat anatomy, Red Delicious (239 w): bridge 4 ("Number eight, Red Delicious."); origin 36 ("Discovered in 1880 by Jesse Hyatt in Iowa"); taste attack 63; price 13 ("At $1.26 per pound," U.S., unsourced); review quotes 28 ("Makes me regret having taste buds"); history 58 ("Washington orchards are now ripping out Red Delicious trees"); closer 37 ("a monument to style over substance"). The beat formula is: origin, what you get, price, reviews, "they're banking on you...", closer. Every beat ends on the "they're banking on you" indictment device: "They're banking on you grabbing the prettiest apple without tasting it first" (Red Delicious); "They're banking on you buying it in October when it's decent" (McIntosh); "They're banking on you buying it for pies" (Granny Smith); "They're not banking on volume. They're banking on delivering the best eating experience" (SweeTango, inverted). That inversion at #1 is the video's turn.

Asks: four (5.6%, 56.0%, 67.9%, 98.7%). The two mid-list asks bracket #3 Fuji: "If this is already saving you from a disappointing snack, do me a favor and hit that subscribe button" and "if you're finding this ranking useful, hit the like button. It really helps the channel cut through the noise."

Close (157 w): the lesson as a pair of negations ("Don't trust the prettiest apple in the bin. Don't assume expensive means good or cheap means bad."), the villain restated ("stop buying Red Delicious just because it's red and called delicious. That's the biggest lie in the produce section."), the rule ("taste before you trust"), subscribe ask, comment ask with a fight built in ("drop a comment telling us which ranking you think we got wrong because I guarantee the Granny Smith fans are coming for..."). No disclaimer.

Rule breaches, for the record: every beat asserts texture and flavour as fact (rule 8); prices are U.S. $/lb with no store or date (rule 5); "customer reviews," "food editors," "critics," "blind taste tests" are unnamed (tier c); "Production is up 17%... Only 60% yield compared to 85% for Cosmic Crisp" is unsourced. Nothing transfers except the comment-ask-with-a-fight and the stated order basis.

### 1c. Tasted & Tested, "6 Apple Varieties To Skip And 3 That Are Actually Grown For Flavor" (28K), 2,071 w

Cold open 181 w (8.7%): opens on a figure ("Every year, the average American eats about 65 apples. That is roughly $80 a year"), the mechanism promise ("sitting in a controlled atmosphere warehouse for over 12 months"), the regulator line ("The United States Department of Agriculture grades apples as extra fancy, fancy, and number one based entirely on color, shape, and skin condition. Flavor is not part of the grading system."), then a 25-word credibility claim at 8.7% ("I spent over 40 hours auditing every major apple variety"). No ask in the open.

Structure is explainer-first and the list is only a third of the words:

| Beat | Words | Share | Starts at |
|---|---|---|---|
| Cold open | 181 | 8.7% | 0.0% |
| "I spent 40 hours" | 25 | 1.2% | 8.7% |
| Mechanism: follow the money | 93 | 4.5% | 9.9% |
| Mechanism: CA storage | 79 | 3.8% | 14.4% |
| Mechanism: wax | 64 | 3.1% | 18.3% |
| "The skip list" header | 15 | 0.7% | 21.3% |
| #1 Red Delicious | 114 | 5.5% | 22.1% |
| #2 Gala | 120 | 5.8% | 27.6% |
| #3 Fuji | 123 | 5.9% | 33.4% |
| #4 McIntosh | 112 | 5.4% | 39.3% |
| #5 Golden Delicious | 127 | 6.1% | 44.7% |
| #6 Granny Smith | 101 | 4.9% | 50.8% |
| Three tests (snap, aroma, PLU + origin) | 227 | 11.0% | 55.7% |
| "The real list" header | 25 | 1.2% | 66.7% |
| Worth-it #1 Honeycrisp | 178 | 8.6% | 67.9% |
| Worth-it #2 Cosmic Crisp | 160 | 7.7% | 76.5% |
| Worth-it #3 SweeTango | 177 | 8.5% | 84.2% |
| Close | 132 | 6.4% | 92.8% |
| Close ask | 18 | 0.9% | 99.1% |

Count and order: 6 skip counted up 1 to 6 (no ranking claimed), then 3 worth-it counted 1 to 3 with the strongest first ("If you buy one variety from this list, make it this one"). The order basis is never stated.

Per-beat anatomy, Red Delicious (114 w): bridge 15; origin 36 ("Originally discovered in Iowa in 1872"); market share 13 ("By the 1990s, it accounted for 75% of Washington state's entire apple production," unsourced); marketing versus reality 36 ("The marketing calls it sweet and crisp. The reality is..."); closer 14 ("This apple is not grown for your mouth. It is grown for a camera."). Every skip beat runs the same five jobs in 100–127 words: bridge, origin, one market fact, marketing-versus-reality, one-line closer.

The three tests block (227 w at 55.7%) is the video's structural centre and the only part that transfers to a Canadian, non-nutrition script: intro 18; snap test 65; aroma test 72; PLU and origin 72 ("check the PLU sticker. That four-digit code identifies the exact variety... More importantly, check the country of origin. If you are buying an apple in North America in October, and it was grown in Chile or New Zealand, that fruit has been traveling and sitting in storage for months."). The snap and aroma tests are taste (rule 8); the PLU-plus-origin test is a label check and is the seed of the new script's "How to protect yourself."

Asks: one, 18 w at 99.1% ("The food industry lies because you let it. Subscribe because you have the right to know the truth."). No like ask, no comment ask.

Close (150 w): summary pair ("Six varieties engineered for warehouses... Three varieties that prove..."), the regulator restated ("the USDA lets them stamp it extra fancy the entire time"), the rule as a triplet ("flip the apple over, smell it, snap it, check the sticker"), the ask.

Lesson: this is a U.S. video (USDA, "the average American," Washington growers, $/lb) and cannot be borrowed for figures. What transfers is the shape of the grade line, which has a Canadian equivalent at tier (a) (see section 3, S30): Canada's apple grades rest on colour, size and defects, and no grade names flavour. It also shows the worth-it beats can be as short as 160–180 words when each carries one breeder fact, one ownership fact and one price line.

### 1d. Channel, ketchup (88K), 3,299 w, the current construction

Cold open 237 w (7.2%): the object in the viewer's own fridge, then a dated national event in the third sentence ("In 2016, this country fought a war over ketchup."), a cascade of dated, specific consequences ("A Facebook post from a construction worker in Orillia got a quarter of a million shares. A grocery giant surrendered to public fury in 24 hours."), the method claim ("we read every label in the ketchup aisle. That's all we did."), three reveals promised as facts ("ingredient lists are, word for word, identical. Both of their parent companies are American. There's a federal law that says ketchup must contain sugar."), the count and order basis stated ("Eight brands, biggest first, three genuinely Canadian winners at the end."), and the sign-on ("Elbows up. Let's flip the bottles."). No ask in the open.

Count and order: eight brands "biggest first," so the order basis is stated on screen in the open and the biggest brand carries the longest beat. Then a separate winners list 3-2-1 that re-uses three of the eight.

| Beat | Words | Share | Starts at |
|---|---|---|---|
| Cold open | 237 | 7.2% | 0.0% |
| #1 Heinz | 567 | 17.2% | 7.2% |
| Like ask | 35 | 1.1% | 24.4% |
| #2 French's | 648 | 19.6% | 25.4% |
| "60 seconds of ketchup law" | 348 | 10.5% | 45.1% |
| #3–7 store brands, as a block | 381 | 11.5% | 55.6% |
| #8 Primo | 131 | 4.0% | 67.2% |
| Winners 3-2-1 | 308 | 9.3% | 71.1% |
| Close: "the ending this story earned" | 486 | 14.7% | 80.5% |
| Checklist | 109 | 3.3% | 95.2% |
| Comment ask + sign-off | 49 | 1.5% | 98.5% |

Per-beat anatomy, Heinz (567 w): bridge 13; recognition and history 82 ("Heinz opened its first Canadian plant in Leamington, Ontario in 1909... For a century, that plant was the town."); ownership plus dated event 71 ("Then in 2013 Warren Buffett's Berkshire Hathaway and a Brazilian private equity firm bought Heinz. And on November 14th, 2013, the axe fell." with Buffett quoted verbatim); numbers and consequence 58 ("225,000 tons of tomatoes a year at $93 to $95 a ton... 740 jobs"); the rescue 74 ("That same day, a rescue consortium called Highbury Canco took over the plant... this is CBC's line, not ours"); return to Canada 55 ("The first Canadian bottle rolled off that line on July 7th, 2021."); the twist 122 ("The tomato paste in every bottle of Canadian-made Heinz ketchup today comes exclusively, their word, from Highbury Canco"); latest event 51 ("In September 2025, Kraft Heinz announced it's splitting into two companies"); label and price 41 ("$6.50 to $7 a liter at time of filming"). Ten dated events in one beat; two verbatim quotes attributed to a named person and a named outlet; one price with a time stamp.

Ask: one like ask, 35 w at 24.4%, placed at the end of the biggest beat and tied to the story ("If you remember picking a side in 2016, and if you're Canadian, you do, hit the like button right now because the next chapter is the one you live through, and we found the receipts."). No subscribe ask anywhere. Comment ask at 98.5% only.

The turn: winners 3-2-1 at 71.1% ("So, the three that are actually Canadian in a video full of Canadian ketchup"), each winner 70–110 words, each restating ownership, factory, origin and $/litre.

Close (644 w from 80.5%): intro 6; the Statistics Canada price series 57 ("Statistics Canada has tracked the average price of a liter of ketchup since 2017. $3.12 then. A peak of $4.95 in October 2025. $4.62 as of June. Up 48%"); the harvest consequence 75; dated 2025 trade-war events 189; survey receipts 79 (Angus Reid, NielsenIQ, both named); fine print on victory 80 ("Heinz's bottles say, 'Prepared in Canada.' Not product of Canada. Because under federal rules, product of Canada requires about 98% Canadian content."); checklist 109 ("So here's the checklist, and it's short. Flip the bottle and read the origin line..."); comment ask and sign-off 42 ("Tell us in the comments which side your family took in 2016... we'll see you at the next label"). No disclaimer.

What transfers to apples: the dated fact in the opening sentences; the stated order basis in the open; ownership-and-origin as the thesis rather than health; a government price series quoted with dates in the close; the origin-label fine print as the checklist's first item; the comment ask framed as a personal memory.

### 1e. Channel, jam (80K), 3,271 w, the current construction

Cold open 178 w (5.4%): the aisle described as a deception ("one of the most misleading stretches of shelf space in the entire building"), the three-part imagery indictment (gingham lids, orchard imagery, handwritten fonts), the count ("Seven brands on the avoid list today, two that earned genuine worth it placements"), and three teasers each carrying a fact (horse-drawn wagon, "sells in over 125 countries," a First World War sweetening method). No ask, no dated fact in the open.

Count and order: seven avoid, unnumbered ("The brand that defined... The second brand... The third brand..."), followed by two worth-it; no stated order basis; the "final brand on the avoid list" is flagged as the one "that will generate the most disagreement," which is the video's tension device.

| Beat | Words | Share | Starts at |
|---|---|---|---|
| Cold open | 178 | 5.4% | 0.0% |
| A1 Smucker's | 353 | 10.8% | 5.4% |
| A2 E.D. Smith | 306 | 9.4% | 16.2% |
| A3 No Name | 247 | 7.6% | 25.6% |
| A4 Compliments | 249 | 7.6% | 33.1% |
| A5 Bonne Maman | 308 | 9.4% | 40.8% |
| Subscribe ask (with a "what this investigation is" note) | 112 | 3.4% | 50.2% |
| A6 Kraft | 246 | 7.5% | 53.6% |
| A7 Greaves | 368 | 11.3% | 61.1% |
| W1 Crofter's | 353 | 10.8% | 72.4% |
| W2 St. Dalfour | 403 | 12.3% | 83.2% |
| Close | 148 | 4.5% | 95.5% |

Per-beat anatomy, Smucker's (353 w): bridge 38; origin 92 ("founded in 1897 by Jerome Monroe Smucker... incorporated in 1921. By 1980... over 25% of the American market"); Canadian context 39; accusation 99 (the sweetener list); the company's own website 45 ("The company's own website acknowledges that some of its fruit spread products contain high fructose corn syrup... A quiet admission"); closer 40 ("The horse-drawn wagon origin story has been marketing for a long time. The ingredient list is the actual product."). The origin block is a quarter of the beat and is where the brand's own mythology is set up so the closer can knock it down.

Greaves (368 w): bridge 48; origin 97 ("founded in 1927 by Mabel and William Greaves in Niagara on the Lake... 55 Queen Street in a building that dates to approximately 1845"); concession first 51 ("The avoid placement is not about authenticity or craftsmanship. Greaves is the most authentically crafted product on this entire avoid list."); accusation 81; price 44 ("among the most expensive on the shelf"); closer 47 ("Greaves asks more of the wallet than the formulation justifies"). The concession-before-accusation order is the model for the new script's Canadian-grown avoid beats.

Ask: one, 112 w at 50.2%, and it is the longest ask on the channel because it doubles as the credibility statement ("the research behind this channel is sourced, fact-checked, and independent of any brand relationship or advertiser revenue. If this investigation is useful to you, subscribe now and turn on notifications."). No like ask, no comment ask, no share ask.

Turn: "Two brands earned the worth it placement" at 72.4%, then each worth-it beat runs origin (a couple, a place, a year), ownership changes with dates ("Frontenac acquired a majority stake in February 2021. In January 2026, Power Sustainable Lios... acquired Crofter's"), and a closer that separates the verdict from the ownership ("the worth it placement is for that formulation, not for any particular ownership structure").

Close (148 w): the aisle thesis restated, three brand call-backs in one sentence each, and the rule ("Reading the ingredient list is the only honest way to navigate this shelf. Fruit first, then everything that comes after it."). No ask, no disclaimer, no comment prompt.

Rule breaches to avoid inheriting: the jam script's accusations are sugar-content and sweetener claims, so its accusation device (rule a) does not transfer; "the healthiest recommendation on this list" appears twice; "30 calories per serving with 1/3 less sugar" is nutrition. What transfers is the shape: origin block with a founder and a date, ownership chain with dates, the company's own words, concession first for a Canadian brand, a closer that names what the brand's imagery claims and what the record shows.

### 1f. Cross-format observations

| Video | Asks (count) | Positions | Mid-list ask? | Comment ask? | Order basis stated? | Dated fact in open? | Disclaimer? |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Feb apples (61K) | 2 | 5.0%, 97.8% | no | no | no | no | no |
| TT ranked (124K) | 4 | 5.6%, 56.0%, 67.9%, 98.7% | two | yes | yes (flavour, opinion) | no | no |
| TT skip (28K) | 1 | 99.1% | no | no | no | figure, not dated | no |
| Ketchup (88K) | 2 | 24.4% (like), 98.5% (comment) | after beat 1 | yes | yes ("biggest first") | yes ("In 2016") | no |
| Jam (80K) | 1 | 50.2% | yes | no | no | no | no |

The channel's two current hits both put the ask inside the list, both open on the object in the viewer's own kitchen, and the better of the two (ketchup) states the order basis in the open and opens on a dated event. February apples did none of those. The recommended script carries exactly one mid-list ask at 61–63% (the band the fried-chicken structure note documents for the channel's three restaurant videos) and one comment ask in the close, which is the ketchup form.

---

## 2. Every February line the new script must not repeat

Tags: (a) nutrition/antioxidant/sugar/pesticide/food-safety, the hard rule; (U) unsourced figure; (US) U.S. or North American figure presented as Canadian; (t) taste or texture asserted as fact (rule 8); (c) tier-(c) source or "consumers say"; (x) factually wrong against a source opened for this note.

### 2a. Nutrition, antioxidant, sugar, pesticide and food-safety lines (rule a): 66 lines, all barred

Cold open
1. "might not be as healthy as you think" (a)
2. "Apples are often seen as one of the healthiest fruits in the world. But what most Canadians do not realize is that not all apples are equally good for you." (a)
3. "bred for sweetness over nutrition... selected for appearance rather than health benefits" (a)
4. "Because apples are eaten with the skin, where most beneficial compounds concentrate, choosing the wrong variety means missing out on the antioxidants and phytonutrients that make apples worth eating." (a)

#8 Honeycrisp
5. "notoriously difficult to grow and highly susceptible to diseases, including bitter pit, fungal infections, and pest damage" (a-adjacent; leads into residue)
6. "Research from Maritime Canada examining Honey Crisp production found that growers often rely on frequent applications throughout the growing season to maintain the visual quality consumers expect." (a, pesticide; U)
7. "The relatively thin skin of Honey Crisp apples allows any residues to remain on the surface. And since Canadians typically eat apples unpeeled, this increases exposure unless the fruit is organically sourced." (a, pesticide)
8. "separate research has found Honey Crisp contains moderate to high levels of certain phenolic compounds, the variety was bred primarily for texture and flavor rather than nutritional content" (a; U)
9. "Honey Crisp was not included in the Landmark Agriculture and Agri Food Canada antioxidant study that compared eight popular Canadian varieties" (a; also unverifiable: the abstract opened, S35, does not list the eight cultivars)
10. "during long storage, natural vitamin levels slowly degrade while treatments are applied to control moisture loss and browning" (a)
11. "that premium reflects growing difficulty and consumer demand, not nutritional superiority" (a)

#7 Red Delicious
12. "nutritionally misleading" (a)
13. "Agriculture and Agri Food Canada's research found Red Delicious to have high antioxidant levels in controlled testing" (a)
14. "The Canadian Food Inspection Agency permits this extended storage, but nutritional integrity declines over time." (a; and the CFIA claim is U)
15. "Natural vitamin levels decrease during storage and post-harvest treatments maintain appearance without preserving nutrients." (a)
16. "This texture reflects reduced moisture content, which correlates with diminished nutrient retention." (a, t)
17. "The thin skin of Red Delicious also absorbs post-harvest treatments more readily than thicker skinned varieties. Because Canadians eat apples with the peel intact, any surface residues are consumed directly." (a, pesticide)
18. "practical nutritional value" (a)

#6 Spartan
19. "extended storage times are associated with vitamin degradation and require calcium dip treatments to prevent storage breakdown. These practices maintain appearance but do not preserve nutritional value." (a; the calcium dips are real per S20 but the framing is nutrition)
20. "Spartan apples are also highly susceptible to disease which historically required intensive crop protection measures during growing." (a, pesticide; x: S20 says Spartan "is much less susceptible [to scab] than 'McIntosh'" and "is usually free of bitter pit, scald and core flush")

#5 Cortland
21. "a variety that sounds healthier than it actually is" (a)
22. "In the Agriculture and Agri Food Canada study on antioxidant levels, Courtland ranked in the middle of the pack, significantly below Red Delicious, Northern Spy, and Ida Red varieties." (a)
23. "Other readily available options deliver substantially more protective antioxidants per serving." (a)
24. "this characteristic has nothing to do with nutritional superiority. It simply means the flesh oxidizes more slowly." (a)
25. "this softness becomes more pronounced with storage, often correlating with diminished nutrient retention" (a, t)

#4 Granny Smith
26. "many Canadians assume is healthier because it tastes less sweet. This assumption is dangerous because lower sweetness does not automatically translate to higher nutritional value." (a)
27. "the tart taste does not mean nutritional superiority" (a)
28. "Many beneficial polyphenols in apples are concentrated in richly colored skins... The green skin of Granny Smith apples lacks the anthocyanins found in darker skinned varieties. Anthocyanins are pigmented compounds with significant antioxidant properties that help protect cells against oxidative stress." (a)
29. "Research has shown that Granny Smith nutrient density has declined due to selective breeding" (a; U)
30. "Another concern is consistent ranking in pesticide residue testing with the smooth thin skin allowing treatments to cling to the surface." (a, pesticide; U, and the residue rankings that exist are U.S. lists)

#3 Fuji
31. "The most significant issue with Fuji apples is their extremely high sugar content." (a)
32. "often containing significantly more natural sugar than fiber relative to other options... this imbalance can lead to more rapid blood sugar increases compared to varieties with better sugar to fiber ratios" (a)
33. "For Canadians managing blood sugar levels, insulin sensitivity, or simply aiming for balanced energy throughout the day, Fuji apples represent a weaker choice from a nutritional perspective." (a)
34. "relatively low in antioxidant diversity, the selective breeding that created their intense sweetness and satisfying crisp texture also reduced concentrations of beneficial polyphenols, quercetin, and flavonoids that support heart health and reduce inflammation" (a; U)
35. "Fuji apples were bred for taste and texture rather than nutritional complexity." (a)
36. "During this time, natural vitamin levels decline and treatments are applied to slow ripening and prevent spoilage." (a)
37. "they are nutritionally imbalanced. Their high sugar concentration, reduced antioxidant content, and extended storage requirements make them one of the weaker options for Canadians prioritizing health focused fruit choices." (a)

#2 Ambrosia
38. "several concerns that nutrition conscious consumers should understand" (a)
39. "The most prominent issue is their very high natural sugar content. Ambrosia apples are naturally low in acid... there is no tartness to balance the sugar." (a, t)
40. "They contain approximately 19 g of sugar per medium apple according to nutritional databases." (a; U)
41. "club apple management often prioritizes appearance, uniformity, and shelf life over nutritional optimization" (a; and x, see 2c)
42. "the fruits convert starch into sugars very quickly" (a)
43. "prioritize marketability over allowing the fruit to develop maximum nutritional complexity" (a)
44. "a variety that Canadians should approach with awareness rather than assume is automatically healthy because it originated here" (a)

#1 Empire
45. "delivers the most disappointing nutritional value of any apple on this list" (a)
46. "when Canadian government researchers at Agriculture and Agri Food Canada in Guelph, Ontario tested eight popular apple varieties for antioxidant content, Empire apples ranked dead last" (a)
47. "The study published in the Journal of Agricultural and Food Chemistry found that Empire apples had the lowest levels of beneficial polyphenols among all varieties tested." (a)
48. "Red delicious apples contained more than two times the antioxidant activity of Empire." (a; U: not in the abstract opened, S35)
49. "The polyphenols that contribute most to apple health benefits, epicatechin and procyanidin B2 were found at significantly lower levels in Empire. Procyanidins account for approximately 60% of antioxidant activity in apple peels." (a; U)
50. "Canadian consumers pay reasonable prices for Empire apples expecting solid nutritional value. But the science tells a different story." (a)
51. "you are choosing the variety that ranked at the absolute bottom of government antioxidant testing. Every other variety tested delivered more health benefits. For Canadians who eat apples specifically for their health properties, Empire represents the worst value proposition on the market." (a)

Worth-it #2 Gala
52. "an apple variety that delivers genuine nutritional value" (a)
53. "excellent balance of sugar, fiber, and beneficial compounds. A medium Gala apple contains approximately 80 to 95 calories, about three grams of dietary fiber, and meaningful amounts of vitamin C, vitamin A, and potassium." (a)
54. "promotes digestive health, helps regulate blood sugar levels by slowing glucose absorption, and contributes to feelings of fullness that support healthy weight management" (a)
55. "rich in antioxidants including quercetin, catechins, and other polyphenols that help combat free radicals and may reduce the risk of chronic diseases. Research has associated regular apple consumption with reduced risk of heart disease, diabetes, and certain cancers." (a)
56. "a more favorable sugar-to-fiber ratio than many competing options" (a)
57. "Gala apples also possess pectin, a natural soluble fiber that has been shown to reduce cholesterol and help prevent heart disease. Pectin supports gut health by feeding beneficial bacteria... immune function to mood regulation." (a)

Worth-it #1 McIntosh
58. "Nutritionally, Macintosh apples deliver what Canadians need. They are a source of dietary fiber with approximately 3 g per medium apple, which supports digestion and helps regulate blood sugar levels. They contain vitamin C for immune support, potassium for fluid balance, vitamin A for organ health, and vitamin E as an antioxidant to protect cells against free radical damage." (a)
59. "fresher apples retain higher concentrations of vitamins and phytonutrients" (a)
60. "Apples bred to survive months of shipping and storage often sacrifice flavor and nutrition for durability." (a, t)
61. "The Canadian Food Inspection Agency monitors and enforces residue limits on both domestic and imported produce, and Health Canada establishes maximum residue levels to ensure that any exposure presents no concerns for human health. A 2009 to 2010 study found that 100% of apple products tested in Canada complied with safety standards." (a, pesticide and food safety; U)
62. "a genuinely homegrown apple with strong nutritional credentials" (a)

Close
63. "the variety you choose matters more than most Canadians realize. Many popular apples in our grocery stores are bred for appearance, sweetness, and shelf life rather than nutrition, leading to higher sugar intake, reduced antioxidant benefits, and missed opportunities for genuine health improvement." (a)
64. "the prettiest apple on the shelf is not necessarily the healthiest one in your hand. Apples that are less engineered for industrial convenience and storage longevity tend to offer better fiber, more diverse antioxidants, and higher overall nutritional value." (a)
65. "subscribe for more food investigations and ingredient breakdowns" (a-adjacent: the channel's promise is no longer ingredient breakdowns)
66. "the apple you reach for tomorrow could make a real difference to your health over time" (a)

### 2b. Unsourced, U.S./North American, taste-as-fact and tier-(c) lines

67. "Per capita apple consumption in North America rose from the early 1990s until 2016, aligned directly with the growth and expanded production of Honey Crisp." (US, U)
68. "Production now includes Canada, where British Columbia growers have steadily increased plantings." (U as spoken; S6 supports the direction, so it can return with the source: BC Honeycrisp acreage rose 2011–2021, 527 acres in 2021)
69. "The variety commands premium prices with organic Honey Crisp receiving approximately 60% higher prices than conventional apples." (U, likely US)
70. "Canadians often pay significantly more for Honey Crisp than other varieties" (U as spoken; S4 gives a documented Ontario farm-gate gap that can replace it)
71. "The most recognizable apple variety in the world" (Red Delicious) (U)
72. "The Red Delicious apples reaching Canadian grocery stores have often been stored for many months in controlled atmosphere facilities." (U)
73. "Consumer complaints about Red Delicious are widespread. The texture is described as mealy, dry, and spongy" (c, t)
74. "That beautiful appearance has come at the cost of flavor, texture, and practical nutritional value." (t, a)
75. "The variety was created by Crossing Macintosh with Newtown Pippin apples." (Spartan) (x: S20, by AAFC's own Summerland authors, says "the amended pedigree is 'McIntosh' X unknown")
76. "Spartan apples can be stored for up to 9 months in controlled atmosphere facilities." (U)
77. "The variety is prone to canker in wetter regions" (U)
78. "British Columbia's tree fruit production guide notes that recent years have seen declining returns for Spartan with very few other growing areas producing this variety." (source named but undated and unlinked; not opened for this note)
79. "Today, Spartan apples are considered a rare specialty variety primarily grown in British Columbia as a home garden novelty rather than a mainstream commercial option." (x against S6: 293 acres, 4.6% of BC apple acreage in 2021, fifth-largest variety; and S4: 183 acres in Ontario, 2.26 million lb in 2024)
80. "Courtland apples were developed at the New York State Agricultural Experiment Station in 1898" (incomplete: the cross was 1898, the release 1915 per S22; say both)
81. "In Nova Scotia's Annapolis Valley, orchardists have increasingly replaced Courtland trees with newer varieties like ambrosia, Gala, and especially Honey Crisp because these command multiple times more money per bin." (U)
82. "most of Canada's Courtland production now occurs in Quebec and Ontario. But even there, it is a declining variety that many growers are phasing out." (U; Ontario Cortland production actually rose 15.1% in 2024 per S4, 7.9 to 9.1 million lb; Quebec variety data unverified)
83. "The variety is now available year round in Canadian supermarkets." (Granny Smith) (U)
84. "Fuji apples were developed in Japan in the late 1930s by crossing two American apple varieties, Red Delicious and Ralls Janet. The name comes from the town of Fujisaki" (no tier a/b source opened; see section 5)
85. "they are increasingly popular in British Columbia and Ontario" (Fuji) (U; S6 does not list Fuji among BC's top eight varieties; S4 shows Ontario Fuji at 379 acres, 2.4%)
86. "Newer varieties like ambrosia, Gala, and Honey Crisp have increasingly replaced Fuji alongside traditional varieties like Red Delicious, Spartan, Macintosh, and Golden Delicious in British Columbia orchards." (U as spoken; partially supported by S6's trend sentence, which names Gala, McIntosh and Spartan as declining, not Fuji)
87. "Ambrosia apples originated when the Mennell family of Cawston, British Columbia, discovered a chance seedling growing in their orchard around 1987 with the tree first producing fruit in the early 1990s." (the year 1987 is U; S16 gives the PBR application date 1994-03-21 and S17 the U.S. filing 1997-06-19; S18 confirms "discovered by chance in Cawston, B.C.")
88. "they were the third most produced variety in 2016" (Ambrosia in BC) (U; S6 says "After 2019, Ambrosia overtook Gala" and Ambrosia was first by acreage in 2021)
89. "Ambrosia is also classified as a club apple variety, meaning an international organization manages production to regulate quality and consistency standards worldwide." (x: Canadian rights expired 2015-08-20 per S16 and the U.S. patent expired 2017-06-19 per S17; in Canada and the U.S. it is an open variety; "international organization" is U)
90. "One of the shortest harvest seasons of any North American apple cultivar" (U)
91. "Empire apples originated from a cross, made by orchardist Lester Anderson in the Hudson Valley" (partly U; S23 gives 1945 and "widely introduced 1966" and the McIntosh x Red Delicious parentage; Anderson's role is not on the Cornell pages opened)
92. "Empire apples are often marketed as a premium choice due to their attractive appearance and firm texture" (U, t)
93. "Canadian consumers pay reasonable prices for Empire apples" (U, no price)
94. "Canadian grown Gala apples are available through much of the year" (U as spoken)
95. "The natural sweetness of Gala apples, balanced by just enough tartness" (t)
96. "by 1870, commercial production had begun" (McIntosh) (U; S21 gives only 1811)
97. "Macintosh is culturally considered Canada's apple and appeared on the 1996 Canadian silver dollar." (U; Mint page not opened, see section 5)
98. "The variety once made up over 40% of the commercial Canadian apple market." (U)
99. "Macintosh harvest begins in late September in Ontario and Quebec" (U; no association harvest calendar opened, see section 5)
100. "The flesh is white, tender, and juicy with a sweet tart flavor that hints at spice. When eaten freshly picked, the texture is crisp with a pleasant snap." (t)
101. "Macintosh prioritizes taste and quality over industrial convenience." (t)
102. "Apples are often marketed as one of the healthiest fruits." (a)

Structural challenges the audience could raise: no stated basis for 8 to 1; the same study (Tsao 2003) is the whole case for #1, #5 and #7; Honeycrisp is called both premium and #8 (mildest); Ambrosia is called Canadian and then placed at #2; the clipped news audio in the open is not the channel's voice; there is no price anywhere with a store, date or unit.

### 2c. Verdicts the new script reverses, with the on-air acknowledgement

February's verdicts: avoid Honeycrisp, Red Delicious, Spartan, Cortland, Granny Smith, Fuji, Ambrosia, Empire; worth it Gala, McIntosh. The new basis (origin, age, ownership, price) reverses Ambrosia (Canadian-found, Canadian-owned rights now expired, the largest variety in BC) and Spartan (the only apple on either list bred by a Canadian government program), and moves Gala from worth-it to the avoid list's first slot (New Zealand-bred, the most imported named variety in Canada). McIntosh stays worth-it. Honeycrisp, Red Delicious, Cortland, Granny Smith, Fuji and Empire stay on the avoid list for documented, non-nutrition reasons.

Mandatory one-sentence acknowledgement, spoken at the top of the list (recommended placement: the last sentence of the cold open or the first sentence of beat #8), and shown as a lower-third:

"In February we ranked these apples by what is inside them; today we rank them by where they come from, how old the variety is, who owns it and what it costs, and on that basis three verdicts change."

---

## 3. Recommended skeleton for the new script

### 3a. Targets

Plain spoken prose, no headers in the body, 21,000–23,000 characters. Built to 3,520 words at the ketchup/jam density of 6.2 chars/word (about 21,800 characters), with the two longest beats able to absorb headroom to 22,600. If the draft comes in above 6.5 chars/word, it has drifted back into February's vocabulary; cut the polysyllables, not the facts.

### 3b. Order basis (state it on screen; it is the whole argument)

Recommended basis: Canadian acreage per variety, using the two provincial variety-level acreage tables that exist and were opened (Ontario Apple Growers' 2024 annual report, S4, acreage 2023; Government of B.C. 2021 Tree Fruit Acreage and Maturity Report, S6). Rank ascending by "distance from a Canadian orchard": the more Canadian acres a variety has, the earlier it appears; #1 is the apple that no Canadian orchard is permitted to grow.

Why this basis and not price per kilo: no retailer page could be opened (Metro returned 403, Loblaws and Walmart returned bot-wall redirects; section 5), so no per-variety price could be sourced at rule-5 standard. Statistics Canada publishes only an all-apples $/kg (S2). Price is therefore a device in the close, not the ranking basis. Why acreage and not import tonnage by variety: Canada's import data by variety (S5, Table 4) names only Honeycrisp, Gala and Granny Smith and lumps 120 million lb into "Unspecified"; acreage covers every variety on both lists.

On-screen wording (spoken once in the cold open, shown as a lower-third on every bridge with the acres and source): "Ranked by how many acres of it Canada grows, using the Ontario Apple Growers' 2024 annual report and the B.C. government's 2021 tree fruit acreage report, the two provinces that publish acreage by variety. Quebec, Nova Scotia and New Brunswick do not, so their acres are not counted and the Canadian totals for McIntosh, Cortland, Empire and Spartan are understated."

That last clause is mandatory: Quebec was 31% of Canada's 2024 crop (118,157 of 386,368 tonnes, S3) and the USDA post in Ottawa writes that "Quebec has the largest McIntosh acreage in Canada" (S7, a U.S. government assessment; attribute it as such if used).

The acreage table (Ontario 2023 from S4 p. 29; BC 2021 from S6 Table 3.2.1; BC lists only its top eight varieties, so a BC blank means fewer than 116 acres):

| Variety | Ontario acres (2023) | Ontario share | BC acres (2021) | BC share | Two-province total | Bred where, when |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Gala | 3,060 | 19.3% | 2,021 | 32.0% | 5,081 | New Zealand, 1934 cross (breeder page not opened; S27 calls Royal Gala a "NEW ZEALAND ORIGINAL") |
| Ambrosia | 1,701 | 10.7% | 2,332 | 36.9% | 4,033 | Cawston, B.C., chance seedling; PBR applied 1994 (S16, S17, S18) |
| Honeycrisp | 2,366 | 14.9% | 527 | 8.3% | 2,893 | University of Minnesota, released 1991 (S12, S14, S28) |
| McIntosh | 1,988 | 12.5% | 362 | 5.7% | 2,350 | Dundela, Ontario, 1811 (S21) |
| Red Delicious | 931 | 5.9% | 116 | 1.8% | 1,047 | Peru, Iowa; rights bought by Stark Bro's 1894 (S25) |
| Empire | 891 | 5.6% | not listed | | 891+ | Cornell Geneva, N.Y., released 1966 (S22, S23) |
| Spartan | 183 | 1.2% | 293 | 4.6% | 476 | AAFC Summerland, B.C., cross 1926, named 1936 (S20) |
| Cortland | 403 | 2.5% | not listed | | 403+ | Cornell Geneva, N.Y., released 1915 (S22) |
| Fuji | 379 | 2.4% | not listed | | 379+ | Japan (no tier a/b page opened; section 5) |
| Granny Smith | in "Other Cultivars" (1,407 acres across 30-plus varieties) | <1.1% | 123 | 2.0% | <302 | Eastwood, N.S.W., ~1868 (S26) |
| Cosmic Crisp (WA 38) | not listed | 0 | not listed | 0 | 0 | Washington State University; Washington-only until 2032 (S9, S10, S11) |

Resulting avoid order, #8 first: Gala 5,081; Honeycrisp 2,893; Red Delicious 1,047; Empire 891+; Cortland 403+; Fuji 379+; Granny Smith <302; Cosmic Crisp 0. Worth-it, in the same ascending-Canadian logic reversed (most Canadian last): Spartan 476, McIntosh 2,350, Ambrosia 4,033.

Golden Delicious (Ontario 456 acres, S4) and Northern Spy (896) are the only two eligible varieties left off; Golden Delicious would slot between Cortland and Empire if the writer wants a swap for Fuji, whose origin could not be sourced at tier (a)/(b).

### 3c. Eligibility rule for a slot

A variety holds a slot only if the record opened for this note gives, at tier (a) or (b): (1) a breeder or place of origin with a date; (2) a rights/ownership status with a date (patent or Plant Breeders' Rights granted and expired, or "never protected"); (3) a Canadian acreage or production figure with a source; and (4) for the avoid list, one documented non-nutrition fact that the beat turns on (origin abroad, import share, rights restriction, farm-gate or shelf price gap, declining acreage). Fuji currently fails (1) at tier (a)/(b) and Cosmic Crisp's tier-(a) WSU announcement was not opened (only Capital Press, tier b); both are flagged in section 5 with the pointer to fix before the script is locked.

### 3d. The eight jobs in every avoid beat (order fixed)

1. Bridge with the number, one-line characterization, name last (10–20 w). Lower-third: acres and source.
2. Recognition (30–40 w): the apple located in the viewer's own memory, one concrete Canadian retail detail (the bin, the bag, the sticker), no taste words.
3. Origin and breeder with a date (40–50 w): who bred or found it, where, when, from the breeder's or licensor's own page or the patent record. Models: "Honey crisp apples were developed at the University of Minnesota and released in 1991" (Feb, keep); "Discovered in 1880 by Jesse Hyatt in Iowa" (TT, fix the year and source: S25 gives the Hiatt farm at Peru, Iowa, and the 1894 rights purchase); "founded in 1897 by Jerome Monroe Smucker, an Orrville, Ohio farmer" (jam).
4. Ownership and rights chain with dates (40–60 w): who held the rights, when they were granted, when they expired, who collects royalties now. Every variety on the list has a record: S14/S15 (Honeycrisp), S16/S17 (Ambrosia), S9 (Cosmic Crisp), S25 (Red Delicious, Stark Bro's bought "the sole rights from Hiatt"), S22/S23 (Cornell releases), S20 (AAFC Summerland). Model: "In 2014, Restaurant Brands International acquired Tim Hortons in a deal worth approximately 11.4 billion dollars" (fried-chicken note); here it reads "The University of Minnesota patented it in 1988, released it in 1991, collected 16.5 million dollars in royalties by 2019, and its Canadian rights expired on August 10, 2019."
5. The Canadian number with its source (40–50 w): acres, production, imports. Models: "Per Euromonitor, French's Canadian ketchup sales more than doubled from 2016 to 2017" (ketchup); here "The Ontario Apple Growers' 2024 annual report puts Gala at 3,060 acres, 19.3 percent of the province's apple trees, and the largest variety in Ontario."
6. The accusation, which is the origin/age/ownership/price fact chain (60–80 w): three sentences at most, each with a date or a number. Models: the Heinz twist ("Heinz walked out of that building in 2014, and now it buys its Canadian soul back from the people who saved it"); the Bonne Maman closer ("The grandmother implied by the name has never existed."). Never a texture, never a nutrient.
7. Concession (20–30 w): what the record does not say, or the variety's genuine Canadian credit. Models: "To be fair to McDonald's, they never claimed to be a specialty coffee company" (BC coffee, via the fried-chicken note); "The avoid placement is not about authenticity or craftsmanship" (jam, Greaves).
8. Closer (15–30 w): the variety's own promise turned. Models: "The name is on the door. The craft is not."; "This apple is not grown for your mouth. It is grown for a camera." (TT skip; keep the shape, drop the taste implication: "It is grown for the box, not the orchard down the road.")

Price appears inside job 6 only where it is documented at rule-5 standard. Right now that means the Ontario farm-gate figures in S4 ("Honeycrisp at $0.47/lb. (down 35%), Gala at $0.29/lb. (down 21%), and Ambrosia at $0.25/lb. (down 28%)", 2023 crop, grower price, not shelf price) and the Statistics Canada all-apples $/kg series (S2). Shelf prices per variety go in only if the writer captures them from a retailer's own app or flyer with date, banner, city, $/kg and $/lb and the origin as displayed (section 5 gives the procedure).

The ask starts at 61.9% of words, inside the 61–63% band. Because the ask must sit after six of eight beats, the six early beats carry 2,050 words between them and the close is compressed; if the writer wants the worth-it beats at February's length (330–420 w each), the ask cannot land in the band. Do not move the ask; trim the worth-it beats.

### 3f. Cold open, 130 words, five moves

1. The dated fact, first sentence, tier (a), Canadian. Recommended: "In 2024 Canada imported 164,852 tonnes of fresh apples, and Agriculture and Agri-Food Canada's own overview, dated July 16, 2025, says that in any given year over 80 percent of them come from the United States, close to 80 percent of that from Washington state." (S3, verbatim: "In any given year, over 80% of Canada's fresh apple imports come from the U.S., with close to 80% coming from Washington state.") Alternative dated facts, in order of strength: "On February 16, 2026, Statistics Canada put Canada's 2025 apple crop at 348,789 tonnes, down from 386,368 in 2024" (S1); "On April 29, 2026, Capital Press reported that Washington State University had extended Washington's exclusive right to grow Cosmic Crisp through May 26, 2032" (S9, tier b; use only if the WSU release in section 5 is opened).
2. The object in the viewer's hand (the ketchup move): the sticker, the bag, the bin sign, one sentence.
3. The reversal: the apple you think of as local usually is not, and the one you think of as premium pays a royalty to someone else.
4. The promise, three provable reveals: one variety no Canadian orchard is allowed to plant until 2032; one Canadian-born variety whose Canadian rights ran out in 2015; one "Canadian" apple that is also the most imported apple in the country.
5. The count and the basis, spoken: "Eight varieties, ranked by how many acres of them Canada actually grows, fewest last, using the growers' own reports. Then the three that are Canadian all the way down." Then the February acknowledgement sentence (section 2c). No subscribe ask in the open. No clipped news audio.

### 3g. The beats, with the documented material each one turns on

All figures below were opened on 29 Sep 2026 at the URLs in section 0b. Quotations are verbatim from those pages.

#8 Gala (320 w). Origin: New Zealand, 1934 cross (the breeder-history page could not be opened; S27 labels Royal Gala a "NEW ZEALAND ORIGINAL"; do not give the breeder's name or the parentage on air until S27's history page or Te Ara is opened, section 5). Canadian number: Ontario 3,060 acres, 19.3%, first (S4); BC 2,021 acres, 32.0%, second (S6); "Combined, Ambrosia and Gala apples account for around 69% of all apple acreage in B.C." (S6). The accusation: Gala is also the most imported named variety, 83,851,657 lb in 2024 (S5, Table 4, "Canadian Fresh Apple Imports (lbs)"), so the same PLU number sits on a Canadian and a Washington apple in the same bin; the sticker tells you the variety, not the country. Concession: S5 says "Gala imports have decreased substantially since 2014 with demand being filled locally." Closer: the most Canadian-grown apple on the list is the one where the label matters most. Price: Ontario grower price $0.29/lb for the 2023 crop (S4), attributed. Verdict wording: avoid the imported one out of season, not the variety.

#7 Honeycrisp (330 w). Origin: University of Minnesota; MNopedia (S28) gives the 1960 cross, the 1982 rescue by David Bedford, the 1988 patent filing, "released in 1991 to nurseries that sell to commercial apple growers," and "$16.5 million in royalties by 2019"; U.S. plant patent PP7,197 filed November 7, 1988, granted March 20, 1990, expired November 7, 2008 (S14); Canadian Plant Breeders' Rights certificate 1007, applied 1994-03-10, granted 2001-08-10, expired 2019-08-10 (S15). The university's own page (S12): "Honeycrisp Apple Trees are no longer under patent protection in the U.S. or Canada. Protected in Europe, New Zealand, Australia, Chile, and South Africa," marketed as "Honeycrunch®" outside North America. Canadian number: Ontario 2,366 acres, 14.9%, second (S4); BC 527 acres, 8.3% (S6); Honeycrisp "not being imported into Canada until 2018," 7,886,711 lb imported in 2024 (S5). The accusation: price. S4: "The three historically top returning varieties being Honeycrisp at $0.47/lb. (down 35%), Gala at $0.29/lb. (down 21%), and Ambrosia at $0.25/lb. (down 28%)" (2023 crop, grower price); S5: "The cultivar that yields the highest grower return in Ontario is Honeycrisp, although yields aren't consistent year to year." Concession: it is grown here in volume now and the royalty has ended in Canada. Closer: the premium is the variety's brand, and the brand is Minnesota's. Attribute the university's description if used: "Explosively crisp, juicy texture" (S12, the university says).

#6 Red Delicious (340 w). Origin: Stark Bro's own account (S25): a chance seedling in Jesse Hiatt's orchard of Yellow Bellflower trees at Peru, Iowa, named Hawkeye, entered in the 1893 and 1894 fruit shows; "The Stark brothers made the trip immediately and bought the sole rights from Hiatt, named the apple 'Stark® Delicious' (only after naming 'Golden Delicious' in 1914 did it become 'Red Delicious')." Canadian number: Ontario 931 acres, 5.9%, down from 6.5% (S4); production 22.8 million lb in 2023 to 20.3 million in 2024, 21.3% below the five-year average (S4); BC 116 acres, 1.8% (S6). U.S. context, labelled: 12% of Washington's 2025 crop forecast (S8). The accusation: the age of the variety and of the Canadian acreage, both declining, from the growers' own tables; the S7 sentence, attributed to the U.S. post in Ottawa: "acreage of McIntosh and Red Delicious varieties continues to decline as producers replace older orchards with varieties that are more in demand with today's consumer." Concession: it is still the fourth-largest apple in Washington and the fifth in Ontario. Closer: the name was a marketing decision in 1914; it still is. No texture words.

#5 Empire (345 w). Origin: Cornell, Geneva, N.Y.; "Cortland (1915), Macoun (1923), Empire (1966) and Jonagold (1968)" (S22); "A cross of McIntosh and Red Delicious," named after "its home state" (S23); Cornell has "released 69 varieties" since 1890 (S23, S24). Canadian number: Ontario 891 acres, 5.6% (S4); production down 10.5% in 2024 and 27.8% below the five-year average (S4). The accusation: a New York variety, half McIntosh, that Ontario is planting less of each year; Quebec's Empire acreage is unverified (section 5), so do not say "Quebec's Empire orchards" without it. Concession: it was bred from Canada's apple. Closer: the Empire in the name is New York's.

#4 Cortland (355 w). Origin: Cornell Geneva, cross 1898, released 1915 (S22 for 1915; the 1898 cross date and the McIntosh x Ben Davis parentage are from search summaries only, so either open a Cornell page for them or say "released by Cornell in 1915"). Canadian number: Ontario 403 acres, 2.5% (S4); production 7.9 to 9.1 million lb, up 15.1% in 2024 but 8.8% below the five-year average (S4). The accusation is the thinnest on the list: a century-old New York variety with a small and flat Canadian footprint; build the beat on the Cornell release chain and the Ontario numbers, and use the concession to say plainly that Cortland's Quebec acreage is not published and may be large. Closer: Cortland County, New York, not Cortland, Ontario.

#3 Fuji (360 w) or Golden Delicious. Fuji origin: no tier (a)/(b) page opened (section 5). If the writer cannot open one, swap in Golden Delicious (Ontario 456 acres, 2.9%, production down 18.6% in 2024, S4; Stark Bro's names it in 1914, S25; U.S. context 3.5% of Washington's 2025 crop, S8) and re-title nothing, since the list count stays eight. If Fuji stays: Ontario 379 acres, 2.4% (S4), production up 42.9% against the five-year average (S4), which is the concession; U.S. context 9.1% of Washington's 2025 crop (S8).

Mid-list ask (55 w, at 61.9%). February form, one interruption only: "If you are getting value from this, take a second to subscribe and drop a comment below. Tell us which apple on this list you had assumed was Canadian. Now, the final two, and the second one is the apple no Canadian farmer is allowed to plant."

#2 Granny Smith (340 w). Origin: City of Ryde's own history page (S26): Maria Ann Smith, born 1799 in Sussex, the Eastwood orchard, the seedling by a creek "around 1868" that she said "had developed from the remains of some French crab apples grown in Tasmania," named "'Granny Smith's Seedling'" by Albert H. Benson of the NSW Department of Agriculture in 1895; she died "9 March 1870." Canadian number: BC 123 acres, 2.0% (S6); Ontario lists it only inside "Other Cultivars" (S4). Imports: 42,448,399 lb in 2024, the second most imported named variety (S5). U.S. context: 14.7% of Washington's 2025 crop (S8). The accusation: a 158-year-old Australian variety that Canada barely grows and imports by the tens of millions of pounds. Concession: none of that is the apple's fault; it is the label's. Closer: the grandmother was real; the orchard on your sticker is not hers.

#1 Cosmic Crisp (380 w). Origin: Washington State University, "Enterprise x Honeycrisp" (S10); the breeding program's own page says it "maintains its texture and flavor in storage for more than a year" and refers licensing to PVM (S11); WSU's variety page lists storage "13 Months" (S10). The rights chain (S9, Capital Press, Kyle Odegard, April 29, 2026): reached consumers in 2019; Washington-only exclusivity originally through 2027; extended through the U.S. patent expiry of May 26, 2032; WSU royalties "more than $8 million" in fiscal 2025; WSU's Jeremy Tamsen quoted: "WSU recognizes that the Washington apple industry's involvement in 'WA 38' continues well beyond its initial commercialization." U.S. context: 9.6% of Washington's 2025 forecast, up from 1% in 2020 (S8). Canadian number: zero acres in either table; S5 notes "Unspecified cultivars have seen an increase of 308% - this could be due to the rise of club cultivars (trademarked/patented)," which is the Ontario sheet's own hedge and must be quoted as a hedge. The accusation: every Cosmic Crisp on a Canadian shelf is imported by rule, not by chance, until at least 2032. Concession: the rule is WSU's to make; nothing improper is alleged. Closer: the most Canadian thing about it is the sticker that says where it is from. This is the beat with the most to answer for on the stated basis, and the closer must say so.

### 3h. The turn (40 w) and the three worth-it beats (95–105 w each)

Turn: "So where does that leave a Canadian who wants an apple from a Canadian tree? Three varieties, in order of how much of them we grow. We have no commercial relationship with any grower, marketer or licensor named in this video." Disclosure here, not later.

Each worth-it beat runs the eight jobs compressed: bridge, origin with date, rights status with date, Canadian acres with source, one concession, one closer. No taste, no health, no "fresh," no "crisp." The attributed breeder description is the only descriptive language allowed and is optional.

Spartan (95 w). "'Spartan' originated from a cross made by R.C. Palmer in 1926"; "The tree designated 26-14-27 first fruited in 1932 and was named in 1936"; "the most commercially successful cultivar released from the Agriculture and Agri-Food Canada apple breeding program in Summerland, B.C."; pedigree "'McIntosh' X unknown" (all S20, AAFC Summerland authors). Never patented: no PBR record. Canadian acres: BC 293, 4.6% (S6), declining (S6: "Gala, McIntosh, and Spartan acreage declined"); Ontario 183 acres, production 2.26 million lb in 2024, up 17.5% (S4). Concession: the acreage is small and shrinking and the variety had a documented storage disorder that AAFC's authors say is "now easily controlled" (S20). Closer: the only apple on either list bred by a Canadian government scientist, in a Canadian government orchard, on Canadian government time.

McIntosh (100 w). Parks Canada, national historic event designated 1999-10-28: "In 1811, John McIntosh discovered an apple sapling on his land in Matilda township. By bringing about its propagation and wide dissemination, he and his family had a significant impact on Canada's fruit growing industry." Location 11530 County Road 18, Dundela (S21). Never patented. Canadian acres: Ontario 1,988, 12.5%, third (S4); BC 362, 5.7% (S6); Quebec unquantified but named by the U.S. post as "the largest McIntosh acreage in Canada" (S7, attribute as USDA's assessment). Concession: acreage is falling in both provinces that publish it (S4: 13.6% to 12.5%; S6: declined 2011–2021). Closer: 215 years old, and the parent of Spartan, Cortland and Empire; the others borrowed from it.

Ambrosia (105 w). Buy BC (S18): "The first Ambrosia apple was discovered by chance in Cawston, B.C. The mother tree is still alive and producing Ambrosia's to this day!" Rights: Canadian PBR application 94-332 filed 1994-03-21 by Sally and Wilfrid Mennell of Cawston, agent Summerland Varieties Corp., certificate 0388 granted 1997-08-20, expired 2015-08-20 (S16); U.S. plant patent PP10,789 filed June 19, 1997, granted February 16, 1999, expired June 19, 2017 (S17); "The fruit of `Ambrosia` matures about the third week in September at Cawston, British Columbia, Canada." (S17). Canadian acres: BC 2,332, 36.9%, first ("After 2019, Ambrosia overtook Gala," S6); Ontario 1,701, 10.7%, fourth, production up 58.2% against the five-year average (S4). U.S. context: 1% of Washington's 2025 crop (S8). Concession and the February reversal in one sentence: in February this channel put Ambrosia on the avoid list on a nutrition basis; on origin, ownership and acreage it is the most Canadian apple in the country. Closer: found by a family, protected by a Canadian certificate, released to every Canadian grower when that certificate ran out.

### 3i. How to protect yourself (130 w, four numbered steps, "First," "Second," "Third," "Fourth")

First, the sticker and the sign. The PLU number identifies the variety, not the country: IFPS, which assigns the codes, says "The PLU number identifies produce items based upon various attributes which can include the commodity, variety, growing methodology (e.g., organic), and size" and "The 4-digit codes are for conventionally grown produce... The prefix of '9' would be placed in front of the 4-digit conventionally grown code for organic produce" (S33). Country of origin is separate: for imported prepackaged fresh fruit the regulation requires "'Product of' or 'Produce of'... 'Grown in'... or 'Country of Origin'... followed by the name of the foreign state" (SFCR s. 269(1), S31), in type at least 1.6 mm high (s. 270(1)), with an exemption for fruit "packaged from bulk at retail" (s. 270(2)). For loose apples, read the bin sign; the rule for bulk display signage was not found on the pages opened (section 5), so the script says "read the sign," not "the law requires the sign."

Second, the calendar. Ontario's own Foodland page lists apples as available in every month except July (S34). The Ambrosia patent gives maturity "about the third week in September at Cawston" (S17); the University of Minnesota gives Honeycrisp harvest "approximately September 20 to October 5 in Minnesota" (S12, U.S. context). Canadian association harvest calendars could not be opened (section 5); do not quote harvest dates for McIntosh, Spartan, Cortland or Empire until one is.

Third, the price per kilo, from the government's series. Statistics Canada's monthly average retail price for apples, Canada: $6.19 per kilogram in June 2025, $4.68 in September, $4.40 in October 2025, back to $5.55 in January 2026, $6.17 in May, $6.33 in June and $6.45 in July 2026 (S2). October's national average was 32 percent below July's. Ontario: $4.17 in October 2025 versus $6.33 in July 2026. Speak the table name and month on air. Per-variety shelf prices only with a retailer capture per rule 5 (procedure in section 5).

Fourth, buy Canadian in season, and say what that means on the stated basis: an apple from the three Canadian-bred varieties, with a Canadian origin sign, in the four months after harvest. Keep February's one good instruction ("Seek out Canadian grown options when available") and make it the rule.

### 3j. Moral, comment ask, tagline (50 w) and disclaimer (45 w)

Moral, February-restaurants triplet shape: "Eight apples with something in common. Not one of them was bred here, and the one that was, was bred to be sold as a brand. The apple does not know where it was grown. The sign does." Comment ask: "Tell us in the comments which apple on this list you had always assumed was Canadian." Share line optional. Tagline, last spoken words before the disclaimer: "the truth is not always on the menu."

Disclaimer, 45 words, spoken and on screen: "Everything in this video comes from government statistics, growers' association reports, breeders' own pages, patent and plant breeders' rights records and named news reports, as of the date shown. Varieties are ranked by published acreage, not by taste or nutrition. Prices are Statistics Canada monthly averages and vary by store."

### 3k. Vocabulary rules for the body

Every event opens with a month and year, or a year where that is all the record gives. Every number carries its source in the same sentence ("according to the Ontario Apple Growers' 2024 annual report"). "Rights expired" and "patent expired" with the date; never "public domain" without the date. "The association says," "the university says," "the growers' report says" for every descriptive or promotional phrase. No "analysts," "experts," "reports," "reviews," "customers say," "consumers complain," "social media." No "we tested," "we tasted," "we visited." No crisp, mealy, juicy, sweet, tart, bland, mushy, flavour, texture, aroma, snap. No sugar, fibre, vitamin, antioxidant, polyphenol, residue, pesticide, spray, wax, chemical, safe, healthy, healthier, nutrition, glycemic, blood sugar, calories, immune. No "engineered" applied to the fruit. U.S. figures only inside a sentence that names the U.S. body ("the Washington State Tree Fruit Association's 2025 forecast," "the USDA post in Ottawa writes"). The February acknowledgement sentence appears once, verbatim from section 2c.

(S16); US PP10,789 granted 1999-02-16, expired 2017-06-19 (S17) | BC 2,332 ac, 36.9%, first (S6); ON 1,701 ac, 10.7%; 54.5 million lb 2024, +58.2% vs 5-yr avg (S4) | WA 1% (S8) | ON grower price $0.25/lb, 2023 crop (S4) |

National frame (S1, S3): marketed production Canada 358,514 t (2023), 386,368 t (2024), 348,789 t (2025); farm gate value $286.2M (2023), $314.2M (2024), $296.5M (2025); 2025 by province: Ontario 148,493 t, Quebec 108,291 t, BC 54,809 t, Nova Scotia 31,830 t, New Brunswick 3,812 t. S3 verbatim: "Canada is a net importer of apples."; "Over the last 5 crop years (2018/19–2022/23), Canada had a trade deficit of 152,261 metric tons in fresh apples (196,946 metric tons of imports vs 44,685 metric tons of exports)."; "British Columbia's apple crop continued to decline for the fifth consecutive year, making the 2024 crop the smallest one in recent years. It fell by 7.1% to 63,538 metric tons." Import origin MY2024/25 (S7, U.S. source, Trade Data Monitor): world 168,732 t, United States 137,023 t (81.2%), New Zealand 8,686 t, Chile 8,232 t, China 2,704 t, EU 1,420 t.

Regulatory frame (S30): apple grades are "Canada Extra Fancy, Canada Fancy, Canada Commercial, Canada Hailed, Canada Commercial Cookers, Canada No. 1 Peelers and Canada No. 2 Peelers"; minimum diameter 60 mm; colour thresholds by grade; no flavour or sugar criterion appears in the grade specifications (document last amended April 15, 2026). This is the Canadian, non-nutrition equivalent of the U.S. video's USDA grade line and is permitted.

FILE: apples_industry.md
==================================================================

# Canadian apple industry record — tier (a) first

Compiled 29 Sep 2026. Every item carries the URL actually opened and the date opened (all opened 29 Sep 2026 unless stated). Tier key: **(a)** government statistics / regulator / court / company-association own site or release / breeder-licensor page; **(b)** named outlet with byline and date; **(c)** aggregator/SEO/Wikipedia/review/Reddit/menu-price = flag only, never a source; **(d)** named consumer survey with publisher, year, methodology.

House-rule reminders applied throughout: no health/nutrition/food-safety commentary (some source pages contain such content; it is deliberately not reproduced); taste/texture appear only as attributed quotations from a breeder, packer or grower association; no wrongdoing stated or implied; U.S. facts appear only as labelled U.S. context; no retailer shelf prices are asserted in this file (none were collected from a retailer's own site/flyer — see UNVERIFIED).

---

## 1. Statistics Canada — apple production, area, farm gate value by province (Table 32-10-0364-01)

**Source (a):** Statistics Canada, Table 32-10-0364-01 "Area, production and farm gate value of marketed fruits" (formerly CANSIM 001-0009). Landing page opened: https://www150.statcan.gc.ca/t1/tbl1/en/tv.action?pid=3210036401 (page states release date **February 16, 2026**; reference period 1926–2025). Data extracted from the full-table CSV download https://www150.statcan.gc.ca/n1/tbl/csv/32100364-eng.zip (file 32100364.csv, dated 2026-02-16), commodity "Fresh apples [114114111]". Farm gate value is in **thousands of dollars**. StatCan quality symbols carried as-is: E = use with caution; F = too unreliable to be published; x = suppressed (confidentiality).

**Companion narrative (a):** The Daily, "Fruit and vegetable production, 2025", released 2026-02-16, https://www150.statcan.gc.ca/n1/daily-quotidien/260216/dq260216c-eng.htm — verbatim: "Fruit and vegetable farm-gate value increased by 11.3% year over year to a record high $3.4 billion in 2025." and "production declined for lowbush blueberries (-12.6%) and apples (-9.7%)". The Daily references tables 32-10-0364-01 (fruits) and 32-10-0365-01 (vegetables).

Method: HS6 file (ODPFN015 for imports; ODPFN019 for exports) filtered to HS6 = 080810 ("Apples, fresh"); value in Canadian dollars (customs basis), quantity in KGM (kg). Country = country of origin for imports; destination for exports. Rows where the origin is coded "CA" (Canada) are Canadian-origin goods re-imported; they are shown as reported and not interpreted. Figures are customs-basis, as released; StatCan revises monthly trade data.

### 2.11 AAFC-published trade figures for comparison (a)

AAFC "Statistical overview of the Canadian fruit industry, 2024" (see section 4) prints 2024 fresh apple imports as **$269,485 thousand / 164,852 tonnes**, top sources by value United States $232,383k, Chile $18,445k, Mexico $9,298k; and 2024 exports **$68,787 thousand / 57,174 tonnes**, top destinations United States $54,302k, Vietnam $7,627k, Cuba $1,807k (Tables 2.2.5 and 2.2.6 as cited on the page). These differ from the CIMT bulk-file totals above (e.g., CIMT 2024 imports $255,955k / 163,875 t; Mexico only 5,515 kg in CIMT). Possible causes: different data vintage/revision, or AAFC's product definition. **Do not mix the two series in one sentence; cite whichever is used with its URL.**

OMAFA "2026 Ontario Apple Industry" (section 9.1) prints "Table 4. Canadian Fresh Apple Imports (lbs)": Honeycrisp 2014: 870,646 (footnoted "2018 data used for Honeycrisp (first year of imports)"), 2024: 7,886,711; Gala 2014: 80,012,865, 2024: 83,851,657; Granny Smith 2014: 29,956,639, 2024: 42,448,399; Unspecified 2014: 29,404,452, 2024: 120,039,138; Total 2014: 177,688,752, 2024: 282,043,159. Verbatim bullets: "Canadian apple imports have changed drastically over the past 10 years, with Honeycrisp not being imported into Canada until 2018." "Gala imports have decreased substantially since 2014 with demand being filled locally." "Unspecified cultivars have seen an increase of 308% - this could be due to the rise of club cultivars (trademarked/patented)."

## 4. Agriculture and Agri-Food Canada apple sector pages

**(a) Statistical overview of the Canadian fruit industry, 2024** — https://agriculture.canada.ca/en/sector/horticulture/reports/statistical-overview-canadian-fruit-industry-2024 (page "date modified" 2025-07-16). As displayed: apple production 2024 386,368 t (41.5% of total fruit), 2023 358,514 t, 2022 382,456 t; farm gate value 2024 $310,263k, 2023 $286,187k, 2022 $284,926k (cited to 32-10-0364-01; see revision note in 1.5); 2024 by province: Ontario 161,641 t ("42% of Canadian production"), Quebec 118,157 t ("largest ever recorded" per page), British Columbia 63,538 t; area 17,685 ha (12.8% of fruit area); trade as in 2.11; "Fresh apples available for consumption (2023): 8.35 kg per person" and adjusted for losses 4.86 kg (page's figures; StatCan table 32-10-0054-01 downloaded 29 Sep 2026 now shows 8.23 / 4.79 for 2023 — revised). A 2025 edition at .../statistical-overview-canadian-fruit-industry-2025 returned HTTP 404 on 29 Sep 2026; the AAFC horticulture reports index https://agriculture.canada.ca/en/sector/horticulture/reports (date modified 2026-06-15) lists 2024 as the most recent fruit overview.

**(a) AAFC Infohort apple storage reports** — https://agriculture.canada.ca/en/sector/horticulture/market-information-infohort/storage-reports/apple?menupos=01.02.02.05 (date modified 2025-11-25). The page describes four monthly report series: storage by facility type and province ("Controlled Atmosphere, Cold, and Common Rooms"), storage by variety and utilization (Fresh, Juice, Peeler), a monthly summary by utilization and variety, and an overall summary; holdings are "as of the first day of each month from November to July"; 2025–26 release dates listed from December 9, 2025 to July 8, 2026. No figures are displayed on the index page itself (the report pages/CSVs were not opened — see UNVERIFIED).
