## 5. CFIA grade standards for apples — Canadian Grade Compendium, Volume 2

**Source (a):** CFIA, "Canadian Grade Compendium: Volume 2 – Fresh Fruit or Vegetables" (document incorporated by reference in the Safe Food for Canadians Regulations), https://inspection.canada.ca/en/about-cfia/acts-and-regulations/list-acts-and-regulations/documents-incorporated-reference/canadian-grade-compendium-volume-2 — page date modified 2026-04-15. Apples are Part 1, sections 5–18 of the same page. The page also links "Fresh Fruit and Vegetable Grade Requirements" (https://fvdrc.com/ffv-flf/) for other commodities.

**Grade names (s. 6):** "Canada Extra Fancy, Canada Fancy, Canada Commercial, Canada Hailed, Canada Commercial Cookers, Canada No. 1 Peelers and Canada No. 2 Peelers."

**Definition of mature (s. 4):** "mature means the fruit has reached the stage of development that ensures completion of the ripening process."

**Minimum size (s. 7(1)(a)):** apples must have "a minimum diameter of 60 mm (2 ⅜ inches)"; all grades must be "mature, clean and sound" (s. 7).

**Canada Extra Fancy (s. 8), verbatim extracts:** "Apples graded Canada Extra Fancy must be smooth and well formed; be free from bruises individually exceeding 19 mm (3/4 inch) in diameter, or affecting an aggregate area per apple exceeding 25 mm (1 inch) in diameter"; hail: "free from hail damage that has broken the skin, has caused discolouration, has surface indentation exceeding 2 mm (1/16 inch) in depth, has caused an individual mark exceeding 3 mm (1/8 inch) in diameter, or affects an aggregate area per apple exceeding 13 mm (1/2 inch) in diameter"; russetting: "free from russetting that is within the stem cavity or calyx basin and is rough and readily apparent and materially affects the appearance of an apple, or is outside the stem cavity or calyx basin and is rough and affects an aggregate area per apple exceeding 6 mm (1/4 inch) in diameter".

**Canada Fancy (s. 9):** "Apples graded Canada Fancy must be smooth and fairly well formed; be free from bruises individually exceeding 19 mm (¾ inch) in diameter, or affecting an aggregate area per apple exceeding 25 mm (1 inch) in diameter"; hail: "free from hail damage that has broken the skin, has surface indentation exceeding 2 mm (1/16 inch) in depth, affects an aggregate area per apple exceeding 13 mm (½ inch) in diameter, or has noticeably depressed the affected areas or materially affects the appearance of an apple".

**Canada Commercial (s. 11):** "Apples graded Canada Commercial must be free from bruises that affect an aggregate area per apple exceeding 38 mm (1 ½ inches) in diameter"; hail: "free from hail damage that has not broken the skin but affects an aggregate area per apple exceeding 19 mm (¾ inch) in diameter, has broken the skin and is not well healed, has broken the skin and is well healed but affects an aggregate area per apple exceeding 6 mm (¼ inch) in diameter, or has noticeably depressed the affected areas or seriously affected the appearance of an apple"; russetting: "free from russetting that is smooth and solid and affects an aggregate area per apple exceeding one-half of the surface area of the apple".

**Canada Hailed (s. 12):** "Apples graded Canada Hailed must meet the requirements of Canada Commercial grade except those set out in paragraph 11(1)(b); have not less than the amount of colour required for Canada Fancy grade; be free from hail damage that has not broken the skin but affects an aggregate area per apple exceeding 38 mm (1 ½ inches) in diameter, has broken the skin and is not well healed, has broken the skin and is well healed but exceeds 10 mm (⅜ inch) in diameter or affects an aggregate area per apple exceeding 25 mm (1 inch) in diameter, or exceeds 6 mm (¼ inch) in depth".

**Colour (s. 16, "Table of Colour Requirements for Major Apple Varieties"):**

| Variety colour group | Canada Extra Fancy | Canada Fancy | Canada Commercial |
|---|---|---|---|
| Solid red or fully striped varieties | 65% | 40% | 15% |
| Partially red or partially striped varieties | 55% | 30% | 15% |
| Red cheeked or blush varieties | "Perceptibly blush-cheeked" | "Tinge of colour" | — |
| Green, yellow or russet varieties | "Characteristic of variety when mature" | "Characteristic of variety when mature" | — |

**What is graded:** shape ("smooth and well formed" / "fairly well formed"), colour (percent surface by variety group), minimum diameter, maturity ("mature, clean and sound"), and defects (bruises, hail marks, russetting, and — per the page's summary of the sections — scab/pinpoint scab, storage scald, insect damage). **Confirmed: the apple sections contain no criteria for flavour, taste, sweetness, crispness, firmness or pressure** (two separate reads of the page on 29 Sep 2026 found no occurrence of "flavour", "taste", "sweet", "crisp", "firm" or "pressure" in the apple grade requirements). Grade name = appearance/size/defect standard, not an eating-quality standard.

**Related SFCR grade-name rules (a):** CFIA "Labelling requirements for fresh fruits or vegetables", https://inspection.canada.ca/en/food-labels/labelling/industry/fruits-or-vegetables-fresh (date modified 2026-02-13; reached via redirect from https://inspection.canada.ca/food-labels/labelling/industry/fresh-fruits-or-vegetables/eng/1631894552081/1631895352383?chap=0): "For consumer prepackaged fresh fruits or vegetables, the grade name must be shown on the principal display panel in characters that are at least 1.6 mm in height" [SFCR 312(2)]. CFIA "Regulatory requirements: Fresh fruits or vegetables", https://inspection.canada.ca/en/food-safety-industry/food-guidance-commodity/fresh-fruits-vegetables/regulatory-requirements (date modified 2026-02-06): "whenever a grade name is used, the fresh fruits or vegetables must meet the grade" (cites SFCR 306, 307, 309).

---

## 6. Country-of-origin labelling for fresh fruit — Safe Food for Canadians Regulations (SFCR)

**Regulation text (a):** Safe Food for Canadians Regulations, SOR/2018-108, Part 11, Division 3, Subdivision H "Fresh Fruits or Vegetables" (ss. 268–271). Full text opened: https://laws-lois.justice.gc.ca/eng/regulations/SOR-2018-108/FullText.html (also https://laws-lois.justice.gc.ca/eng/regulations/SOR-2018-108/page-17.html). Verbatim:

> **269 (1)** The expression "Product of" or "Produce of" or "produit de", "Grown in" or "cultivé dans" or "Country of Origin" or "pays d'origine", followed by the name of the foreign state in which the fresh fruits or vegetables were grown, or other words that clearly indicate that foreign state, must be shown on the principal display panel of imported prepackaged fresh fruits or vegetables in close proximity to the declaration of net quantity or the grade name.
> **(2)** If prepackaged fresh fruits or vegetables that are labelled in accordance with this Part are placed inside of a second container and the resulting product is prepackaged fresh fruits or vegetables, other than consumer prepackaged fresh fruits or vegetables, the second container is not required to be labelled with the information referred to in subsection (1) if that information is easily visible and legible without having to open the second container and that information is not obscured by the container.
> **(3)** This section applies whether or not the imported prepackaged fresh fruits or vegetables are subsequently repackaged in Canada.
> **270 (1)** The information that is required by section 269 must be shown in boldface type in characters that are at least 1.6 mm in height.
> **(2)** Subsection (1) does not apply in respect of consumer prepackaged fresh fruits or vegetables that are packaged from bulk at retail or that are catch-weight foods sold by a retailer. [SOR/2025-192, s. 13]
> **271** [Repealed, SOR/2025-192, s. 14]

**CFIA guidance (a):** "Labelling requirements for fresh fruits or vegetables" https://inspection.canada.ca/en/food-labels/labelling/industry/fruits-or-vegetables-fresh (date modified 2026-02-13), verbatim: "The country of origin represents the country in which the fresh fruits or vegetables were grown. The country of origin must be shown on the principal display panel of imported prepackaged fresh fruits or vegetables and in close proximity to the declaration of net quantity or the grade name. The country of origin declaration must be shown in boldface type in characters that are at least 1.6 mm in height [269(1), 270(1), SFCR]." — "All country names must be written out in full, except for the United States which may be abbreviated to 'USA' as it is recognized worldwide." — "The country of origin declaration is mandatory on all containers of imported fresh fruits or vegetables, regardless of whether they are packaged whole or packaged fresh-cut (minimally processed) fresh fruits or vegetables. This requirement applies whether or not imported prepackaged fresh fruits or vegetables are repackaged in Canada [269(3), SFCR]." — "Indicating the country of origin is voluntary on fresh fruits or vegetables grown in Canada." — "The above type size requirement for country of origin declaration does not apply to consumer prepackaged fresh fruits or vegetables that are packaged from bulk at retail or that are catch-weight foods sold by a retailer (for example, bulk whole green beans prepackaged in cello tray packs in-store) [270(2), SFCR]." Definition quoted on the same page: "Consumer prepackaged, in respect of a food, means packaged in a container in the manner in which the food is ordinarily sold to or used or purchased by an individual, or in which the food may reasonably be expected to be obtained by an individual, without being repackaged, to be used for non-commercial purposes [1, SFCR]."

**CFIA consumer page (a):** "How to identify Canadian food", https://inspection.canada.ca/en/food-labels/labelling/consumers/canadian-food (date modified 2025-06-04): "In Canada, these imported food products are required to include the country of origin on their labels: dairy products, eggs, fish, fresh fruits and vegetables, maple and honey products, meat, wine, brandy, and some processed fruits and vegetables."

**What this means for the retail display (stated only as far as the opened texts go):**
- Imported apples in a consumer package (bag, tray, clamshell): "Product of / Produce of / Grown in / Country of Origin + country" on the principal display panel, bold, ≥1.6 mm — s. 269–270.
- Imported apples packed from bulk at the store (retailer's own bag/tray, or catch-weight): the origin declaration still applies (s. 269), but the 1.6 mm bold type-size rule does not — s. 270(2).
- Imported apples in shipping cartons (prepackaged but not consumer prepackaged): the carton must carry the origin declaration (s. 269(1)–(2)); the CFIA says it is "mandatory on all containers of imported fresh fruits or vegetables".
- **Loose apples in a bulk bin (not prepackaged at all):** neither SFCR ss. 268–271 nor the CFIA guidance pages opened contain a rule requiring a bin sign/shelf tag to state the country of origin. The opened CFIA regulatory-requirements page "does not contain specific text about … Bulk (non-consumer prepackaged) fresh fruits or vegetables … Point-of-sale signage requirements". Treat "a sign is required on bulk bins" as **UNVERIFIED** (see final section); provincial rules (e.g., Quebec) were not checked.

---

## 7. Post-harvest coatings / wax on apples in Canada

**Health Canada permitted additives (a):**
- "8. List of Permitted Food Additives with Other Purposes of Use" (Lists of Permitted Food Additives), https://www.canada.ca/en/health-canada/services/food-nutrition/food-safety/food-additives/lists-permitted/8-other-accepted-uses.html (date modified 2026-02-04). Entries whose column 2 is "Fresh fruits and vegetables", purpose "Coating agent", maximum 3,000 ppm: **M.8 Mineral Oil**; **P.2 Paraffin Wax**; **P.3 Petrolatum**. Also **B.1 Beeswax** — "Unstandardized foods", "Antisticking agent", "4,000 ppm". Two reads of this list found no entries for carnauba wax, shellac, candelilla wax, morpholine, oleic acid, sucrose acetate isobutyrate or polyethylene against fresh fruits.
- "7. List of Permitted Glazing Agents" https://www.canada.ca/en/health-canada/services/food-nutrition/food-safety/food-additives/lists-permitted/7-glazing-polishing-agents.html (date modified 2024-12-18). Entries returned: A.1 Acacia Gum, B.1 Beeswax, C.1 Candelilla Wax, C.2 Carnauba Wax (4,000 ppm), M.2 Mineral Oil (1,500 ppm), P.1 Petrolatum (1,500 ppm), S.2 Spermaceti Wax — all listed against "Unstandardized confectionery"; the page as read contained no "fresh fruits" entries. (Whether shellac appears elsewhere on List 7 was not confirmed by the read — flagged in UNVERIFIED.)
- Health Canada archived "Fact Sheet: Use of Morpholine in Apple Coatings" (dated 2002-12-09; archived), https://www.canada.ca/en/health-canada/services/food-nutrition/food-safety/information-product/fact-sheet-use-morpholine-apple-coatings.html — regulatory-status statements only: "Morpholine has been used for about twenty-five years in Canada as part of some coatings." and "It is normally added to the wax as morpholine oleate and dissolves and spreads the wax, allowing for application of the coating in a water-based liquid form." (The remainder of that page is a health assessment and is out of scope under house rule 1; do not use.)

**Declaration at retail — CFIA (a):** "Labelling requirements for fresh fruits or vegetables" (URL above, date modified 2026-02-13): "Wax coating compounds, their components and other protective edible coatings are not required to be shown on the label of prepackaged fresh fruits or vegetables as an ingredient or component thereof" [FDR B.01.008(3)(a)]; exception: "If any food allergens or gluten are present in such coatings, they must be declared on the labels of prepackaged fresh fruits or vegetables" [FDR B.01.010.1(9)]. CFIA "List of ingredients and allergens on food labels", https://inspection.canada.ca/en/food-labels/labelling/industry/list-ingredients-and-allergens: "Wax coating compounds, their components and other protective edible coatings are not required to be shown on the labels of prepackaged fresh fruits or fresh vegetables as an ingredient or a component" [B.01.008(3)(a), FDR]; "if any food allergens, gluten or added sulphites are present in these coatings, they must be declared on the labels of prepackaged fruits and vegetables". Same page: prepackaged products "packed on retail premises from bulk" are among those exempt from a list of ingredients [B.01.008(2)(a), FDR]. The CFIA FFV page also states (per search result on the same site) that produce "refrigerated in controlled-atmosphere storage, irradiated, waxed or washed in a mild chlorine or acid solution may be called 'fresh'" (composition/quality claims section — quoted from the CFIA search snippet; the exact page sentence should be re-checked before on-screen use).

**Bottom line for Canada (as far as opened sources go):** no Canadian federal requirement was found to declare a wax/coating on apples at retail — neither on consumer packages (explicit FDR exemption) nor on bulk bins (no provision found).

**U.S. contrast only (not Canadian):** 21 CFR 101.4(b)(22), https://www.law.cornell.edu/cfr/text/21/101.4 — "Wax and resin ingredients on fresh produce when such produce is held for retail sale, or when held for other than retail sale by packers or repackers shall be declared collectively by the phrase 'coated with food-grade animal-based wax, to maintain freshness' or the phrase 'coated with food-grade vegetable-, petroleum-, beeswax-, and/or shellac-based wax or resin, to maintain freshness' as appropriate." And 21 CFR 101.100(a)(2), https://www.law.cornell.edu/cfr/text/21/101.100 — a food received in bulk at retail is exempt from package labelling if displayed with "(i) The labeling of the bulk container plainly in view … or (ii) A counter card, sign, or other appropriate device bearing prominently and conspicuously, but in no case with lettering of less than one-fourth of an inch in height, the information required to be stated on the label". FDA CPG Sec. 562.550 "Safety and Labeling of Waxed (Coated) Fruits and Vegetables" exists at https://www.fda.gov/regulatory-information/search-fda-guidance-documents/cpg-sec-562550-safety-and-labeling-waxed-coated-fruits-and-vegetables (listed in search results; not opened).

## 8. Controlled-atmosphere (CA) storage, 1-MCP (SmartFresh), and storage windows

### 8.1 Ontario — OMAFA (Ontario Ministry of Agriculture, Food and Agribusiness) via ONfruit

**(a)** ONfruit "What the Crop?! Apple Update: August 16, 2024", https://onfruit.ca/2024/08/16/what-the-crop-apple-update-august-16-2024/ — ONfruit is OMAFA's fruit extension blog; authors Erika DeBrouwer (Tree Fruit Specialist) and Kristy Grigg-McGuffin (IPM Specialist); the storage content is credited: "The following information was provided by Jennifer DeEll, OMAFA Horticulture Fresh Market Quality Specialist." Verbatim: "For successful long-term storage, apples must be harvested when they are physiologically mature but not fully ripe. Each cultivar must be harvested at the proper maturity to achieve maximum storage life and marketing season, while minimizing postharvest losses."

Table 1 (harvest maturity indices for long-term storage, as printed): Cultivar | Starch Index (1–8) | Firmness (lb) | Internal Ethylene (ppm): Ambrosia 2.5–4 | >17 | less than 1; Cortland 2.5–3.5 | >15 | >0.2 in <20%; Crispin/Mutsu 3.5–4.5 | >17 | >0.2 in <20%; Delicious (Red) 2.5–3.5 | >17 | >0.2 in <20%; Empire 2.5–3.5 | >17 | >0.2 in <20%; Gala 2.5–3 | >18 | less than 1; Golden Delicious 3–4 | >16 | >0.2 in <20%; Honeycrisp ~5 | >15 | Not useful; Idared 3–4 | >15 | Not useful; McIntosh 2.5–3.5 | >15 | >0.2 in <20%; Northern Spy 2.5–3.5 | >18 | >0.2 in <20%. (Footnotes: "Cornell Starch Iodine Index Chart (1-8)"; "No more than 0.2 ppm in less than 20% of apples".)

"Table 2. Common controlled atmosphere storage regimes for apples" (verbatim rows; source line: "Adapted from DeEll, J.R. 2020. Pome fruits: Apple quality and storage, p. 293-298. In: M.I. Gil and R. Beaudry (eds). Controlled and Modified Atmosphere for Fresh and Fresh-cut Produce. Elsevier Academic Press, United Kingdom."):

| Cultivar | O2 (%) | CO2 (%) | Temp (°C) | Comments |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Ambrosia | 1.2-1.7 | 1-1.5% | 0.5-1 | less than 1% O2 with fruit monitoring (SafePod, etc..); slow cooling with 1-MCP |
| Cortland | 1.5-2.5 | 1.5-2 | 0.5-2 | Highest temp with 1-MCP; sensitive to CO2 |
| Crispin / Mutsu | 1.5-2.5 | 1.5-2.5 | 0-0.5 | – |
| Delicious (Red) | 1-2 | 1-2 | 0-0.5 | less than 1% O2 fruit monitoring (SafePod, etc..) |
| Empire | 1.5-2.5 | 1-2 | 2 | Extremely sensitive to CO2; if not treated with DPA keep CO2 < 0.5% for 1st month |
| Fuji | 1.5-2 | 0.5-1 | 0-1 | Sensitive to CO2; delay CA with 1-MCP |
| Gala | 1-2 | 0.5-1.5 | 0.5-2 | Highest temp with 1-MCP; less than 1% O2 with fruit monitoring (SafePod, etc..) |
| Golden Delicious | 1-2 | 1-2 | 0-1 | less than 1% O2 with fruit monitoring (SafePod, etc..); slow stepwise cooling with 1-MCP |
| Granny Smith | 1.5-2 | 0.5-2 | 0-1 | less than 1% O2 with fruit monitoring (SafePod, etc..) |
| Honeycrisp | 2-3 | 1-3 | 3 (after conditioning at 10°C for 7 days) | Delay CA for 1st month |
| Idared | 1.5-2.5 | 1.5-2.5 | 0-1 | – |
| Jonagold | 1.5-2.5 | 1.5-2 | 0-1 | Highest temp with 1-MCP |
| McIntosh | 2.5 | 2 for 1st month, then 4.5 | 3 | Sensitive to CO2 with 1-MCP |
| Northern Spy | 1.5-2.5 | 1.5-2 | 0-0.5 | Sensitive to CO2 |
| Spartan | 2-3 | 2-2.5 | 0.5-1 | – |

The 2024 post also gives harvest-timing colour for that season: early cultivars being picked were "Sunrise, Paulared, Gingergold, and Zestar"; "Premier Honeycrisp harvest will begin shortly, with discussions of Gala harvest beginning in early region before the end of the month"; apples "roughly 14 days ahead in the early regions" (2024).

**(a)** Ontario Apple Growers blog "Why storage research is important to apple production", January 30, 2017, https://onapples.com/blog/why-storage-research-is-important-to-apple-production.php (opened via curl with a browser user-agent; the site shows a bot-verification interstitial to automated fetchers): "Apples stored in a commercial refrigerated or cold storage will keep for two to three months, but for long term storage (up to 12 months), apple growers use controlled atmosphere (CA) storage." Also: "Apples have long been stored in low oxygen environments, but until now, it had never been possible to determine how low the oxygen levels for a specific variety can go before the fruit's quality begins to suffer." (describing OMAFRA's SafePod project).

**Ontario availability windows by variety (a) — the association's published season, which is the practical "storage window" statement:** Ontario Apple Growers, "Ontario Apple Varieties", https://www.onapples.com/ontario-apple-varieties (© 2026; opened via curl): Gala (September to June); Honeycrisp (late September to June); Ambrosia (October to June); McIntosh (September to June); Red Delicious (October to July); Empire (October to July); Red Prince (February to June); Golden Delicious (October to May); Cortland (October to April); Idared (October to July); Fuji (mid-late October to April); Crispin (October to April); Jonagold (late September to February); Russet (Late October to January); Spartan (October to April); Northern Spy (October to May).

**OMAFRA "Commercial production of Ambrosia apples in Ontario"** https://www.ontario.ca/page/commercial-production-ambrosiatm-apples-ontario returned HTTP 403 on 29 Sep 2026 (see UNVERIFIED).

**(a)** PPQ release via Newswire, "Une récolte record en 15 ans : la saison des pommes du Québec est amorcée!", 29 August 2024, https://www.newswire.ca/fr/news-releases/une-recolte-record-en-15-ans-la-saison-des-pommes-du-quebec-est-amorcee--857544434.html: "427 productrices et producteurs de pommes"; "plus de 109 000 tonnes (5,6 millions de minots) de pommes" (average annual production); "revenus à la ferme de plus de 79 M$" (2022-2023); the 2024 harvest "la plus abondante des 15 dernières années". (The release's own "10 times longer refrigerated" storage tip is the association's claim; not needed.)

**(a-adjacent, dated)** Jennifer DeEll (OMAFRA, Simcoe), "Pre- and Postharvest 1-MCP Technology for Apples", slide deck hosted by Agri-Réseau (Réseau-pommier, Quebec), https://www.agrireseau.net/reseaupommier/documents/DeEll%20QC%20Talk%201.pdf (slides marked "DeEll, 2010"): "1-Methylcyclopropene (1-MCP) — Inhibits ethylene action, delays fruit ripening"; SmartFresh: "Volatile form — Postharvest application within an air-tight environment — Used commercially worldwide"; Harvista: "Orchard spray for tree fruit, within 2 weeks of harvest — Not registered in Canada yet" (2010 status; Harvista 1.3 SC is now registered — see 8.5); "SmartFresh — Reduces senescence related disorders i.e. senescent breakdown — Increases stress-related disorders i.e. external CO2 injury, flesh browning"; "For cultivars susceptible to CO2 injury e.g. 'McIntosh', 'Empire', 'Cortland' … If no DPA, keep CO2 low (<0.2%) for the first 60 days"; "SmartFresh on 'Honeycrisp' — reduces ethylene and greasiness, but has little effect on firmness (~0.5 lb)". Use only with the 2010 date stated.

### 8.4 Nova Scotia — Nova Scotia Fruit Growers' Association (NSFGA) and Perennia

**(a)** NSFGA, "Buy NS Apples", https://www.nsfruitgrowers.ca/buy-ns-apples (opened via curl): storage statement — "After apples are picked, they are brought by the farmer to different storage facilities. Each variety of apple can have different storage requirements! Controlled Atmosphere rooms, or 'CA' rooms, are used to remove the oxygen to slow the respiration rate and put the apples in a dormant state to slow their ripening process to extend the marketing season. When they are taken out of storage to be brought to the store, they taste as if they were just picked off the tree!" (association's claim). Varieties listed with the association's own descriptions (attributed): Ambrosia — "Ambrosia means 'Food of the Gods'. This apple variety is sweet, crisp, juicy and is best described as refreshing when you bite into one… The colour is almost entirely red with a distinctive red/ pink blush and faint stripes."; Cortland — "juicy white flesh with a tart yet sweet flavour… This apple is a cross between McIntosh and Ben Davis which came from Cornell University, NY in 1898."; Honeycrisp — "Nova Scotia's warm sunny days, and nights cooled by the ocean breeze makes our Honeycrisp apples sweeter and redder than all the others… this apple variety is a cross between a Keepsake and MN 1627 (an unreleased variety from the University of Minnesota)."; Gala — "creamy yellow flesh with a mild, sweet flavour. This variety has a thin skin…"; McIntosh — "originated as a wild seedling found by John McIntosh in St. Lawrence Valley, Canada. The flesh of this apple variety is white, crisp, and tart-sweet…"; Pazazz® — "a descendent of the beloved Honeycrisp…"; SweeTango® — "a cross between Honeycrisp and Zestar…". Also: "If you see any of the logos from the partners below, you know you are supporting local farmers. And if you aren't sure, ask your local retailer and they can help tell you which apples are local." NSFGA home page https://www.nsfruitgrowers.ca/: "Close to 95% of tree fruit production in Nova Scotia is apples, with the rest in pears, peaches, plums, and cherries." NSFGA "Economic Impact of Our Industry" https://www.nsfruitgrowers.ca/economic-impact-of-our-industry shows page images of "NSFGA Summary Economic Impact Assessment May 2024" ("Strong from Root to Stem") — figures not machine-readable (see UNVERIFIED).

**(a)** Perennia (Nova Scotia's provincial agri-food development agency), "Tree Fruits", https://www.perennia.ca/portfolio-items/tree-fruits/ (page dated 2026-05-12): "The tree fruit industry in Nova Scotia is predominantly apple production, with relatively small volumes of peach, pear, plum and sweet cherry production. The majority of apples are sold wholesale in Canada, with a growing export market for premium NS apples in the United States… The other major development is the replacement of processing varieties such as Gravenstein and McIntosh with plantings of new varieties including Honeycrisp, Ambrosia and 'club' varieties that are high-value in the fresh market."

**Nova Scotia harvest calendar by variety:** no tier (a) calendar found (NSFGA and Perennia pages opened contain none). See UNVERIFIED.

### 8.5 1-MCP (SmartFresh) — Canadian registration and label (a)

- Health Canada PMRA, "Re-evaluation Decision RVD2023-15, 1-Methylcyclopropene and Its Associated End-use Products", 26 September 2023, https://www.canada.ca/en/health-canada/services/consumer-product-safety/reports-publications/pesticides-pest-management/decisions-updates/reevaluation-decision/2023/1-methylcyclopropene.html — decision: "continued registration of products containing 1-methylcyclopropene is acceptable". Registered end-use products listed in Appendix I include: SmartFresh Technology (Reg. No. 27778, AgroFresh Inc.); SmartFresh SmartTabs (28781); SmartFresh ProTabs (30872); Ripelock Tabs 2.0 (31590); Harvista 1.3 SC (32752, AgroFresh); Fysium (33072, Janssen Pharmaceutica NV); Easyfresh (34124, Fine Agrochemicals Limited); Freshvue Pods (34549, Pace International, LLC); plus EthylBloc products for ornamentals. Pre-harvest use description: applied "3 to 21 days prior to apple harvest using ground application equipment to delay fruit maturation and prevents fruit drop" (Harvista-type use); post-harvest use: "fumigation treatment in airtight treatment area (container or room)".
- PMRA Pesticide Label Search, Reg. No. 27778: https://pr-rp.hc-sc.gc.ca/ls-re/result-eng.php?p_search_label=&searchfield1=REGNU&operator1=EXACT&criteria1=27778 — "SmartFresh Technology", Registrant "Agrofresh Inc.", status "Registered". Label PDF https://pr-rp.hc-sc.gc.ca/1_1/view_label?p_ukid=439349025 (label marked 2023-5473, 2024-05-22; "SMARTFRESH TECHNOLOGY™ Powder, Plant Growth Regulator, For post-harvest use on apples and pears only. FOR USE ONLY BY AGROFRESH-TRAINED OPERATORS… ACTIVE INGREDIENT: 1-Methylcyclopropene 3.3%"). Verbatim directions: "SmartFresh Technology is a postharvest tool for counteracting many of the undesirable effects of internal ethylene produced in harvested apple and pear fruit. SmartFresh Technology maintains fruit firmness through its effect in delaying the rise in internal ethylene production and respiration that occurs during ripening." — "SmartFresh Technology is compatible with and complementary to fruit stored under both controlled atmosphere and regular air fruit storage conditions. SmartFresh Technology must be used in air tight treatment areas." — "Harvested apples and pears must be exposed to the volatile active ingredient of SmartFresh Technology in air tight ambient temperature, refrigerated, or controlled atmosphere food storage facilities with a volume of at least 40 cubic metres." — "For best results, SmartFresh Technology should be used shortly after harvesting, prior to storage, shipment, or sale." — "Alternatively, for fruit that are cooled to 0-3°C within one day after harvest, SmartFresh Technology may be applied to the treatment room within 10 days after harvest." — "For fruit stored in a controlled atmosphere, any repeat application should be made within 240 days of harvesting." — "A maximum of four (4) applications may be made to any one lot of apples or pears." — Rate table: "Apple 5.8 – 6.8 grams per 100 m3 — Do not apply more than 6.8 g/100 m3 (equivalent to 1.0 ppm active ingredient) per treatment. Limit of four (4) treatments." — "The doors to the storage area should remain sealed for the 24 hours to ensure effective SmartFresh Technology treatment." — "For Apples: To reduce superficial scald, SmartFresh Technology should be applied within 3 days after harvest to apples that were cooled to 0-3°C within one day after harvest. Maximum reduction in superficial scald can be expected for apples harvested at optimum maturity and stored for up to 180 days."

**Grower-use statistics for 1-MCP by province:** none found at tier (a) — see UNVERIFIED.

# Who owns and licenses the apple varieties sold in Canada — research file

Prepared 29 Sep 2026. All URLs below were opened on 29 Sep 2026 unless a line says otherwise. Tier codes: **(a)** primary (government/regulator/court/company or breeder's own page); **(b)** named outlet with byline and date; **(c)** aggregator/SEO/Wikipedia/review — flag only, never a source; **(d)** named consumer survey. Items that could not be opened or confirmed at (a)/(b)/(d) are in the final section "UNVERIFIED / DO-NOT-USE".

House-rule reminders that apply to every line of this file: no health/nutrition/food-safety commentary; taste and texture language appears only as attributed quotation from a breeder or grower association; legal items carry allegation/finding/settlement/admission labels; U.S. facts are labelled as U.S. context; no retail prices are stated because no retailer page could be opened (see UNVERIFIED).

---

## 0. How rights work in Canada (regulator pages)

| Fact | Source (tier) | URL opened 29 Sep 2026 |
|---|---|---|
| Canada's Plant Breeders' Rights (PBR) Act came into force 1 Aug 1990. The rights-holder has the exclusive right to "Sell, and produce the variety in Canada for the purpose of selling, the propagating material of the protected variety" and to license third parties. Under the original Act protection lasted 18 years; a trademark, by contrast, "protects only the name and not the cultivar itself" and can run indefinitely. | BC Fruit Testers Association document "PICO, Plant Breeders' Rights and You" (1999), quoting PICO Operations Officer Ken Haddrell (a-/b: association document, dated, author not bylined) | https://www.bcfta.ca/docs/misc/plant-breeders-rights.pdf |
| CFIA PBR Office status page (last updated 2024-05-27) lists varieties by crop kind, denomination and botanical name. | CFIA (a) | https://inspection.canada.ca/en/plant-varieties/plant-breeders-rights/varieties |
| CFIA apple crop list (denominations with individual record links). Note: **Cripps Pink, Sunrise, Nicola/Salish as trade names, GD743/GS784 and "Arctic" do not appear** on the apple list as rendered. | CFIA (a) | https://active.inspection.gc.ca/english/plaveg/pbrpov/cropreport/appe.shtml |
| Ontario Apple Growers' own explanation of club varieties: "Traditionally, apple breeders would bring a new variety to market and charge apple growers a royalty fee for each tree sold to help fund more research and breeding activities. In order to standardize apple production and quality, some apple breeders began charging an additional marketing fee on every box of apples sold annually as well as limiting the number of growers who would have access to particular varieties." Names Pink Lady and Jazz as club examples Ontario consumers may recognize. | Ontario Apple Growers blog, "Club Varieties Explained", 8 Nov 2021 (a) | https://www.onapples.com/blog/club-varieties-explained (opened via curl; Cloudflare blocks ordinary fetch) |

CIPO trademark database: the search interface at https://ised-isde.canada.ca/cipo/trademark-search/ requires JavaScript; the page opened but returned an empty results grid. **No Canadian trademark registration numbers in this file are verified** (see UNVERIFIED). Ownership statements below come from the owners' own legal-notice pages instead.

Breeders David Bedford and Jim Luby. | UMN Landscape Arboretum blog, Lynette Kalsnes, 5 Nov 2025 (a) https://arb.umn.edu/blog/2025/11/05/legacy-one-million-apple |
| Parentage (UMN's page) | "Keepsake; (MN447 x Northern Spy) x unknown" | UMN Minnesota Hardy (a) https://mnhardy.umn.edu/honeycrisp |
| Patent status (Canada & U.S.) | UMN states verbatim: "Honeycrisp Apple Trees are no longer under patent protection in the U.S. or Canada." | UMN Minnesota Hardy (a) https://mnhardy.umn.edu/honeycrisp |
| Canadian PBR record | Denomination 'Honeycrisp'; applicant Regents of the University of Minnesota (St. Paul); breeders James Luby and David Scott Bedford; agent Ridout & Maybee, Toronto; application 1994-03-10 (No. 94-316); grant 2001-08-10, certificate 1007; **termination 2019-08-10 — rights expired.** | CFIA PBR record (a) https://active.inspection.gc.ca/english/plaveg/pbrpov/cropreport/app/app00000085e.shtml |
| U.S. context: plant patent | USPP7197, "Apple tree: Honeycrisp", inventors Jim Luby and David S. Bedford, original assignee Regents of the University of Minnesota, filed 7 Nov 1988, granted 20 Mar 1990; Google Patents shows status "Expired - Lifetime". | Google Patents rendering of USPTO record (U.S. context; aggregator of official record — verify against USPTO before quoting numbers) https://patents.google.com/patent/USPP7197P/en |
| U.S. context: royalty history | Fruit Growers News (Jan 2008, no byline shown) reported UMN received $1.30 per Honeycrisp tree sold under the patent, expiring Nov 2008, and that UMN "retains ownership of the Honeycrisp name". Treat as (b-) — outlet and date but no byline captured. | https://fruitgrowersnews.com/article/honeycrisp-patent-expiration-not-expected-to-change-sales/ |
| Name/trademark | UMN's page says "Honeycrunch® is a registered trademark owned by the Regents of the University of Minnesota" (the name used in Europe, NZ, Australia, Chile, South Africa). The Canadian trademark status of the word "Honeycrisp" itself is **not verified** (CIPO not searchable). | https://mnhardy.umn.edu/honeycrisp |
| UMN description (attributed, opinion) | UMN describes it as having an "Explosively crisp and juicy texture" and a "Well-balanced, sub-acid and mildly aromatic flavor" — UMN's words. | https://mnhardy.umn.edu/honeycrisp |
| Canadian growing — Ontario | Ontario acreage rank: Gala 19.9%, **Honeycrisp 16%**, McIntosh 12.2%, Ambrosia 11.0%, Red Delicious 5.6% (based on 2025 OAG Annual Report). 2025 yield share: Gala 22.0%, Honeycrisp 14.3%, Ambrosia 12.2%, McIntosh 9.3%. "The cultivar that yields the highest grower return in Ontario is Honeycrisp, although yields aren't consistent year to year." | OMAFA fruit specialists' ONfruit sheet "2026 Ontario Apple Industry" (a) https://onfruit.ca/wp-content/uploads/2026/06/A_01_Ontario-Apple-Industry.pdf ; publisher confirmed at https://onfruit.ca/about/ |
| Canadian growing — Ontario 2024 yield | Honeycrisp 61,360,000 lb (1,461,000 bu) in 2024, +34.2% vs 5-yr average. | OAG 21st Annual Report (2024), appendix "2024 Apple Yield Report by Variety" (a) https://www.onapples.com/uploads/images/files/OAG%20Annual%20Report%202024%20FINAL.pdf (opened via curl) |
| Canadian growing — Nova Scotia | Scotian Gold (grower-owned co-op, Coldbrook NS) names Honeycrisp among its premium varieties and says it handles "60 percent of the apple production in Atlantic Canada". USDA/FAS Ottawa (U.S. government report about Canada): "Nova Scotian growers have invested in Honeycrisp acreage". CBC (29 Dec 2010, CBC News, no individual byline): Honeycrisp "helped save Nova Scotia's tree fruit industry in 1996"; "About 70 per cent of the crop is exported to U.S. markets." | https://scotiangold.com/about/ (a); USDA FAS CA2025-0039, 2 Dec 2025, prepared by Alexandrea Watters (a, U.S. gov) https://apps.fas.usda.gov/newgainapi/api/Report/DownloadReportByFileName?fileName=Fresh+Deciduous+Fruit+Annual_Ottawa_Canada_CA2025-0039.pdf ; CBC (b-) https://www.cbc.ca/news/canada/nova-scotia/n-s-apple-growers-seek-sweetango-success-1.867361 |
| Canadian growing — Quebec, BC | Quebec government lists Honeycrisp among "new" varieties now grown locally; BC Tree Fruits lists Honeycrisp among its apples. | https://www.quebec.ca/agriculture-environnement-et-ressources-naturelles/agriculture/industrie-agricole-au-quebec/productions-agricoles/culture-pomme-pomiculture (a, updated 5 Mar 2026); https://www.bctreefruits.com/fruits/apples/ (a) |
| Imports | OMAFA sheet: "Canadian apple imports have changed drastically over the past 10 years, with Honeycrisp not being imported into Canada until 2018." Table 4 (Canadian fresh apple imports, lbs): Honeycrisp 7,886,711 lb in 2024. OAG 2024 report import table by province (2023, lbs): Honeycrisp imports Ontario 7,086,824; BC 4,306,615; total 11,967,041. | ONfruit sheet (a); OAG 2024 Annual Report p.35 (a) |
| Autumn shelf origin (what the sources support) | Ontario Apple Growers lists Ontario Honeycrisp availability "late September to June". Honeycrisp is grown commercially in ON, QC, NS and BC per the association/government pages above; imports (largely U.S.) are additional. | https://onapples.com/ontario-apple-varieties.php (a, via curl) |

U.S. patent text: "a naturally occurring chance seedling" in "the cultivated commercial orchard of Sally Mennell at Cawston, British Columbia, Canada", propagated by budding in 1990; possible parents "'Starking Delicious' and 'Golden Delicious'". | https://www.organicambrosiaapple.ca/ambrosia-apples/ambrosia-people/ (a: grower group's own site); Google Patents USPP10789 (U.S. context) https://patents.google.com/patent/USPP10789P/en |
| Canadian PBR record | Denomination 'Ambrosia' (Malus pumila); holders Sally Mennell and Wilfrid Mennell, Cawston BC; agent Summerland Varieties Corp.; application 1994-03-21 (No. 94-332); grant 1997-08-20, certificate 0388; **termination 2015-08-20 — expired.** | CFIA PBR record (a) https://active.inspection.gc.ca/english/plaveg/pbrpov/cropreport/app/app00000055e.shtml |
| U.S. context: patent | USPP10789 "Apple tree named 'Ambrosia'", inventors Wilfrid John Mennell and Sally Beth Mennell; original assignee Okanagan Plant Improvement Co Ltd; filed 19 Jun 1997, granted 16 Feb 1999; Google Patents shows "Expired - Lifetime" and a 2021 assignment to Mothertree Fruit Inc. | Google Patents (U.S. context; verify at USPTO) https://patents.google.com/patent/USPP10789P/en |
| Manager/licensor | PICO (Okanagan Plant Improvement Company) "was established in 1993, is owned by the B.C. Fruit Growers Association and is funded by the industry and the federal government"; "On behalf of the variety owners it represents, PICO collects royalties from every sale of commercially released varieties"; purchasers "sign a non-propagation agreement". The organic growers' site says PICO was "now renamed Summerland Varieties Corporation". SVC's home page lists Ambrosia among the trademarks it manages. | BCFTA PICO document 1999 (a-/b) https://www.bcfta.ca/docs/misc/plant-breeders-rights.pdf ; https://www.organicambrosiaapple.ca/ambrosia-apples/ambrosia-people/ ; https://summerlandvarieties.com/ (a) |

| Royalty/licence structure | SVC Budwood Catalogue 2024 lists Ambrosia with **no "Grower Agreement Required" flag and no per-tree royalty** (contrast Nicola/Salish at $1.25/tree). budwood.ca Ambrosia page still says "Release of the variety in the world will be under controlled planting and production agreements" and "Growers are urged to contact SVC when deciding to plant this variety." Good Fruit Grower (Geraldine Warner, 18 Jun 2015 — deck visible, body paywalled): "By 2019, Ambrosia will be an open variety in both the United States and Canada." | https://www.budwood.ca/files/Budwood%20Catalogue%202024.pdf (a); https://www.budwood.ca/varieties/product/3/Ambrosia/ (a); https://goodfruit.com/ambrosia-restrictions-to-end/ (b, deck only) |
| Where grown — BC | BC government: Ambrosia is "the top grown variety by producers in B.C."; the province has "approximately 400 commercial growers who cultivate about 15,000 acres" of tree fruits, 80% in the Okanagan. USDA/FAS (U.S. gov): 2025 B.C. crop hit by hail and heat, "with the Ambrosia crop particularly impacted." | https://www2.gov.bc.ca/gov/content/industry/agriculture-seafood/animals-and-crops/crop-production/tree-fruits (a); OAG 2024 report p.7 (a); USDA FAS CA2025-0039 (a, U.S. gov) |
| Where grown — Ontario | Ambrosia 4th by acreage (11.0%) and 3rd by 2025 yield (12.2%); 2024 production 54,470,000 lb, +58.2% vs 5-yr average. OAG lists Ontario Ambrosia availability "October to June". Blue Mountain Fruit Company (Thornbury ON) lists Ambrosia among its varieties. | ONfruit sheet (a); OAG 2024 report appendix (a); https://onapples.com/ontario-apple-varieties.php (a); https://bluemountainfruitcompany.com/red-prince/ (a, via curl) |
| Where grown — Quebec, Nova Scotia | Quebec government lists Ambrosia among newer varieties now cultivated locally; Scotian Gold (NS) lists Ambrosia. | Quebec gov page (a); https://scotiangold.com/about/ (a) |

## 3. Cosmic Crisp (WA 38)

| Item | Fact | Source / URL (29 Sep 2026) |
|---|---|---|
| Breeder & dates | Washington State University. CFIA record: "This variety originated from a cross between 'Enterprise' and 'Honeycrisp' made in 1997 in Wenatchee, Washington, USA"; selected 2004. PVM: seeds germinated 1998; Dr Kate Evans named as the WSU apple breeder evaluating since 2008. CFIA lists the breeder as Bruce H. Barritt (Okanagan Centre, BC). | CFIA (a) https://active.inspection.gc.ca/english/plaveg/pbrpov/cropreport/app/app00010507e.shtml ; PVM (a) https://provarmanagement.com/cosmic-crisp/ |
| Canadian PBR record | Denomination 'WA 38'; holder Washington State University Office of Commercialization, Pullman WA; agent Oyen Wiggs Green & Mutala LLP, Vancouver; application 2016-11-21 (No. 16-9038); grant 2022-09-08, certificate 6655; **termination 2047-09-08 — rights granted and in force.** | CFIA (a) same URL |
| Licensor | "Proprietary Variety Management (PVM) was engaged to interface IP management between the University and licensees." | WSU/CAHNRS case study "Cosmic Crisp WA 38 — A Case Study in Commercialization" (Oct 2016) (a) https://s3.wp.wsu.edu/uploads/sites/2002/2016/10/cosmic-crisp-wa-38-establishing-commercial-protections.pdf |
| Is it grown in Canada? | No — by licence design. PVM: "Cosmic Crisp® is exclusive to Washington State for all of North America." WSU case study: "sales of WA 38 trees are limited to Washington growers for at least the first decade"; "The 10 year exclusive rights for growers in North America is firmly in place"; "Presently, there are no plans for allowing any importation into North America" (i.e., of foreign-grown fruit). WSU FAQ: "Trees are available to all Washington State growers from 2018 forward." | PVM (a); WSU case study (a); WSU FAQ (a) https://treefruit.wsu.edu/cosmic-crisp-faqs/ |
| Exclusivity end date | FreshPlaza, Marieke Hemmes, 7 May 2026: Washington exclusivity for North America runs "Through May 26, 2032"; quotes Kevin Brandt (PVM): "After 2032, it is basically up to WSU to decide whether areas outside Washington can sell the apple with the trademark or not." Same article: over 22 million trees planted in Washington; 12 million 40-lb boxes in 2025-26; "Cosmic Crisp is prohibited from imports to protect Washington growers" (foreign-grown fruit cannot be imported into U.S./Canada/Mexico). | (b) https://www.freshplaza.com/north-america/article/9835916/washington-state-is-north-america-s-exclusive-grower-of-cosmic-crisp-apples-until-2032/ |
| Royalty structure (WSU's own FAQ; U.S. growers) | Tree royalty US$1.00 per tree; production royalty "4.75% of total box sale price if the price is at or above $20.00 USD. There is no production royalty if the box sells for less than $20.00"; farmgate/direct sellers pay $1.00 tree royalty plus "$2.00 USD per tree per year" from year two. | WSU FAQ (a) https://treefruit.wsu.edu/cosmic-crisp-faqs/ |
| Can Washington fruit be sold in Canada? | WSU FAQ: "Washington State grown fruit can be marketed anywhere in the world. There are no restrictions as to where your fruit can be sold." | WSU FAQ (a) |
| International licences (context) | PVM, 2 Dec 2016: "The first Cosmic Crisp international licenses have been signed and the first budwood has been shipped overseas" (two Italian companies). | PVM (a) https://provarmanagement.com/cosmic-crisp-to-be-grown-overseas-hort-show-preview/ |
| Canadian shelves | All Cosmic Crisp on Canadian shelves must therefore be imported Washington-grown fruit (from the exclusivity terms above). Canadian retailer product listings exist in search results (Sobeys, Loblaws, Real Canadian Superstore, Metro, Food Basics, Walmart.ca) but **none could be opened** — no price or displayed origin is recorded (see UNVERIFIED). | — |

## 4. Pink Lady® / Cripps Pink

| Item | Fact | Source / URL (29 Sep 2026) |
|---|---|---|
| Breeder & year | Western Australia Department of Agriculture breeding program (John Cripps). Opened at tier (a): DPIRD Research Library entry for "A tale of two ladies: Pink Lady and Sundowner", John Cripps and Eleanor Melvin-Carter, Journal of the Department of Agriculture, Western Australia, Series 4, Vol. 34 No. 2 (1993): "these crisp, sweet flavoursome apples have become top-selling, premium fruit" and "one million fruiting Pink Lady apple trees are needed to meet anticipated export sales" (authors' words). The commonly cited 1973 cross date and Golden Delicious × Lady Williams parentage were **not confirmed** on any (a)/(b) page opened (see UNVERIFIED). | https://library.dpird.wa.gov.au/journal_agriculture4/vol34/iss2/8/ (a) |
| Brand owner | APAL's own site: "Pink Lady® is a trademark of Apple and Pear Australia Limited and reserved for the exclusive use of licensees." APAL calls it "The world's first branded apple" and says it is "loved in over 120 countries". Caution: the APAL home page as served contained injected third-party spam text (Polish-language casino copy) — treat the page's integrity with care. | https://www.pinkladyapples.com/ (a, via curl) |
| Who holds North American / Canadian rights | PVM (U.S. company, Yakima WA) page: "On May 28, 1996, Brandt's Fruit Trees, Inc. was given registration in the United States for the trade name Pink Lady®"; 1999 Brandt's granted exclusive rights to Pink Lady USA LLC (later Pink Lady America); 2019 joint commercial agreement between Pink Lady America and APAL. Territory table on that page: US & Mexico — Pink Lady America; **Canada — Pink Lady America (exports only)**; international — APAL as master licensee. Brand covers cultivars Cripps Pink, Rosy Glow, Ruby Pink, Maslin, Barnsby, Pinkebelle. Pink Lady America is described as a PVM subsidiary. | https://provarmanagement.com/pink-lady/ (a: company's own page; U.S. company) |
| Canadian PBR | 'Cripps Pink' does **not** appear on the CFIA apple crop list as rendered — i.e., no Canadian PBR record was found for the cultivar. (The cultivar itself predates Canada's 1990 Act.) The brand protection in Canada is by trademark; CIPO record not verified. | CFIA apple list (a) https://active.inspection.gc.ca/english/plaveg/pbrpov/cropreport/appe.shtml |
| Canadian growers | BC Tree Fruits lists "Pink Lady®" among the apples it markets: "Originating in the late 20th century in Western Australia, the Pink Lady® comes from the parentage of Golde..." (truncated on page). Ontario Apple Growers names Pink Lady as a club variety "Ontario consumers may recognize" but does not list it among Ontario-grown varieties on its varieties page. | https://www.bctreefruits.com/fruits/apples/ (a); https://www.onapples.com/blog/club-varieties-explained (a) |
| U.S. legal item (U.S. context only) | Capital Press, Mateusz Perkowski, 13 Nov 2025: Brandt's Fruit Trees (Yakima WA) **alleged** Olsen Bros. Ranches (Prosser WA) sold apples under the Pink Lady brand from ~55,000 unlicensed 'Lady in Red' trees in 2018, 2020, 2022 and 2023. **Outcome: settlement, terms undisclosed; case dismissed with prejudice. No finding of liability and no admission is reported.** Olsen Bros.' attorney: "per the agreement between the parties, we are not able to comment on this resolution." No Canadian territory is mentioned. | (b) https://capitalpress.com/2025/11/13/dispute-over-pink-lady-apple-trademark-resolved/ |
| Autumn shelf origin | Imports table in OAG 2024 report does not break out Pink Lady (falls under "unspecified", which OMAFA notes rose 308% and "could be due to the rise of club cultivars"). BC production exists per BC Tree Fruits. Southern-hemisphere and U.S. shares of total imports: see Section 12. | OAG 2024 report p.35 (a); ONfruit sheet (a) |

| SVC itself | "Established during the earliest days of Plant Breeders' Rights in Canada"; provides "Intellectual Property Management for World-Class Tree Fruits"; manages trademarks Ambrosia, Snowflake, Sunpunch, Nicola, Salish, Aurora and the Summerland™ series. | https://summerlandvarieties.com/ (a) |
| Where grown / harvest | BC Tree Fruits lists Salish, Nicola and Sunrise among its apples. Salish harvest "Late September to first week of October (Summerland)"; Nicola "Mid to late season, after Ambrosia™"; Sunrise mid-Aug to early Sep. BC Tree Fruits on Salish (attributed): "The newest sensation from the apple breeding program in Summerland, BC. ## 10. Public (non-club) varieties — breeder, year, rights status

All of these have no Canadian PBR record on the CFIA apple list as rendered (their release predates the 1990 Act) and no per-tree royalty is listed for Spartan, Sunrise, Honeycrisp or McIntosh in SVC's 2024 catalogue. Names are generic; some are marketed under owners' logos (e.g., Summerland™ McIntosh trademark held by the Crown/AAFC).

| Variety | Breeder / origin & year (as sourced) | Source / URL (29 Sep 2026) | Canada growing & season (association/government) |
|---|---|---|---|
| McIntosh | Parks Canada national historic event designation (28 Oct 1999), plaque text: "In 1811, John McIntosh discovered an apple sapling on his land in Matilda township. By bringing about its propagation and wide dissemination, he and his family had a significant impact on Canada's fruit growing industry... A number of well-known hybrids, such as the Cortland, Empire, Lobo and Spartan, were derived from this fruit." Location: 11530 County Road 18, Dundela, Ontario. | https://www.pc.gc.ca/apps/dfhd/page_nhs_eng.aspx?id=1888 (a) | Quebec: McIntosh "plus du tiers de la production totale" (Quebec government); USDA/FAS (U.S. gov): "Quebec has the largest McIntosh acreage in Canada". Ontario: 3rd by acreage (12.2%); 36,940,000 lb in 2024, -15% vs 5-yr average; OAG season "September to June". BC Tree Fruits lists McIntosh. |
| Empire | Cornell (NYSAES, Geneva NY): "A McIntosh and Red Delicious cross"; "Seed developed in 1945; widespread introduction occurred in 1966". Roger Way named it (Cornell Chronicle, Krishna Ramanujan, 12 Jun 2019: Way joined NYSAES Geneva in 1953; "Empire is ninth in U.S. production"). | https://alumni.cornell.edu/cornellians/apple-varieties/ (a, Melissa Newcomb, 19 Sep 2024); https://news.cornell.edu/stories/2019/06/empire-apple-breeder-roger-way-dies-100 (a) | Ontario 2024: 18,435,000 lb (-27.8% vs 5-yr avg); OAG season "October to July". Quebec government lists Empire among main varieties after McIntosh. |
| Cortland | Cornell: "A cross between Ben Davis and McIntosh varieties", "Named after a neighboring county to Ithaca". The 1915 naming year was **not** confirmed on an opened Cornell page (search snippet only — UNVERIFIED). | https://alumni.cornell.edu/cornellians/apple-varieties/ (a) | Ontario 2024: 9,095,000 lb; OAG season "October to April". Quebec: among the main varieties. NS Dept of Agriculture (2009) lists Cortland among cultivars most in demand. |
| Spartan | Bred at the federal research station in Summerland, BC (AAFC). BC Tree Fruits (attributed): "The Spartan apple was the first new breed of apple created by the Pacific Agri-food Research Centre in Summerland." Trademark "Spartan" held by the Crown/AAFC per SVC legal notice. The 1936 introduction date and R.C. Palmer attribution were **not** confirmed at (a)/(b) (UNVERIFIED). | https://www.bctreefruits.com/fruits/apples/ (a); https://summerlandvarieties.com/legal-notices/ (a) | Ontario 2024: 2,255,000 lb; OAG season "October to April". Quebec: main variety after McIntosh. BC Tree Fruits lists Spartan. |
| Gala / Royal Gala | New Zealand origin (J.H. Kidd, 1930s; Kidd's Orange Red × Golden Delicious) — **only found at tier (c)/search-snippet level; Te Ara (NZ government encyclopedia) returned 403 twice.** UNVERIFIED for breeder/year. | — | Ontario: #1 by acreage (19.9%) and 2025 yield (22.0%); 79,635,000 lb in 2024; OAG season "September to June". Imports: Gala 83,851,657 lb in 2024 (OMAFA); "Gala imports have decreased substantially since 2014 with demand being filled locally." BC Tree Fruits lists Royal Gala. |
| Fuji | Japan, Tohoku horticultural research station at Fujisaki, Aomori; cross programme from 1939; 'Ro-628' (Delicious pollen × Ralls Janet/"Kokkoh") first fruited 1951; announced as "Tohoku number 7" in 1958 — per an Apples & People (UK heritage project) English translation of a Japanese account. **NARO's own English page returned connection resets; treat the translation as (c) — flag only.** | https://applesandpeople.org.uk/wp-content/uploads/2021/04/Fuji-Apple-60-Years-English.pdf (c) | Ontario 2024: 11,735,000 lb (+42.9% vs 5-yr avg); OAG season "mid-late October to April". BC Tree Fruits: "Originating in Japan, the Fuji is a cross between a Red Delicious and a lesser-known variety called Ralls Janet." (a) |
| Granny Smith | City of Ryde (NSW local government) history page: seedling on Maria Ann Smith's Eastwood farm, 1868 account; exhibited as "Smith's seedling" 1890; "Granny Smith's seedlings" won cooking-apple prize 1891; 1895 NSW Department of Agriculture Fruit Expert Albert H. Benson listed it as suitable for export and "initiated the first large scale cultivation of the apple at the Government Experimental Station in Bathurst." | https://www.ryde.nsw.gov.au/Library/Local-and-Family-History/Historic-Ryde/Historic-Figures/Granny-Smith (a) | Not in Ontario yield table (not a significant Ontario crop). Imports: Granny Smith 42,448,399 lb in 2024 (OMAFA), of which Ontario 29.2M lb and BC 14.2M lb in 2023 (OAG report). BC Tree Fruits lists Granny Smith. |
| Red Delicious | Stark Bro's (the nursery that commercialised it; company's own history page): chance seedling found by Jesse Hiatt, Peru, Iowa, originally named 'Hawkeye'; shown at Stark's 1893 fruit fair; Stark bought sole rights after the 1894 fair and renamed it "Stark® Delicious", later Red Delicious after Golden Delicious appeared in 1914. | https://www.starkbros.com/growing-guide/article/a-delicious-discovery (a) | Ontario: 5th by acreage (5.6%); 20,285,000 lb in 2024 (-21.3% vs 5-yr avg); USDA/FAS: acreage of McIntosh and Red Delicious "continues to decline" in Ontario; OAG season "October to July". Imports 2023: Red Delicious 27.1M lb total (BC 12.7M, ON 13.9M). |
| Golden Delicious | Stark Bro's: found on the farm of A. H. Mullins, West Virginia, 1914 ("A 1,000-mile railroad trip, plus a 20-mile horseback ride through West Virginia mountain wilds, brought me to the farm of Mr. A. H. Mullins" — Paul Stark's account). Purchase price not on the opened page. | https://www.starkbros.com/growing-guide/article/trail-of-the-golden-apple (a) | Ontario 2024: 9,340,000 lb; OAG season "October to May". Imports 2023: 8.3M lb. BC Tree Fruits lists Golden Delicious. |

## 11. Grower-return data (association survey, not shelf prices)

Ontario Apple Growers' 2023 marketing survey (average grower price for fresh apples, $/lb, representing all apples in the bin): Honeycrisp $0.47 (down 35% vs 2022), Gala $0.29 (down 21%), Ambrosia $0.25 (down 28%); McIntosh $0.17; Empire $0.10; Red Delicious $0.23; Spartan $0.27; Fuji $0.24; Golden Delicious $0.26; Cortland $0.24. Source: OAG 21st Annual Report (2024), pp. 9 and 33 (a) — https://www.onapples.com/uploads/images/files/OAG%20Annual%20Report%202024%20FINAL.pdf . These are farm-gate returns, not retail prices; no retail price appears in this file (rule 5; see UNVERIFIED).

## 12. Where autumn shelf apples come from — national statistics and trade

| Fact | Source (tier) | URL (29 Sep 2026) |
|---|---|---|
| 2024 Canadian apple production 386,368 t: Ontario 161,641 t (41.9%), Quebec 118,157 t (30.6%), BC 63,538 t (16.4%), other provinces 42,932 t (11.1%). Farm gate value $310.3M. "Ontario remains Canada's largest apple-producing province, accounting for close to 42% of the country's apple crop in 2024." | AAFC, Statistical Overview of the Canadian Fruit Industry 2024 (16 Jul 2025) (a) | https://agriculture.canada.ca/en/sector/horticulture/reports/statistical-overview-canadian-fruit-industry-2024 |
| Imports 2024: $269.5M, 164,852 t; United States ≈80% of fresh apple imports; "Canada is a net importer of apples." Exports 2024: $68.8M, 57,174 t, 78.9% to the U.S. | AAFC (a) | same |
| 2025: "production declined for lowbush blueberries (-12.6%) and apples (-9.7%)"; Atlantic provinces had "prolonged periods of below-average rainfall from July through November 2025". | Statistics Canada, The Daily, 16 Feb 2026 (a) | https://www150.statcan.gc.ca/n1/daily-quotidien/260216/dq260216c-eng.htm |
| Statistics Canada Table 32-10-0364-01 "Area, production and farm gate value of marketed fruits" (released 2026-02-16) — interface opened, data values not rendered in fetch. | StatCan (a) | https://www150.statcan.gc.ca/t1/tbl1/en/tv.action?pid=3210036401 |
| Acreage (2024, citing Statistics Canada): Canada 44,418 acres; Ontario 15,850; Quebec 12,750; BC 8,713; Nova Scotia 5,200 (+9.3%); New Brunswick 891. Ontario ~46% of Canadian volume in 2023. | OAG 2024 Annual Report (a) | URL in §11 |
| Ontario 2025 marketed production 326,234,000 lb (-17.9%); "Roughly 90% of apples produced in Ontario are sold into the fresh market". Canadian fresh apple imports 2024 total 282,043,159 lb; "Unspecified cultivars have seen an increase of 308% - this could be due to the rise of club cultivars (trademarked/patented)." | OMAFA ONfruit sheet (a) | https://onfruit.ca/wp-content/uploads/2026/06/A_01_Ontario-Apple-Industry.pdf |
| Import shares by origin, MY 2024/25 (Jul–Jun, metric tons): World 168,732; United States 137,023 (81.2%); New Zealand 8,686 (5.1%); Chile 8,232 (4.9%); China 2,704 (1.6%); EU 1,420 (Italy 1,260). FAS/Ottawa forecasts imports +4% in MY 2025/26 on a large U.S. crop; Canada approved market access for Ukrainian apples (Mar 2025) and eased Australian access (Sep 2025). Ontario produces "close to 50 percent of the country's apples"; "Gala and Honeycrisp varieties now dominate in Ontario production while acreage of McIntosh and Red Delicious varieties continues to decline". | USDA FAS Ottawa, Fresh Deciduous Fruit Annual CA2025-0039, 2 Dec 2025, prepared by Alexandrea Watters (a — U.S. government report about Canada; label as such) | https://apps.fas.usda.gov/newgainapi/api/Report/DownloadReportByFileName?fileName=Fresh+Deciduous+Fruit+Annual_Ottawa_Canada_CA2025-0039.pdf |
| Quebec: 395 apple enterprises (2022), ~110,000 t/yr, $66M; regions Montérégie 38%, Laurentides 14%, Capitale-Nationale 9%, Chaudière-Appalaches 9%, Estrie 6%; McIntosh over one-third of production, then Spartan, Cortland, Empire; Gala, Honeycrisp, SweeTango, Ambrosia now grown locally. | Gouvernement du Québec (a), page updated 5 Mar 2026 | https://www.quebec.ca/agriculture-environnement-et-ressources-naturelles/agriculture/industrie-agricole-au-quebec/productions-agricoles/culture-pomme-pomiculture |
| Quebec growers' association release, 4 Sep 2026: 422 producers; annual production over 256 million lb (6.1 million bushels); farm revenue ~$90M in 2024-25; harvest/u-pick September–October; Éric Rochon (president): "Choisir les pommes du Québec, c'est encourager des entreprises d'ici, soutenir notre autonomie alimentaire et profiter d'un fruit frais, polyvalent et accessible". | Les Producteurs de pommes du Québec via Newswire (a) | https://www.newswire.ca/fr/news-releases/la-saison-des-pommes-du-quebec-est-lancee-un-automne-a-croquer-du-verger-au-marche-public-808703373.html |
| Nova Scotia: "The Annapolis Valley of Nova Scotia is the centre of apple production on Canada's Eastern Seaboard"; ~2.5 million bushels/yr ("between eight and nine percent of the Canadian production"); at least 30 cultivars grown commercially; most in demand (harvest order): Gravenstein, McIntosh, Cortland, Gala, Honeycrisp, Spartan, Red Delicious, Golden Delicious, Idared, Northern Spy. (Report is dated Fall 2009 — old.) | NS Department of Agriculture, Lori Kittilsen, Fall 2009 (a, dated) | https://novascotia.ca/agri/documents/business-research/AppleProductionReport.pdf |
| BC Tree Fruits Cooperative: "established in 1936", Oliver BC; apple list on its site. (Note: the opened site does not mention any wind-down; reports of a 2024 closure are UNVERIFIED here.) | BC Tree Fruits (a) | https://www.bctreefruits.com/ ; https://www.bctreefruits.com/fruits/apples/ |
| Ontario seasonal availability by variety (OAG): Gala Sep–Jun; Honeycrisp late Sep–Jun; Ambrosia Oct–Jun; McIntosh Sep–Jun; Red Delicious Oct–Jul; Empire Oct–Jul; Red Prince Feb–Jun; Golden Delicious Oct–May; Cortland Oct–Apr; Idared Oct–Jul; Fuji mid-late Oct–Apr; Crispin Oct–Apr; Jonagold late Sep–Feb; Russet late Oct–Jan; Spartan Oct–Apr; Northern Spy Oct–May. | OAG (a) | https://onapples.com/ontario-apple-varieties.php |

**Bottom line for "where the autumn apple came from" (all sourced above):** In September–October, Canadian-grown Gala, Honeycrisp, Ambrosia, McIntosh, Empire, Cortland, Spartan, Sunrise/Salish/Nicola (BC) and SweeTango (NS/QC) are in season per the association/government pages. Cosmic Crisp, Envy, Jazz, Arctic and (per T&G/PVM/OSF pages) any Pink Lady not grown in BC are imports — overwhelmingly from Washington State in autumn (U.S. ≈80–81% of all Canadian apple imports per AAFC and USDA/FAS). Red Prince is Canadian-grown but stored and released in winter.

Everything below could not be confirmed at tier (a)/(b)/(d) on 29 Sep 2026. Pointers show where a verifier might look.

1. **Retail prices and displayed origin for any variety.** Sobeys (https://www.sobeys.com/products/apple-cosmic-crisp-), Loblaws (https://www.loblaws.ca/en/apple-cosmic-crisp/p/21240884001_KG), Metro (https://www.metro.ca/en/online-grocery/aisles/fruits-vegetables/fruits/apples-pears/apple/p/3507), Walmart.ca (https://www.walmart.ca/en/ip/cosmic-crisp/6000207926111) and Real Canadian Superstore all returned 403/bot-check. No $/kg or $/lb figure appears in this file. Do not use search-snippet prices.

CIPO search UI requires JavaScript; returned empty grid. Owner statements in this file come from owners' legal notices, not the register.
3. **Trademark status of the word "Honeycrisp" in Canada/U.S.** UMN page mentions only Honeycrunch®. FGN 2008 (no byline) says UMN "retains ownership of the Honeycrisp name" — treat as unconfirmed. Also the "$16.5 million total royalties" and "$8 million" figures seen only in search snippets — do not use.
4. **Ambrosia royalty amount (Can$2/tree) and the exact year Ambrosia became "open" in Canada.** Good Fruit Grower body paywalled (https://goodfruit.com/ambrosia-restrictions-to-end/); Country Guide "The apple from heaven" (https://www.country-guide.ca/guide-business/the-apple-from-heaven/) returned 403; ambrosiaapples.ca (all pages) returned 503/connection reset. Also unverified: any BC Ambrosia promotion levy / New Variety Development Council arrangement; BC gov 2013 release https://news.gov.bc.ca/releases/2013AGRI0029-001091 returned 503; Ontario gov Ambrosia production page https://www.ontario.ca/page/commercial-production-ambrosiatm-apples-ontario returned 403. Ambrosia BC acreage "~2,100 acres" (search snippet, 2020) — do not use. Check the BC 2025 Tree Fruit Acreage Report PDF linked from the BC gov tree-fruits page (not opened).
5. **PICO's current corporate status/ownership and the PICO→SVC relationship in detail.** picocorp.com is a parked domain; Good Fruit Grower "PICO has new direction" returned 404; Mothertree Fruit Inc. ownership not opened anywhere. The statement that SVC holds "exclusive evaluation, distribution, propagation and commercialization rights from the Minister of AAFC" appeared only in a search snippet.
6. **Cosmic Crisp:** the U.S. plant patent number and WSU's Cosmic Crisp trademark registration were not opened; cosmiccrisp.com returns empty/202 pages (JS-rendered). Whether Canadian retailers currently stock it is inferred from search-result URLs only.
7. **Pink Lady/Cripps Pink breeding facts** (John Cripps, 1973 cross, Golden Delicious × Lady Williams, DAFWA holds PBR in Australia) — only in search snippets/(c) pages; APAL industry pages (https://apal.org.au/industry-info/pink-lady/ , https://apal.org.au/pink-lady/) returned 403; pinkladyamerica.com home page had no substantive text. Which Canadian growers (other than BC Tree Fruits' listing) hold Pink Lady licences — not found. APAL's "number one apple brand in the UK" claim cites Worldpanel by Numerator 52 w/e 2 Nov 2025 — survey methodology not opened, so do not use as (d).
8. **Envy/Jazz:** commercial launch years (Jazz 2004, Envy 2008) and "40 years" story appear only in search snippets (Bioeconomy Science Institute page https://www.bioeconomyscience.co.nz/... returned 403; FreshPlaza/Fruitnet not opened). Any Canadian Envy/Jazz growing — none found; absence is not proof.
9. **SweeTango Quebec licensee name** ("Jean-Yves Boileau et Fils") — from a search snippet of Growing Produce (403 on open). sweetango.com "Canada" regional pages redirect to a game page. Number of NS growers within Scotian Gold growing SweeTango (22, 45 NBT partners) — snippet only.
10. **Red Prince** trademark registration in Canada; royalty terms between Wilton International and the Botdens; the "Red Jonaprince" U.S. patent expiry (~2017) and the "Elstar/Jonathan" parentage claims — snippet/(c) only. redprinceapple.ca and bluemountainfruitcompany.com were only reachable via curl with a browser user-agent.

# Apple variety dossiers (set A) — McIntosh, Empire, Cortland, Spartan, Honeycrisp, Gala/Royal Gala, Ambrosia, Red Prince — as sold in Canada

Research date: all URLs below were opened on **29 Sep 2026** unless a line says otherwise. Every fact carries the URL actually opened. Where a page could not be opened, the item is in the final **UNVERIFIED / DO-NOT-USE** section.

House-rule compliance notes for the writer
- No health, nutrition, antioxidant, sugar, glycemic, pesticide-residue, vitamin or food-safety content is included anywhere below. Some source pages (notably redprinceapple.ca FAQ and onapples.com "Health benefits" tab) contain such content; it was deliberately not extracted and must not be used.
- Taste/texture words appear **only** inside attributed verbatim quotes from a breeder, grower association, or the variety's own rights-holder/marketer, and are labelled as that party's description.
- Tier labels: **(a)** primary (government statistics, regulator, association's/company's/breeder's own page or report); **(b)** named outlet with byline and date; **(c)** aggregator/Wikipedia/SEO/flyer-aggregator = flag only, never a source; **(d)** named consumer survey with methodology (none used).
- U.S. material is labelled "U.S. context" and is never presented as Canadian. The USDA FAS/Ottawa report is a U.S. government report *about Canada*; it is labelled as such wherever used.
- No legal/regulatory item below alleges wrongdoing. Plant Breeders' Rights records are administrative registrations, not proceedings.

---

## 1. Cross-variety reference tables

### 1.1 Ontario acreage by variety (OAG tree census, Agricorp/OAG ADaMS DMS; "includes bearing and non-bearing acreage") — S3, PDF pp. 35–37

| Variety | 2024 acres | % of 15,889 | 2023 acres | 2019 acres | Change 2024 vs 2019 (as printed) | Acres planted ≥31 yrs ago (pre-1994) |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Gala | 3,162 | 19.9% | 3,058 | 2,520 | +17% (column headed "2025 vs 2021" as printed) | 62 |
| Honeycrisp | 2,538 | 16.0% | 2,365 | 1,830 | +14% | 2 |
| McIntosh | 1,943 | 12.2% | 1,992 | 2,556 | −14% | 1,217 |
| Ambrosia | 1,740 | 11.0% | 1,701 | 1,260 | +8% | 1 |
| Empire | 848 | 5.3% | 891 | 1,135 | −18% | 553 |
| Cortland | 391 | 2.5% | 403 | 416 | −7% | 116 |
| Spartan | 190 | 1.2% | 183 | (not in 5-yr table) | — | 160 |
| Red Prince | listed only inside "*Other Cultivars" (1,584 acres, 10.0%) — no separate acreage published | | | | | |

Notes: S3 p.35 title "2024 Ontario Apple Tree Acreage By Variety, By District"; p.36 "…By Tree Age"; p.37 "Ontario Apple Acreage by Variety, by Year (2020 – 2024) 5-Year Acreage Analysis". The 5-year table's percentage columns are headed "2025 vs 2024" and "2025 vs 2021" although the data columns are 2024…2019; reproduced as printed. S4 (2024 report) p.29 gives the prior census: Gala 3,060 (19.3%), Honeycrisp 2,366 (14.9%), McIntosh 1,988 (12.5%), Ambrosia 1,701 (10.7%), Empire 891 (5.6%), Cortland 403 (2.5%), Spartan 183 (1.2%) of 15,850 acres.

OMAFA summary (S5, "Table 1. Cultivar Ranking Based on Acreage", source given as "Ontario Apple Growers 2025 Annual Report"): Gala 19.9%, Honeycrisp 16%, McIntosh 12.2%, Ambrosia 11.0%, Red Delicious 5.6%.

| Empire | 20,603 | 18,435 | 15,300 | −17.0% | 23,152 | −33.9% |
| Gala | 90,155 | 80,135 | 80,350 | +0.3% | 80,026 | +0.4% |

OMAFA (S5, Table 2 "Cultivar Ranking Based on Yield", 2025): Gala 22.0%, Honeycrisp 14.3%, Ambrosia 12.2%, McIntosh 9.3%. S5 also: "Roughly 90% of apples produced in Ontario are sold into the fresh market … Roughly 10% … processing or juice." and "The cultivar that yields the highest grower return in Ontario is Honeycrisp, although yields aren't consistent year to year."

### 1.3 Ontario grower returns (not retail prices) — OAG Marketing Survey, fresh $/lb, S3 p.39 (2024) and S4 p.33 (2023)

| Variety | Fresh $/lb 2022 | 2023 | 2024 | Net return/840-lb bin 2024 |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Ambrosia | 0.34 | 0.25 | 0.31 | $262 |
| Cortland | 0.25 | 0.24 | 0.19 | $161 |
| Empire | 0.20 | 0.10 | 0.21 | $174 |
| Gala | 0.37 | 0.29 | 0.36 | $304 |
| Honeycrisp | 0.73 | 0.47 | 0.67 | $561 |
| McIntosh | 0.23 | 0.17 | 0.21 | $177 |
| Spartan | 0.31 | 0.27 | not listed separately in 2024 | $225 (2023) |

OAG text (S3 p.10): "Average grower returns for fresh market apples increased by $0.08/lb. compared to 2023 across all varieties … The three historically highest-returning varieties were: Honeycrisp at $0.67/lb. (up 42%), Gala at $0.36/lb. (up 24%), Ambrosia at $0.31/lb. (up 24%)". OAG text (S4 p.9): "Returns to growers for the 2023 crop were well below expectations… Honeycrisp at $0.47/lb. (down 35%), Gala at $0.29/lb. (down 21%), and Ambrosia at $0.25/lb. (down 28%)."

### 1.4 British Columbia acreage/production by variety — S15 (Tables 7 and 8; production-insurance data) and S16

| Variety | BC insured acres 2026 (S15 T7) | BC production 2026 (tons, S15 T8) | BC acres 2021 (S16 Table 3.2.1) | % of BC apple acres 2021 |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Ambrosia | 2,274 | 41,649 | 2,332 | 36.9% |
| Gala | 1,631 | 24,095 | 2,021 | 32.0% |
| Honeycrisp | 599 | 10,650 | 527 | 8.3% |
| Mac (McIntosh) | 325 | 4,636 | 362 | 5.7% |
| Spartan | 183 | 3,143 | 293 | 4.6% |
| Total apples | 5,682 | 95,422 | — | — |

S16 verbatim: "Ambrosia and Gala apples are by far the most popular varieties in the province. Combined, Ambrosia and Gala apples account for around 69% of all apple acreage in B.C." and "Gala, McIntosh, and Spartan acreage declined, while Ambrosia and Honeycrisp varieties increased. In 2011, Gala apples represented the largest acreage in the province. After 2019, Ambrosia overtook Gala. Similarly, Honeycrisp overtook Spartan and McIntosh acreage around the same time."
S15 verbatim (Executive Summary): "Over that time, B.C. tree fruit acreage has shifted decisively from apples toward cherries, with cherries becoming the largest tree fruit planted by about 2023." and "The industry is consolidating around a smaller number of commercially successful varieties, particularly Staccato and Sentennial cherries, Ambrosia and Gala apples." S15 apple section: "As noted, apple acreage has seen a steady decline over the past decade, though production trends remain far more stable…"; "Table 7) and production (Table 8) are increasingly concentrated in Ambrosia, Gala and Honeycrisp". S15 South Okanagan: "The main stories in the South Okanagan have been the decline of apple acreage in general, the decline of Ambrosia and Gala in particular…" (Empire, Cortland and Red Prince do not appear in the BC top-12 tables.)
S17 (Province of B.C. page, 22 Sep 2026): "Ambrosia apples are the top grown variety by producers in B.C."

### 1.5 Quebec — variety shares and stats (no per-variety acreage table found at tier a for 2024–26; see UNVERIFIED)

- S10 (Gouvernement du Québec, updated 5 Mar 2026): 2022: "395" entreprises; average annual production "110 000 tonnes"; receipts "66 millions $"; McIntosh "plus du tiers de la production totale" of dessert apples, followed by Spartan, Cortland and Empire; 2023 fresh-apple imports into Quebec "18,7 millions $", exports "2,4 millions $".
- S11 (MAPAQ Bioclips, 9 Mar 2021) verbatim: "Les quatre variétés de pommes traditionnellement produites au Québec, la McIntosh, la Cortland, la Spartan et l'Empire, composaient 77 % des pommes récoltées en 2018, avec une part de près de 34 % pour la McIntosh." Also: "des variétés qui étaient auparavant importées en totalité, comme la Honeycrisp et la Gala, sont maintenant produites au Québec en volume suffisant pour être commercialisées en épicerie durant une partie de l'année"; "la part de la production de pommes destinée au marché frais … se situe en moyenne à 45 % au Québec contre 77 % en Ontario pour la période de 2014 à 2019"; "L'omniprésente McIntosh est plus sujette aux blessures, ce qui entraîne son déclassement aux postes d'emballage."
- S3 p.7 (OAG citing Statistics Canada): "Quebec's acreage decreased by 2.9% to 12,721 acres and British Columbia's by 11.7% to 8,549 acres … Nova Scotia up 12.9% (5,177 acres), New Brunswick up 86% (total 1,028 acres), and PEI with 296 acres." (Ontario "relatively stable"; Canada 43,700 acres in 2024 vs 44,092 in 2015.)
- S36 (La Presse Canadienne, 5 Sep 2026): PPQ 2026 estimate 5.7 million minots (~19 kg each), "dans la moyenne"; PPQ president Éric Rochon quoted; Honeycrisp "arriving around September 20"; Quebec apples' grocery market share 55%, target 70%.

### 1.6 Nova Scotia

- S12 (NSFGA "Buy NS Apples"): lists Ambrosia, Cortland, Honeycrisp, Gala, McIntosh, Pazazz®, SweeTango® with descriptions (quoted in dossiers). No harvest calendar or statistics on the page.
- S13 (NS Dept. of Agriculture, Fall 2009 — dated, use only as historical context): "Nova Scotia currently produces an annual average of approximately 2.5 million bushels"; cultivars "most in demand … (Listed in order of Harvest): Gravensteins, McIntosh, Cortland, Gala, Honeycrisp, Spartan, Red Delicious, Golden Delicious, Idared, Northern Spy".
- S31 (U.S. gov report about Canada, Dec 2025): "Nova Scotian growers have invested in Honeycrisp acreage and that variety has been reported as being particularly impacted, with sizing down."
- 2026 crop forecast (NSFGA, quoted by FreshPlaza with no byline — **tier c, flag only**): 2.2 million bushels vs 1.7 million in 2025. See UNVERIFIED.
