S9 verbatim (PPQ, 4 Sep 2026): "Dès la fin août, les premières pommes comme la Paulared et la Sunrise font leur apparition. Puis, au fil du mois de septembre et jusqu'à la mi-octobre, plusieurs autres variétés s'ajoutent, dont la Ginger Gold, la Lobo, la McIntosh, la Gala, la Honeycrisp, la Spartan, la Cortland, l'Empire, la Délicieuse rouge et l'Ambrosia." S9 caveat: "les périodes de disponibilité peuvent varier légèrement selon les régions, les conditions météo et les vergers."
S7 (OMAFA, 22 Aug 2026): early cultivars Sunrise, Zestar!, Gingergold, Paulared being harvested in southern Ontario; "Generally, growers are stating good size with lower yields in comparison to normal."

### 1.8 Imports — who supplies Canada, and which of these varieties are imported

**Canada-level (a):** S18 (AAFC, 2024): fresh apple imports $269,485,000 CAD; 164,852 t; exports $68,787,000 / 57,174 t; production 386,368 t (Ontario 161,641 t = 41.9%; Quebec 118,157 t = 30.6%; BC 63,538 t = 16.4%); apple area 17,685 ha; farm gate value $310,263,000.

**By source country (U.S. government report about Canada, S31, Table 2, marketing year July–June, metric tons, source "Trade Data Monitor"):**

| MY | World | United States | U.S. share | Chile | New Zealand |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 2022/23 | 184,906 | 149,979 | 81.1% | 12,715 | 9,501 |
| 2023/24 | 163,871 | 140,182 | 85.5% | 6,993 | 8,696 |
| 2024/25 | 168,732 | 137,023 | 81.2% | 8,232 | 8,686 |

S31 verbatim: "A large Washington apple crop is also going to pressure the B.C. market and apple pricing."; "Canadian retailers are likely to take advantage of the large U.S. apple crop when sourcing and imports will be supported as a result."; "Canada also approved market access for Ukrainian apples in March 2025 with the first shipments reported to begin arriving in Fall 2025."

**By variety (OAG, Statistics Canada data, lbs, "crop year"; "The province denotes the port of entry and may not necessarily reflect the final provincial destination") — S3 p.41 (2024 crop year) and S4 p.35 (2023 crop year):**

| Variety | Canada imports 2024 crop yr | Ontario port-of-entry 2024 | Canada 2023 crop yr | Canada 5-yr avg 2020–24 |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Honeycrisp | 7,886,711 | 4,246,431 | 11,967,041 | 8,405,756 |
| Gala | 83,851,657 | 40,120,947 | 79,246,223 | 89,467,041 |
| McIntosh | 0 | 0 | 44,419 | 69,730 |
| Unspecified | 120,039,138 | 38,685,497 | 106,123,925 | 105,342,554 |
| Total | 282,043,159 | 125,749,710 | 277,695,650 | 279,747,567 |

(Empire, Cortland, Spartan, Ambrosia and Red Prince are not separate lines in the Statistics Canada import classification used by OAG; any such imports would sit in "Unspecified".)

OMAFA reading of the same data (S5, "Canadian Fresh Apple Imports", Table 4, lbs): Gala imports 80,012,865 (2014) → 83,851,657 (2024); Honeycrisp 7,886,711 (2024; "first year of imports" 2018); "Gala imports have decreased substantially since 2014 with demand being filled locally."; "Unspecified cultivars have seen an increase of 308% – this could be due to the rise of club cultivars (trademarked/patented)."

Scale check (for the writer, arithmetic on the sources above): Ontario's own 2024 Gala production was 80,135,000 lbs (S3) versus 83,851,657 lbs of Gala imported into all of Canada in the 2024 crop year (S3); Ontario's 2024 Honeycrisp production 61,360,000 lbs versus 7,886,711 lbs imported nationally; Ontario McIntosh 37,440,000 lbs versus 0 imported.

**Washington as the origin (b):** S33 (The Grower, 28 Apr 2025): Brian Rideout notes Canada traditionally imports apples from Washington State annually. S34 (The Grower, 21 Mar 2026): Washington state export volume to Canada for August–December 2025 was "11.1% lower than same period year ago"; Washington Gala "15.4% lower", Honeycrisp "19.7% lower" with prices "56.3% higher compared to a year ago" (as reported by The Grower). S32 (The Grower, 16 Feb 2025): five provincial associations (Québec, BC, Ontario, Nova Scotia, New Brunswick) wrote to the Prime Minister asking that apples be included in any retaliatory tariffs; letter quoted: "Canadian apples are grown commercially in five provinces and have a farm gate value of $265 million."; "53 per cent of all U.S. apple exports are shipped to Canada and Mexico."

**Month-by-month import data were not obtained** (see UNVERIFIED). The statements that can be supported: (i) the U.S. supplies 81–86% of Canadian fresh-apple imports by volume (S31); (ii) Gala is the largest named imported variety and is imported year-round in volume comparable to Ontario's entire Gala crop (S3); (iii) McIntosh imports are effectively nil (S3, S4).

### 1.9 Storage — published regimes and durations

**Ontario (a), S6 — OMAFRA/ONcore "Common controlled atmosphere storage regimes for apples", adapted from DeEll, J.R. 2020 (no durations given in the table):**

| Cultivar | O2 (%) | CO2 (%) | Temp (°C) | Comments (verbatim) |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Ambrosia | 1.2–1.7 | 1–1.5 | 0.5–1 | "<1% O2 with fruit monitoring (SafePod, etc..); slow cooling with 1-MCP" |
| Cortland | 1.5–2.5 | 1.5–2 | 0.5–2 | "Highest temp with 1-MCP; sensitive to CO2" |
| Empire | 1.5–2.5 | 1–2 | 2 | "Extremely sensitive to CO2; if not treated with DPA keep CO2 <0.5% for 1st month" |
| Gala | 1–2 | 0.5–1.5 | 0.5–2 | "Highest temp with 1-MCP; <1% O2 with fruit monitoring (SafePod, etc..)" |
| Honeycrisp | 2–3 | 1–3 | 3 (after conditioning at 10°C for 7 days) | "Delay CA for 1st month" |
| McIntosh | 2.5 | 2 for 1st month, then 4.5 | 3 | "Sensitive to CO2 with 1-MCP" |
| Spartan | 2–3 | 2–2.5 | 0.5–1 | — |
| Red Prince | not in table | | | |

**Storage-holdings reporting (a):** S3 p.10: "OAG continues to collect storage holdings for the industry. This data is entered into AAFC's InfoHort system and published on their website." S31 Figure 2 (U.S. gov report, source "Infohort, Agriculture and Agri-Food Canada") plots Canadian storage holdings Dec–Jun for Honeycrisp, McIntosh, Gala, Ambrosia, Cortland, Empire, Red Delicious — i.e., all seven of these are held in storage into spring. (Figure values not machine-readable.)

**Durations (breeder / U.S. context only, labelled):**
- Honeycrisp, breeder (S23, U. Minnesota): "outstanding flavor and texture can be maintained for at least seven months in refrigerated storage without atmosphere modification" and "ten or more months in CA storage" is possible (disorders noted); "should not be stored at 32° F or lower." (These are the breeder's own claims.)
- Gala, **U.S. context** (S42, Cornell, 17 Sep 2024): "Store Gala at 38°F"; maximum 4 months in air without PGR treatment; CA options "5% oxygen and 1% CO2 recommended for long term storage" or "2% oxygen and 1% CO2 for standard CA".
- McIntosh, **U.S. context** (S43, U. Maine): "For varieties that soften quickly after harvest such as McIntosh, CA storage may be beneficial for shorter storage durations of two to four months." and "For fruit that will be marketed four months or longer after harvest, CA storage can prevent losses."
- Red Prince, company (S27 FAQ): "cellared from the fall until the new year"; OAG availability "February to June" (S1).
- **No OMAFRA month-by-month table of regular-cold-storage vs CA life by variety was found** (the legacy OMAFRA "apstorage" page returns 404 and https://www.ontario.ca/page/apple-storage returns 404) — see UNVERIFIED. The OAG availability windows (S1) are the best tier-(a) proxy for how long each Ontario variety is marketed from storage.

---

### 2.1 McIntosh

| Item | Finding | Source (opened 29 Sep 2026) |
|---|---|---|
| Origin | Parks Canada designation "Discovery and Development of the McIntosh Apple National Historic Event", designated 28 Oct 1999, at 11530 County Road 18, Dundela, Ontario. Statement of significance (as extracted): in 1811 John McIntosh found an apple sapling on his Matilda township property; through his family's efforts to propagate and distribute it they significantly influenced Canada's fruit industry; the McIntosh became the parent of hybrids including Cortland, Empire, Lobo and Spartan and remains "one of the most popular varieties grown in Canada and abroad." Parks Canada also describes the apple as having "a highly desirable taste, texture, aroma and appearance" (Parks Canada's words). | S22 https://www.pc.gc.ca/apps/dfhd/page_nhs_eng.aspx?id=1888 |
| Origin (association) | PPQ: "Cette variété a été découverte en Ontario et introduite dans les vergers en 1836." | S8 https://lapommeduquebec.ca/varietes/mcintosh/ |
| Where grown / how much | Ontario: 1,943 acres (12.2%, 3rd), of which 1,217 acres planted pre-1994; 2025 production 33,960,000 lbs (−9.3% y/y; −16.4% vs 5-yr avg); 2024: 22.85M lbs fresh + 14.59M lbs "other processing". Quebec: "plus du tiers de la production totale" (Gouv. du Québec, 5 Mar 2026); 34% of 2018 harvest (MAPAQ). BC: 325 insured acres, 4,636 tons (2026). Canada: FAS/Ottawa (U.S. gov) — "Quebec has the largest McIntosh acreage in Canada". | S3, S10, S11, S15, S31 |
| Acreage trend | Ontario 2,556 acres (2019) → 1,943 (2024). U.S.-gov report: "acreage of McIntosh and Red Delicious varieties continues to decline as producers replace older orchards with varieties that are more in demand with today's consumer." BC 2021 report: "Gala, McIntosh, and Spartan acreage declined". | S3 p.37; S31; S16 |
| Harvest/availability | Ontario "September to June" (OAG). Quebec: available "À l'année"; harvest in September to mid-October (PPQ 2026 calendar text). NS: 2nd in harvest order after Gravenstein (2009 list). | S1, S8, S9, S13 |
| Storage | OMAFRA CA regime: 2.5% O2; CO2 2% first month then 4.5%; 3 °C; "Sensitive to CO2 with 1-MCP". U.S. context (U. Maine): CA "beneficial for shorter storage durations of two to four months" for varieties that soften quickly "such as McIntosh". Held in Canadian storage Dec–Jun per InfoHort chart. | S6, S43, S31 |
| Imports | Canada imported **0 lbs** of McIntosh in the 2024 crop year (44,419 lbs in 2023; 5-yr avg 69,730 lbs). | S3 p.41, S4 p.35 |
| Public/licensed | Public (a 19th-century chance seedling; no PBR record sought — not a protected variety). | S22 |
| Association description (verbatim, attributed) | PPQ (FR): "La McIntosh est une pomme excellente à croquer et en compote. On la préfère en raison de son jus abondant et sucré. Cette variété demeure très populaire auprès des consommateurs québécois. Le Québec, avec ses automnes frais, offre d'ailleurs des conditions exceptionnelles pour cette variété." Tag line: "Croquante / juteuse / aromatique". NSFGA: flesh "white, crisp, and tart-sweet"; "Originated as wild seedling in St. Lawrence Valley." | S8, S12 |
| Grower return | Ontario fresh $/lb: 0.23 (2022), 0.17 (2023), 0.21 (2024); bin $177 (2024). | S3, S4 |
| 2023–26 news (b/a) | (a) OAG 2024 report: "Returns to growers for the 2023 crop were well below expectations". (a, U.S. gov, 2 Dec 2025) FAS/Ottawa: McIntosh "a variety which is no longer popular with consumers for fresh consumption, and phase outs of these orchards should continue" — this is FAS/Ottawa's characterisation, attribute as such. (b) The Grower 28 Apr 2025: McIntosh among varieties marketed by Canadian producers during the 2025 "buy Canadian" movement. | S4, S31, S33 |
| **One-line reading** | **Canadian-grown and in season in October** — OAG lists McIntosh from September; PPQ calendar puts it in the Sept–mid-Oct harvest run; Canada imported zero McIntosh in the 2024 crop year (S1, S9, S3). |

### 2.7 Ambrosia

| Item | Finding | Source |
|---|---|---|
| Origin | CFIA PBR record: Breeder "Wilfrid Mennell, Cawston, British Columbia; Sally Mennell, Cawston, British Columbia"; Applicant/Holder Sally Mennell and Wilfrid Mennell, Barcello Road, Cawston, BC; Application 1994-03-21 (No. 94-332). Rights-holder site: "When a wild seedling took root on Wilfrid and Sally Mennell's British Columbia orchard, they let it grow to become the bountiful Ambrosia mother tree of all Ambrosia apples." PPQ: "L'Ambrosia est une variété provenant de la Colombie-Britannique". **Discovery year (c. 1980s/"early 1990s") and the Jonagold-row detail appear only in tier (c) — see UNVERIFIED.** | S20 https://active.inspection.gc.ca/english/plaveg/pbrpov/cropreport/app/app00000055e.shtml ; S28 ; S8 |
| Public/licensed | CFIA: 'Ambrosia' Canadian Plant Breeders' Rights Certificate 0388, **granted 1997-08-20, "Grant of Rights Termination Date: 2015-08-20"**; Agent in Canada: Summerland Varieties Corp. (SVC). → The variety itself is **no longer under PBR in Canada** (since 20 Aug 2015). The **name/mark remains a managed trademark**: SVC lists "Ambrosia" among its trademark apple varieties, with "™ denotes trademarks used under license"; the ambrosiaapples.com site asks buyers to "look for this mark" and lists licensed "marketing partners" (Canada: Consolidated Fruit Packers Ltd.; also EU, Chile, NZ, Australia partners). Good Fruit Grower (18 Jun 2015, paywalled; visible sentence): "By 2019, Ambrosia will be an open variety in both the United States and Canada." (b; refers to both countries' protections). | S20, S29, S28, S41 |
| Where grown / how much | BC: #1 variety — 2,274 insured acres / 41,649 tons (2026); 2,332 acres, 36.9% (2021); "Ambrosia apples are the top grown variety by producers in B.C." (Province). Ontario: 1,740 acres (11.0%, 4th); production 47,773,000 (2023) → 54,130,000 (2024) → 44,420,000 lbs (2025; #3 by yield). Quebec: "de plus en plus introduite dans les vergers du Québec" (PPQ); named by PPQ president among new plantings (5 Sep 2026). NS: listed by NSFGA. | S15, S16, S17, S3, S5, S8, S36, S12 |
| Acreage trend | Ontario 1,260 (2019) → 1,740 (2024). BC: overtook Gala after 2019 (2021 report) but the 2025 report notes South Okanagan "decline of Ambrosia and Gala in particular" while Central Okanagan Honeycrisp and Ambrosia "have grown steadily". | S3, S16, S15 |
| Harvest/availability | Ontario "October to June" (OAG). Quebec "Mi-octobre" (PPQ page); in the Sept–mid-Oct run (2026 calendar text). BC general Aug–Nov. NS: "October" (FreshPlaza, flag). | S1, S8, S9, S14 |
| Storage | OMAFRA CA: 1.2–1.7% O2, 1–1.5% CO2, 0.5–1 °C; "<1% O2 with fruit monitoring (SafePod, etc..); slow cooling with 1-MCP". U.S. context (Cornell 2024): "Starch values for long term storage should be between 2.5 and 4"; lower oxygen "leads to less internal browning". Held in Canadian storage Dec–Jun (InfoHort chart). | S6, S42, S31 |
| Imports | No separate Statistics Canada line ("Unspecified"). Licensed growers exist in Chile, New Zealand, Australia and the EU (S28), so counter-season imports are possible in principle; **no Canadian import figure for Ambrosia was found** (UNVERIFIED). | S3, S28 |
| Association/rights-holder description (verbatim) | Rights-holder (ambrosiaapples.com): "This wonder of nature bore irresistible fruit, so perfectly crisp, juicy and honey-kissed, it was named Ambrosia, food of the Gods." and "our standards of crisp, juicy, honeyed sweetness with every bite." PPQ (FR): "Sa forme conique et sa couleur rose sur fond de jaune-vert font qu'elle se démarque facilement des autres variétés en magasin. C'est une pomme très polyvalente… sa chair ne brunit pas rapidement à l'air ambiant et qu'elle garde sa forme à la cuisson." NSFGA: "sweet, crisp, juicy and is best described as refreshing", "almost entirely red". | S28, S8, S12 |
| Grower return | Ontario fresh $/lb 0.34 (2022), 0.25 (2023), 0.31 (2024); bin $262 (2024). | S3, S4 |
| 2023–26 news | (a, U.S. gov) FAS 2 Dec 2025: "B.C. also saw high temperatures and regional hail damage, with the Ambrosia crop particularly impacted"; "Reportedly, Ambrosia production in B.C. will be impacted and there are some quality concerns." (a) OAG 2024: Ambrosia in-store sampling, 137 stores, 56% sales lift during sampling. (b) BC Tree Fruits Cooperative closure 26 Jul 2024 (S37, S38) — BC's largest packer; Ambrosia is BC's #1 variety (S15). (b) Creston Valley Advance 11 Aug 2026: new national body "Apples Canada" (five provinces, ~900 growers) names Ambrosia among "The main varieties are Gala, Honeycrisp, Ambrosia, and McIntosh"; forecast 18.9 million bushels for 2026. | S31, S4, S37, S38, S39 |
| **One-line reading** | **Canadian-grown and in season in October (mid-October in Quebec); PBR-expired in Canada since 20 Aug 2015 but sold under a licensed trademark program** (S1, S8, S20, S29). |

### 2.8 Red Prince® (cultivar 'Red Jonaprince')

| Item | Finding | Source |
|---|---|---|
| Origin | CFIA PBR record: "'Red Jonaprince' arose as a mutation of the variety, 'Jonagold'. It was discovered in an orchard in Weert, the Netherlands in 1994." Breeder: "W.J.F.A. Princen, Wilton Weert B.V., Weert, Netherlands"; Holder: Wilton International B.V., Weert. Company FAQ: "The Red Prince® apple brand whose cultivar is Red Jonaprince arose as a mutation. The Princen brothers who are apple growers based in the town of Weert along the German-Dutch border discovered this apple in their orchard in 1994. The Botden Family was inspired by the deep red colour of the apple and decided to trademark it as the Red Prince® in Canada in 2012." Company FAQ also states it is "a natural cross between two apples: A Golden Delicious and a Red Jonathan" (the company's description of Jonagold parentage). | S21 https://active.inspection.gc.ca/english/plaveg/pbrpov/cropreport/app/app00001524e.shtml ; S27 https://redprinceapple.ca/faq/ |
| Public/licensed | **Licensed/club.** CFIA: Application 1997-08-22 (No. 97-1137); **Grant of Rights 2015-04-30; Certificate 5017; Termination Date 2040-04-30**; Agent in Canada Smart & Biggar LP. Tests and trials at AAFC Pacific Agri-Food Research Centre, Summerland, 2011–2013. Company: the Botdens "negotiated the rights to be the exclusive growers of Red Prince® apples in the country" before immigrating from the Netherlands. | S21, S27 https://redprinceapple.ca/story/ |
| Where grown / how much | Company: "over 150,000 cases of Red Prince® apples leave our headquarters in Thornbury, Ontario, along the shores of Georgian Bay. Due to consumer demand in other provinces, we are now growing Red Prince Apples® locally in Quebec and the Maritimes". "What's New": "2020 NEW RED PRINCE PLANTING — Red Prince continues to expand its plantings in Quebec and Maritimes beyond our Ontario home base." and "2020 NEW PACKING PLANT … 160,000 ft2". OAG census: Red Prince appears only inside "*Other Cultivars" — **no separate Ontario acreage or production figure is published**. Not in PPQ variety list; not in NSFGA list; not in BC tables. | S27, S3 |
| Harvest/availability | OAG: "Red Prince (February to June)". Company FAQ: "The Red Prince® apple is harvested in the Fall and cellared for several months"; "only available for a limited time. It is locally available from the Winter until the Summer, where as other local apples are typically sold immediately after they are harvested in the fall. The Red Prince® is cellared from the fall until the new year". | S1, S27 |
| Storage | Company: "cellared from the fall until the new year"; "stays crisp for weeks if stored in the fridge" (company claim). Not in OMAFRA CA table. CFIA descriptor: "mid-season harvest maturity, early to mid-season time of eating maturity". | S27, S6, S21 |
| Imports | None identified; the Canadian PBR and Red Prince® trademark are held/used by the Botden operation (company: "exclusive growers … in the country"). | S27, S21 |
| Company description (verbatim, attributed — non-nutrition parts only) | "It has a very unique flavour combination – it is both sweet and juicy (like the Golden Delicious) and crisp and tangy (like the Jonathan)." "It stores well, retaining its crisp texture, and is a premium Ontario apple that is available only from Winter through to Summer." On price: "It is up to retailers to decide pricing… the Red Prince® is a premium apple, so it is priced similar to other premium apples in any grocery store." **The same FAQ contains nutrition/antioxidant statements that must not be used.** CFIA (technical descriptor): fruit "large", "conic shape", overcolour "medium to dark red, flushed, striped and mottled", flesh "firm, cream". | S27, S21 |
| Grower return | Not published separately (inside "Other Varieties": Ontario fresh $0.28/lb 2024, S3). | S3 |
| 2023–26 news | None found at tier (b) for 2023–26. Company "What's New" archive ends October 2020 (packaging items). Older items (2015 design awards; 2018 OPMA award) predate the window. | S27 https://redprinceapple.ca/whats-new/ |
| **One-line reading** | **Licensed/club; long-storage variety released in winter — not an October apple** (OAG window "February to June"; CFIA PBR in force to 30 Apr 2040; company describes exclusive Canadian rights and fall harvest/winter release) (S1, S21, S27). |

## UNVERIFIED / DO-NOT-USE

Everything below could not be confirmed at tier (a)/(b)/(d) on 29 Sep 2026. Do not use as fact; pointers are given for follow-up.

1. **Retail shelf prices (any variety).** Loblaws (https://www.loblaws.ca/en/food/fruits-vegetables/fresh-fruits/apples/c/29722) returned HTTP 403 "Access Denied"; Metro (https://www.metro.ca/en/online-grocery/aisles/fruits-vegetables/fruits/apples) returned a human-verification page; Sobeys/Voilà not attempted after those failures. Search-result snippets from flyer-aggregator sites (caflyers.ca, flyers-on-line.com, flyerca.ca — tier c) suggested Sobeys Ontario Honeycrisp "product of Ontario" $1.99/lb (24–30 Sep 2026) and Metro McIntosh/Paula Red/Ginger Gold $0.99/lb (10–16 Sep 2026) — **flag only; not opened on a retailer's own site**. Pointer: open retailer flyers directly in a browser, record date, banner, region, $/lb and $/kg, and origin as displayed.
2. **Month-by-month import data (e.g., October share of McIntosh or Gala on Ontario shelves).** Statistics Canada CIMT monthly data (HS 0808.10) were not retrieved; the StatCan table landing page opened (S19) is annual production, not trade. Only crop-year/marketing-year totals were obtained (S3, S4, S31). Pointer: Statistics Canada, Canadian International Merchandise Trade Web Application, HS 080810 by month and province of clearance; AAFC InfoHort storage-holdings reports (referenced in S3) for month-end holdings by variety.
3. **OMAFRA/Ontario published storage-life table (months in regular cold storage vs CA by variety).** https://www.omafra.gov.on.ca/english/crops/facts/apstorage.htm → 404 (content migrated); https://www.ontario.ca/page/apple-storage → 404; https://www.ontario.ca/page/commercial-production-ambrosiatm-apples-ontario and …honeycrisptm-apples-ontario → 403/"We can't find that page" (ontario.ca requires JavaScript). The OMAFRA CA-regime table (S6) has conditions but no durations. Pointer: search ontario.ca for "Commercial production of Ambrosia apples in Ontario" and "Honeycrisp" factsheets; OMAFRA Publication 360 (Fruit Crop Protection Guide) will not have storage life; the DeEll 2020 book chapter cited in S6 does.
4. **Spartan breeder and parentage detail** (R.C. Palmer; 1936 introduction from the Dominion Experimental Station, Summerland; second parent disputed). Found only in Wikipedia and nursery pages (tier c). AAFC's Summerland page (S30) confirms apple breeding began there in 1924 but does not name Spartan; the AAFC Summerland RDC page (https://agriculture.canada.ca/en/science/research-centres-and-collections/british-columbia/summerland-research-and-development-centre) returned 404; www.spartanapple.com did not resolve. Tier-(a) support that exists: PPQ (1936, McIntosh × Yellow Newtown, Pacific Agri-Food Research Centre) and Parks Canada (Spartan is a McIntosh hybrid). Use only those.
5. **Gala breeder (J.H. Kidd, Greytown, 1934; selected by Don McKenzie, DSIR) and Royal Gala sport (Bill ten Hove, Matamata, 1969/1970).** Te Ara — the Encyclopedia of New Zealand (https://teara.govt.nz/en/apples-and-pears/page-3) returned 403 ("Just a moment…"). Pointer: reopen Te Ara in a browser; also Plant & Food Research NZ. Until then use only PPQ (1965 introduction; New Zealand) and USApple (parentage; Royal Gala naming story — U.S. context).
6. **Ambrosia discovery year and "chance seedling in a row of Jonagold trees".** Only Wikipedia/aggregators (tier c). The CFIA record gives the PBR application date (21 Mar 1994) and breeders; ambrosiaapples.com gives "wild seedling" on the Mennell orchard. https://ambrosiaapples.ca/about/ could not be opened (connection reset, 503). The Good Fruit Grower 2015 article is paywalled beyond one sentence. Pointer: Summerland Varieties Corp (info via https://www.summerlandvarieties.com/) and ambrosiaapples.com "Our Story" page.
7. **Ambrosia royalty amounts (CA$2–3.75 per tree) and the claim that Canadian rights "expired in 2021".** Search snippets attributed to Good Fruit Grower conflict with the CFIA record (termination 20 Aug 2015). The CFIA record is authoritative for the PBR; the 2021 reference may concern the U.S. or another jurisdiction. Do not use the 2021 date.
8. **Honeycrisp acreage in Nova Scotia and Quebec; NS variety-level acreage generally.** No NSFGA or NS Department of Agriculture variety table for 2023–26 was found; https://www.nsfruitgrowers.ca/economic-impact → 404; the only NS government document opened is from 2009 (S13). CBC 2017 "Complete turnaround: Honeycrisps reviving Nova Scotia apple farms" (https://www.cbc.ca/news/canada/nova-scotia/honeycrisp-apples-nova-scotia-s-cash-crop-1.4346780) returned 403. Pointer: NSFGA annual report; Scotian Gold Cooperative (https://scotiangold.com/apples/ returned HTTP 202 shell, JS-rendered).
9. **Nova Scotia 2026 crop forecast (2.2 million bushels, +30% from 1.7 million) and NS harvest dates (Honeycrisp ~25 Sep; Gala/Ambrosia October).** Source is FreshPlaza (14 Sep 2026) with no byline → tier (c) flag. CBC "'Things are sizing up': N.S. apple crop bouncing back…" (https://www.cbc.ca/news/canada/nova-scotia/ns-apples-recovering-after-drought-9.7318147) returned 403. Pointer: reopen CBC; NSFGA news page.
10. **Quebec per-variety orchard shares from the PPQ-commissioned orchard portrait** (McIntosh 24.8%, Cortland 24.7%, Empire 23.2%, Spartan 23.3% of traditional varieties; Honeycrisp 46%, Gala 30.3%, Ambrosia 23.7% of newer trees; ~90 varieties). La Terre de chez nous article (https://www.laterre.ca/productions/pommes/environ-90-varietes-de-pommes-differentes-recensees-au-quebec/) returned 429 then 403 (Cloudflare). Pointer: La Terre (byline/date needed) and PPQ (https://producteursdepommesduquebec.ca/ — annual-report PDF link from search returned 404).
11. **Statistique Québec apple production by region/variety 1999–2024.** Landing page opened (https://statistique.quebec.ca/en/document/production-and-marketing-of-apples-by-apple-growing-region-quebec) but the tables were not retrieved; unknown whether variety-level data exist.
12. **BC government news releases on the co-op closure** (https://news.gov.bc.ca/releases/2024AF0035-001295 and …2024AF0037-001496) — 503 via fetch; TLS failure via curl. CBC BC stories (https://www.cbc.ca/news/canada/british-columbia/bc-tree-fruits-cooperative-closure-rotten-apples-1.7290574) → 403; The Narwhal → bot check. Figures used for the closure come from Country Life in BC and Global News (S37, S38) only.
13. **Cornell primary page for Cortland dates (1898 cross / 1915 naming).** Confirmed only via NSFGA (S12: "Cornell University, 1898") and PPQ (S8: 1915). New York Apple Association pages (https://www.applesfromny.com/varieties/cortland/ and /empire/) returned HTTP 202 JS shells. Cornell Empire dates (1945 seedling; 1966 introduction; Roger Way) are confirmed by Cornell's own publications (S25, S26).
14. **Ontario 2026 crop estimate.** Only a single grower's estimate ("maybe 30 per cent less than last year", Weather Network, S40) and the national 18.9-million-bushel forecast via Creston Valley Advance (S39) were found; no OAG 2026 July/November estimate document was opened. Pointer: OAG news releases at https://onapples.com/ (site shows bot-check to fetchers; curl with a browser UA worked).
15. **Red Prince production volume beyond the company's "over 150,000 cases"; Ontario acreage.** OAG publishes Red Prince only within "Other Cultivars". No third-party figure found. https://redprinceapple.ca/our-apples/ → 404.
16. **Import origin of "Unspecified" apples (120M lbs in 2024 crop year).** OMAFA speculates ("could be due to the rise of club cultivars"); no breakdown found.
17. **Any consumer survey (tier d)** on Canadian apple preferences — none found; OAG mentions a Numerator study (S3 p.11) but results/method were not published in the report text extracted.
18. **AAFC statistical overview statement "predominantly Washington state".** The fetched summary of S18 attributed U.S. imports as "predominantly Washington state"; the exact wording on the AAFC page was not captured verbatim. Use S31 (U.S. share by MY) and The Grower (S33, S34) for the Washington attribution instead.
19. **Tier-(c) pages encountered and NOT used as sources** (flag only): Wikipedia (McIntosh, Empire, Cortland, Spartan, Gala, Honeycrisp, Ambrosia), orangepippin.com, specialtyproduce.com, pomiferous.com, honeycrisp.com, grokipedia, FreshPlaza (no bylines), flyer-aggregator sites, ontarioplaques.com, blogTO (2010), Ottawa Life (undated), nursery catalogue pages.

# Apple variety dossiers (set B) — Fuji, Granny Smith, Red Delicious, Golden Delicious, Pink Lady (Cripps Pink), Cosmic Crisp, Envy, Jazz, SweeTango — as sold in Canada

Research date: every URL below was opened on **29 Sep 2026** unless a line says otherwise. Every fact carries the URL actually opened. Where a page could not be opened, the item sits in the final **UNVERIFIED / DO-NOT-USE** section with a pointer.

House-rule compliance notes for the writer
- No health, nutrition, antioxidant, sugar, glycemic, pesticide-residue, vitamin or food-safety content is included anywhere below. Several source pages (WSAC variety pages say "nutritious"; the NWHC export manual has a chemical-residue section; onapples.com has a "Health benefits" tab) contain such content; it was deliberately not extracted and must not be used. Two verbatim quotes from Washington State Apple Commission pages contain the word "sugar"/"acid" as a flavour descriptor only ("high sugar, high acid with an effervescent finish"); those are the association's own tasting words, attributed, and are not nutrition claims — use them only as attributed quotes or drop them.
- Taste/texture words appear **only** inside attributed verbatim quotes from a breeder, grower association, rights-holder/marketer, or a government plant-variety record, and are labelled as that party's description.
- Tier labels: **(a)** primary (government statistics, regulator/plant-variety records, court records, the association's/company's/breeder's/licensor's own page, release or report); **(b)** named outlet with byline and date; **(c)** aggregator/Wikipedia/SEO/review/flyer-aggregator/no-byline trade page = flag only, never a source; **(d)** named consumer survey with publisher, year and methodology (none used).
- U.S. material is labelled **U.S. context** (Washington State Apple Commission, WSU, Northwest Horticultural Council, USDA FAS) and is never presented as Canadian. The USDA FAS/Ottawa report is a U.S. government report *about Canada* and is labelled as such. New Zealand, Australian and Chilean material is labelled by country.
- No legal/regulatory item below alleges wrongdoing. Plant Breeders' Rights (CFIA) and U.S. plant patents are administrative registrations, not proceedings. The WSU "exclusivity" items are the licensor's own commercial decisions, reported by the licensor and by a bylined outlet.
- "Season in Canadian stores" for imported fruit is given as: (i) what the Canadian association/retailer pages actually show, plus (ii) the exporter's own stated harvest/shipping window, labelled by country. Where only the brief's general framing (Southern Hemisphere harvest Feb–May → Canada spring/summer; Washington harvest Sep–Nov → stored to the following summer) applies, that is said explicitly and is not sourced as fact.

## 1. Cross-variety reference tables

### 1.1 Is it grown commercially in Canada? (association / government variety lists)

| Variety | Ontario (S1 OAG list; S2 OAG acreage 2024) | British Columbia (S6 BCTFPG list; S7 BC Ministry 2026 acreage / production) | Quebec (S10 gov; S11–S13 PPQ) | Nova Scotia (S14 NSFGA) | Reading |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Fuji | Listed: "Fuji (mid-late October to April)"; 383 acres (2.4% of 15,889); 2024 production 11,235,000 lbs | Listed ("Mid to late October"); 55 acres; 736 tons | Not on PPQ variety list or calendar | Not listed | **Grown in Canada (ON, BC), small volume** |
| Granny Smith | Not on OAG variety list; in "*Other Cultivars" footnote (no separate acreage) | Listed ("Mid to late October"); 112 acres; 1,741 tons | Not listed | Not listed | **Grown in BC in small volume; Ontario only within "Other"** |
| Red Delicious | Listed: "Red Delicious (October to July)"; 885 acres (5.6%, rank 5); 2024 production 20,285,000 lbs | Not on BCTFPG list; "Red Del" 61 acres; 717 tons (S7) | PPQ "Délicieuse rouge": "Disponibilité : Fin octobre"; "Produite en petite quantité au Québec" | Not listed on NSFGA page | **Grown in Canada (ON mainly; QC, BC small)** |
| Golden Delicious | Listed: "Golden Delicious (October to May)"; 431 acres (2.7%); 2024 production 9,340,000 lbs | Listed ("Mid to late September"; "Long storage in CA"); "Gold Del" 25 acres; 404 tons | PPQ "Délicieuse jaune": "Disponibilité : Mi-octobre"; "produite en petite quantité au Québec" | Not listed | **Grown in Canada (ON mainly; QC, BC small)** |
| Pink Lady (Cripps Pink) | Not on OAG list; "Cripps Pink" in "*Other Cultivars" footnote; OMAFRA 2012: "Cripp's Pink require higher heat accumulations" | Listed ("Cripps Pink (Pink Lady®)", "Late October to early November"); "Pink Lady" 229 acres (5th-largest BC apple by acreage); 5,164 tons | Not listed | Not listed | **Grown in BC (Similkameen/South Okanagan); Ontario only within "Other"** |
| Cosmic Crisp (WA 38) | Not listed | Not listed | Not listed | Not listed | **Not grown in Canada** — licensor restricts to Washington (S27, S24, B1) |
| Envy (Scilate) | Not listed | Not listed | Not listed | Not listed | **No Canadian growing found** (T&G names "13 locations" but not the countries — see UNVERIFIED) |
| Jazz (Scifresh) | Not listed | Not listed (CFIA record notes Okanagan trial maturity data, S15) | Not listed | Not listed | **No Canadian commercial growing found** |
| SweeTango (Minneiska) | Not listed (Zestar! and RAVE appear in "Other Cultivars"; SweeTango does not) | Not listed | Gov page: "la Gala, la Honeycrisp, la SweeTango et l'Ambrosia" are varieties consumers seek, "généralement importées" but that "les producteurs d'ici ajoutent maintenant à leurs vergers" | **Listed** by NSFGA as a Nova Scotia apple; Scotian Gold (Annapolis Valley) is a licensed SweeTango grower (S36) | **Grown in Canada (Nova Scotia confirmed; Quebec per gov statement)** |

S7 verbatim: apple acreage and production "are increasingly concentrated in Ambrosia, Gala and Honeycrisp, with several other recognizable varieties forming a second tier. The majority of the remainder (see 'all others') comes from 7 varieties: Cripps Pink, Gingergold, Cider, Jonagold, Nicola, Orin and Aurora." (Note the report lists "Pink Lady" as a named line and "Cripps Pink" in "all others" — the two names are used for the same cultivar/brand elsewhere; the report itself does not reconcile them.)
S8 (BC gov, 22 Sep 2026): "approximately 400 commercial growers who cultivate about 15,000 acres"; "Ambrosia apples are the top grown variety by producers in B.C."
S9 (BCFGA): Apples — harvest "Mid August to Late November".

### 1.4 Imports — who supplies Canada, and how much of each variety (Canada-level)

**By source country** (S20, USDA FAS/Ottawa — U.S. government report about Canada; Table 2, marketing year July–June, metric tons, source "Trade Data Monitor"):

| MY | World | United States | U.S. share | Chile | New Zealand | China | EU |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 2022/23 | 184,906 | 149,979 | 81.1% | 12,715 | 9,501 | 1,901 | 3,722 |
| 2023/24 | 163,871 | 140,182 | 85.5% | 6,993 | 8,696 | 2,397 | 677 |
| 2024/25 | 168,732 | 137,023 | 81.2% | 8,232 | 8,686 | 2,704 | 1,420 |

S16 (AAFC, 2024 edition): "In any given year, over 80% of Canada's fresh apple imports come from the U.S., with close to 80% coming from Washington state."; 2024 fresh apple imports $269,485 thousand / 164,852 t; exports $68,787 thousand / 57,174 t; "Over the last 5 crop years (2018/19–2022/23), Canada had a trade deficit of 152,261 metric tons in fresh apples".
S20 also: "Retailers are still likely to dabble at diversifying their supply chain and this will see support for imports from alternative source markets such as New Zealand and Chile."; "Australia is also the beneficiary of a September 2025 market access announcement".

**By variety** (S2 OAG p. 42, Statistics Canada data by port-of-entry province, lbs; "The province denotes the port of entry and may not necessarily reflect the final provincial destination"; and S3 OMAFA Table 4):

| Variety | Canada imports, 2024 crop yr (lbs) | 2023 crop yr | 5-yr avg 2020–24 | 2024 vs 2023 | Port of entry 2024: Ontario / BC / Quebec |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Granny Smith | 42,448,399 | 44,804,979 | 44,297,640 | −5% | 27,350,586 / 12,973,660 / 1,738,352 |
| Red Delicious | 20,313,913 | 27,106,206 | 22,733,706 | −25% | 10,288,505 / 9,362,501 / 144,107 |
| Golden Delicious | 7,361,850 | 8,342,619 | 8,437,921 | −12% | 4,920,211 / 2,290,900 / 47,774 |
| Gala (comparator) | 83,851,657 | 79,246,223 | 89,467,041 | +6% | 40,120,947 / 38,096,973 / 834,898 |
| Honeycrisp (comparator) | 7,886,711 | 11,967,041 | 8,405,756 | −34% | |
| Unspecified | 120,039,138 | 106,123,925 | 105,342,554 | +13% | 38,685,497 / 77,075,832 / 1,050,726 |
| Total | 282,043,159 | 277,695,650 | 279,747,567 | +2% | 125,749,710 / 142,700,910 / 4,193,178 |

S3 (OMAFA) Table 4, 10-year view: Granny Smith 29,956,639 lbs (2014) → 42,448,399 (2024); "Unspecified cultivars have seen an increase of 308% - this could be due to the rise of club cultivars". **Fuji, Pink Lady, Cosmic Crisp, Envy, Jazz and SweeTango have no separate import line** in the Statistics Canada classification used by OAG/OMAFA; any imports of those sit in "Unspecified" (120.0M lbs in 2024 — the largest single line).

Scale checks (arithmetic on S2 only): Ontario's 2024 Red Delicious crop 20,285,000 lbs ≈ Canada's Red Delicious imports 20,313,913 lbs; Ontario Golden Delicious 9,340,000 lbs vs 7,361,850 lbs imported; Granny Smith imports (42.4M lbs) have no comparable Canadian crop line (BC's 1,741 tons ≈ 3.8M lbs, S7).

**Duty status:** S50 (NWHC, U.S. industry manual, section "IV. TARIFFS"): "Apples, pears, stone fruits, and sweet cherries are allowed into Canada duty-free." S2 (OAG 2025 report): "While CUSMA-compliant goods (including apples) remained tariff-free, we continue to work with national partners to advocate for Ontario growers at the federal level." S18 (Finance Canada, list effective 8 Sep 2026): HS chapters present are 04, 14, 19, 33, 35, 39, 44, 48, 57, 61, 62, 68, 70, 72 — **no chapter 08 (edible fruit) and no 0808 line**. S19 timeline: 4 Mar 2025 25% on $30B of U.S. goods (list names "orange juice, peanut butter, wine, spirits, beer, coffee, appliances" — apples not named); 13 Mar 2025 steel/aluminum package; 1 Sep 2025 removal of the March packages ("Canada's tariffs on steel, aluminum, and automobiles remain in place").

### 1.5 Season / availability windows

**Canadian-grown (association pages):**

| Variety | Ontario (S1, OAG) | BC harvest (S6) | Quebec (S12/S13 PPQ) |
|---|---|---|---|
| Fuji | "mid-late October to April" | "Mid to late October" | — |
| Granny Smith | — (not listed) | "Mid to late October" | — |
| Red Delicious | "October to July" | — (not on list) | "Fin octobre" |
| Golden Delicious | "October to May" | "Mid to late September" | "Mi-octobre" |
| Cripps Pink / Pink Lady | — | "Late October to early November" | — |
| SweeTango | — | — | — (NSFGA lists it without dates; UMN: "typically ripens in early September", U.S.) |

**Imported fruit — exporter's own windows (labelled by country):**
- **Washington (U.S. context, S28 WSAC availability calendars):** Fuji, Granny Smith, Red Delicious, Golden Delicious, Cosmic Crisp — calendar shows all 12 months; Cripps Pink/Pink Lady "November through July"; Jazz "September through July"; Envy "October through March"; SweeTango "August through November". These are the U.S. association's marketing windows for Washington fruit, not Canadian shelf data.
- **Chile (S48, exporters' association):** Chilean apples "available from late January through November"; top-5 export varieties by volume "Royal Gala, Richared Delicious, Granny Smith, Gala and Tenroy Royal Gala"; export destinations by region: Europe 39.0%, Latin America 26.9%, Far East 15.6%, **North America 11.0%**, Middle East 7.5% (Canada not broken out). B9 (Frutas de Chile column, 6 May 2026) 2026 export forecast by variety: Gala 246,510 t (+5%); Fuji 123,324 t (−4%); Pink Lady 89,011 t and Cripps Pink 27,249 t (family −6%); "Green apples (non-club)" 77,209 t (−3%); total 580,867 t (+2%).
- **New Zealand (S41, T&G, 2 Apr 2025):** new-season NZ Envy launch at start of April 2025; 2025 T&G forecast "4.4 million tray carton equivalents (TCEs)", "79,200 tonnes" across all varieties; "the largest crop since 2022". S42 (T&G, 29 Mar 2022): NZ Jazz "First exported from New Zealand in 2004"; exports to "Europe, the United Kingdom, North America and Asia". S49 (NZAPI): NZ apples and pears "enjoyed in more than 80 countries".
- **Brief's general framing (not a sourced fact):** Southern Hemisphere fruit (harvest Feb–May) reaches Canadian shelves spring–summer; Washington fruit (harvest Sep–Nov) is stored and sold through the following summer. The source-backed version is: Chile ships "late January through November" (S48); NZ Envy shipping begins in April (S41); Washington's own calendars run year-round for the storage varieties (S28); Canadian storage holdings for Red Delicious run at least Dec–Jun (S20, Fig. 2).

### 1.6 Storage — published regimes and durations, labelled by source country

**Canada — Ontario (S4, OMAFRA/ONcore, "Common controlled atmosphere storage regimes for apples", adapted from DeEll 2020; conditions only, no durations):**

| Cultivar (as printed) | O2 (%) | CO2 (%) | Temp (°C) | Comments (verbatim) |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Delicious (Red) | 1-2 | 1-2 | 0-0.5 | "<1% O2 fruit monitoring (SafePod, etc..)" |
| Fuji | 1.5-2 | 0.5-1 | 0-1 | "Sensitive to CO2; delay CA with 1-MCP" |
| Golden Delicious | 1-2 | 1-2 | 0-1 | "<1% O2 with fruit monitoring (SafePod, etc..); slow stepwise cooling with 1-MCP" |
| Granny Smith | 1.5-2 | 0.5-2 | 0-1 | "<1% O2 with fruit monitoring (SafePod, etc..)" |
| (Cripps Pink, WA 38, Scilate, Scifresh, Minneiska) | not in table | | | |

**Canada — British Columbia (S6, BC Tree Fruit Production Guide, "Storage" line per variety, verbatim):** Fuji — "Good with very good CA potential and long shelf life"; Granny Smith — "Good with very good CA potential and long shelf life"; Cripps Pink (Pink Lady®) — "Good with promising CA potential and long shelf life"; Golden Delicious — "Long storage in CA". (No month figures.)

**Canada — marketed-from-storage windows as a proxy (S1, OAG):** Red Delicious to July; Golden Delicious to May; Fuji to April. **Canada — storage holdings (S20, U.S. gov report citing AAFC InfoHort, Fig. 2):** Red Delicious is one of seven varieties whose Canadian storage holdings are plotted Dec–Jun for MY 2024/25 (values not machine-readable).

**United States (U.S. context; S21 WSU variety pages, "Storage" field; S22; S23; S25; S26):**

| Variety | WSU "Storage" | Other U.S. figures |
|---|---|---|
| Fuji | "12 Months" | S22: commercial CA "1%-2% oxygen and less than 1.0% carbon dioxide at 33°F" |
| Granny Smith | "12 Months" | S22 notes ILOS/DPA scald management (no duration) |
| Red Delicious | "12 Months" | — |
| Golden Delicious | "11 Months" | — |
| Cripps Pink | "10 Months" | — |
| WA 38 / Cosmic Crisp | (no WSU variety page opened) | S26 patent: "'WA 38' loses little of its crispness, sugar, and acid following five months of storage, whereas that of its parents declines considerably." S25 (WSU Foundation): "In controlled-atmosphere storage, its crunch lasts into the next harvest" and "can stay fresh for a year in storage." S23: study stored at "33°F", "1% carbon dioxide, and 2% oxygen", fruit held to March/April 2018 (≈5–6 months). S27 (PVM): "flavor and texture is maintained even through storage" (no duration). |

**Breeder / plant-patent statements (labelled):**
- Envy/'Scilate' (S32, U.S. patent, NZ breeder): "Stores well in air cool storage (0±0.5° C.) for at least 3 months. … Fruit remains in a good condition for at least 14 days at 20° C."
- SweeTango/'Minneiska' (S33, U.S. patent, U.S. breeder): "an unusually long storage life for an early ripening variety" — "storage life of 90 to 120 days in common storage (34 to 37° F.)".
- Jazz/'Scifresh': no storage statement obtained (patent page unavailable — see UNVERIFIED).

**Duration caveat for the writer:** the only Canadian tier-(a) storage material is the Ontario CA regime table (conditions) and the BC guide's qualitative lines; every month-figure above is U.S. (WSU) or breeder-patent context and must be labelled as such.

## 2. Dossiers

### 2.1 Fuji

| Item | Finding (with source) |
|---|---|
| Breeder / origin | **Japan.** WSU (S21, U.S. university): origin "Japan"; parentage "Red Delicious x Ralls Janet"; harvest "Late". WSAC (S28, U.S. association): "Named for Fujisaki, Japan", "a cross between Red Delicious and Ralls Janet". **Year of cross (1939) and registration (1962), and the breeding station (Tohoku Research Station, Ministry of Agriculture and Forestry) were found only at tier (c)** — see UNVERIFIED item 1. No CFIA PBR (S15). |
| Grown in Canada? | **Yes, small.** Ontario: OAG list "Fuji (mid-late October to April)" (S1); 383 acres, 2.4% of Ontario acreage, up 16% on 2019 (S2); 2024 production 11,235,000 lbs, +27.2% vs 5-yr avg (S2); grower price $0.34/lb fresh (S2). OMAFRA 2012: October harvest; "Fuji … require higher heat accumulations" (S5). BC: on BCTFPG list, "Mid to late October" (S6); 55 acres, 736 tons (S7). Quebec, NS: not listed (S10–S14). |
| Import sources | No separate Statistics Canada import line — Fuji imports sit in "Unspecified" (120.0M lbs, 2024 crop yr; S2/S3). Country picture: U.S. 81–86% of all Canadian apple imports, Chile and NZ ~5% each (S20, U.S. gov). WSAC (U.S.) markets Fuji year-round (S28). Chile forecasts 123,324 t of Fuji exports in 2026 (B9), with North America 11.0% of Chilean apple exports (S48). |
| Season in Canadian stores | Canadian-grown: Ontario "mid-late October to April" (S1); BC harvest mid–late Oct (S6). Imported: Washington calendar all 12 months (S28, U.S.); Chile "late January through November" (S48). |
| Storage life | Ontario CA regime: O2 1.5–2%, CO2 0.5–1%, 0–1 °C, "Sensitive to CO2; delay CA with 1-MCP" (S4, no duration). BC guide: "Good with very good CA potential and long shelf life" (S6). **U.S. context:** WSU "12 Months" (S21); commercial CA "1%-2% oxygen and less than 1.0% carbon dioxide at 33°F" (S22). |
| Licensing | Public variety; no CFIA PBR record (S15). |
| Own descriptions (attributed, verbatim) | WSAC (U.S. association): "Ultra sweet. Flavor forward. Refreshingly juicy."; "one of the world's sweetest apples" (S28). WSU variety page flavour field: "sweet, juicy, medium firm" (S21). BC guide has no tasting text; OAG list has none. |
| 2023–2026 news (Canada) | No Fuji-specific Canadian item found. General: B4 (28 Apr 2025) retailers carrying "just one or two U.S. varieties"; B5 (21 Mar 2026) Washington shipments to Canada Aug–Dec 2025 −11.1%. |
| Retail price | Voilà 29 Sep 2026: "Apple Fuji 1 Count" $1.79; organic 1.36 kg $8.99; Lil Snappers organic $7.35/kg (S51; region/origin not shown). |
| **One-line reading** | **Grown in Canada but small (2.4% of Ontario acreage, 55 BC acres) and harvested late (mid–late October), so October shelf Fuji is as likely Washington/Chile storage fruit as Ontario/BC new crop — read the origin label; public variety (S1, S2, S6, S7, S20, S28).** |

### 2.2 Granny Smith

| Item | Finding (with source) |
|---|---|
| Breeder / origin | **Australia, 1868 chance seedling, Maria Ann Smith, Eastwood (Sydney).** City of Ryde Council (S30, government): earliest documented account in the *Farmer and Settler*, 25 June 1924 (Herbert Rumsey interviewing Edwin Small), who recalled that "in 1868 he and his father had been invited by Maria to examine a seedling apple growing by a creek on her farm"; "The seedling had developed from the remains of some French crab apples grown in Tasmania"; exhibited as 'Smith's seedling' at the 1890 Castle Hill show, prize for cooking apples 1891; by 1895 the NSW Department of Agriculture's Albert H. Benson began "first large scale cultivation of the apple at the Government Experimental Station in Bathurst". WSU (U.S.): origin "Australia"; parentage "French Crab x unknown (possibly Rome Beauty)" (S21). |
| Grown in Canada? | **Marginal.** BC: on BCTFPG list, "Mid to late October" (S6); 112 acres, 1,741 tons (S7). Ontario: not on OAG variety list; appears only in the "*Other Cultivars" footnote with no separate acreage (S1, S2). Quebec, NS: not listed. |
| Import sources | **The largest named imported variety after Gala.** Canada imported 42,448,399 lbs of Granny Smith in the 2024 crop year (5-yr avg 44,297,640; 2014: 29,956,639) — ports of entry Ontario 27.35M lbs, BC 12.97M lbs, Quebec 1.74M lbs (S2, S3). Country split not published by variety; overall U.S. share 81–86% (S20). Chile lists Granny Smith among its top-5 export varieties (S48); Chile's 2026 "Green apples (non-club)" forecast 77,209 t (B9). |
| Season in Canadian stores | Imported year-round per Washington calendar (S28, U.S.); Chile "late January through November" (S48). BC new crop mid–late October (S6). |
| Storage life | Ontario CA regime: O2 1.5–2%, CO2 0.5–2%, 0–1 °C (S4). BC guide: "Good with very good CA potential and long shelf life" (S6). **U.S. context:** WSU "12 Months" (S21). |
| Licensing | Public; no CFIA PBR (S15). |
| Own descriptions (attributed, verbatim) | WSAC (U.S. association): "The iconic green apple"; "Pucker up, tart apple lovers. Beyond the iconic light-green skin of this apple awaits a lemon-like acidity with just enough sweetness." (S28). WSU flavour field: "tart" (S21). |
| 2023–2026 news (Canada) | B4 (The Grower, 28 Apr 2025) names Granny Smith among varieties discussed in the context of retailers stocking "just one or two U.S. varieties" after March 2025. Import line −5% in 2024 vs 2023 (S2). |
| Retail price | Voilà 29 Sep 2026: "Apple Granny Smith Large 1 Count" $1.39; 3-count "$8.80 per kilogram"; organic 1.36 kg $9.99 (S51; region/origin not shown). |
| **One-line reading** | **Essentially an imported apple in Canada (42.4M lbs imported in 2024 vs ~112 BC acres and no Ontario acreage line): in October it is Washington or Southern-Hemisphere storage fruit, not a Canadian harvest; public variety (S2, S3, S6, S7, S20, S28, S48).** |

### 2.3 Red Delicious

| Item | Finding (with source) |
|---|---|
| Breeder / origin | **Peru, Iowa, chance seedling on Jesse Hiatt's farm; commercialised by Stark Bro's 1893–94.** Stark Bro's own history page (S29): Hiatt "originally called it 'Hawkeye'"; entered Stark Bro's first International New Fruit Show in 1893, "entered again in 1894, and this time the apple won first prize"; Clarence Stark: "My! This apple is delicious!"; Stark "purchased exclusive rights from Hiatt and registered 'Stark® Delicious' as a trademark"; renamed "Red Delicious" after Golden Delicious was introduced in 1914. WSU (U.S.): origin "Iowa"; parentage "sport of Delicious" (S21). WSAC: "discovered as a chance seedling on the farm of Jesse Hiatt" (S28). (Year the seedling was first found — 1870s — not confirmed at tier a/b; UNVERIFIED item 2.) |
| Grown in Canada? | **Yes — Ontario's 5th-largest variety, declining.** OAG list "Red Delicious (October to July)" (S1); 885 acres, 5.6% of Ontario acreage, down 22% from 1,213 acres in 2019 (S2; OMAFA S3 "Red Delicious 5 5.6%"); 2024 production 20,285,000 lbs, −17.4% vs 5-yr avg; grower price $0.26/lb fresh (S2). OMAFRA 2012: October harvest; "Red and Golden Delicious, Fuji, and Cripp's Pink require higher heat accumulations" (S5). Quebec: PPQ "Délicieuse rouge — Disponibilité : Fin octobre"; "Produite en petite quantité au Québec" (S12); on the 2026 PPQ availability calendar (S11). BC: 61 acres, 717 tons (S7); not on BCTFPG list (S6). NS: not on NSFGA page (S14). USDA FAS (U.S. gov about Canada): "acreage of McIntosh and Red Delicious varieties continues to decline" in Ontario (S20). |
| Import sources | Separate import line: 20,313,913 lbs in 2024 crop yr (−25% vs 2023's 27,106,206; 5-yr avg 22,733,706); ports of entry Ontario 10.29M, BC 9.36M lbs (S2). Country split not published by variety; U.S. 81–86% overall (S20). Chile lists "Richared Delicious" in its top-5 exports (S48). NWHC (U.S. industry) uses "Red Delicious" as its example of the variety name required on export cartons to Canada (S50). |
| Season in Canadian stores | Ontario "October to July" (S1); Quebec "Fin octobre" (S12). Canadian storage holdings of Red Delicious tracked Dec–Jun (S20, Fig. 2, InfoHort). Washington calendar all year (S28, U.S.). |
| Storage life | Ontario CA regime "Delicious (Red)": O2 1–2%, CO2 1–2%, 0–0.5 °C, "<1% O2 fruit monitoring (SafePod, etc..)" (S4). OAG marketed-to-July window (S1). **U.S. context:** WSU "12 Months" (S21). |
| Licensing | Public (historic "Stark® Delicious" trademark per S29; no CFIA PBR, S15). |
| Own descriptions (attributed, verbatim) | PPQ (Quebec growers): "la Délicieuse rouge à une chaire blanche crème et juteuse. Elle a une forme allongée et elle est excellente à croquer et à cuisiner." (S12). WSAC (U.S.): "The quintessential red apple"; "A well-known favorite that's popular for its tried-and-true flavor: mildly sweet and crunchy." (S28). Voilà (retailer): "large, firm, sweet and juicy … dark red colour with an elongated shape" (S51). |
| 2023–2026 news (Canada) | S20 (2 Dec 2025, U.S. gov about Canada): Ontario Red Delicious acreage "continues to decline". S2 (OAG 2025): acreage −5% year on year, −22% over five years. Import line −25% in 2024 (S2). |
| Retail price | Voilà 29 Sep 2026: "Apple Red Delicious Large 1 Count" $1.39; "Delicious Apples Red 1.36 kg" $7.99 ($0.59/100 g); 3-count "$7.69 per kilogram" (S51). |
| **One-line reading** | **Canadian-grown and in season from October (Ontario "October to July"; Quebec "Fin octobre"), but a shrinking variety (Ontario acreage −22% in five years) and roughly half of what Canadians buy is imported (20.3M lbs imported vs 20.3M lbs Ontario crop) — check the label; public variety (S1, S2, S12, S20).** |

### 2.4 Golden Delicious

| Item | Finding (with source) |
|---|---|
| Breeder / origin | **Clay County, West Virginia, chance seedling; introduced by Stark Bro's in 1914.** Stark Bro's (S29): Stark Bro's introduced the "Golden Delicious" variety in 1914 (the page frames the renaming of the original Delicious around this). WSU (U.S.): origin "WV", "chance seedling possible of Grimes Golden x Golden Reinette"; harvest "Early" (S21). WSAC: "originates from Clay County, West Virginia. Its exact parentage remains undocumented, though it may descend from Golden Reinette and Grimes Golden" (S28). PPQ (Quebec growers, S13): "Introduite en 1916, elle serait issue d'un croisement entre Grimes Golden et Golden Reinette." (note PPQ says 1916; Stark says 1914 — both are association/company statements). (A.H. Mullins / Odessa WV detail and any West Virginia statute — not confirmed; UNVERIFIED item 3.) |
| Grown in Canada? | **Yes, small and declining.** Ontario: "Golden Delicious (October to May)" (S1); 431 acres, 2.7%, down 18% from 529 in 2019; 2024 production 9,340,000 lbs; grower price $0.29/lb (S2). OMAFRA 2012 lists Golden Delicious among cultivars in new plantings "because demand for these cultivars is growing" and harvest in October (S5 — a 2012 statement). BC: on list, "Mid to late September", "Long storage in CA" (S6); 25 acres, 404 tons (S7). Quebec: "Délicieuse jaune — Disponibilité : Mi-octobre"; "produite en petite quantité au Québec"; "excellente pour une longue conservation" (S13). |
| Import sources | Separate import line: 7,361,850 lbs in 2024 crop yr (−12%; 5-yr avg 8,437,921); Ontario port 4.92M, BC 2.29M lbs (S2). U.S. 81–86% of all apple imports (S20). |
| Season in Canadian stores | Ontario "October to May" (S1); Quebec "Mi-octobre" (S13); BC harvest mid–late Sept (S6). Washington calendar all year (S28, U.S.). |
| Storage life | Ontario CA regime: O2 1–2%, CO2 1–2%, 0–1 °C, "slow stepwise cooling with 1-MCP" (S4). BC: "Long storage in CA" (S6). **U.S. context:** WSU "11 Months" (S21). |
| Licensing | Public; no CFIA PBR (S15). |
| Own descriptions (attributed, verbatim) | PPQ: "une pomme croquante, à chair blanche crème, juteuse, légèrement acidulée, sucrée et au goût de miel" (S13). WSAC (U.S.): "Golden in every way"; "A crisp yet mellow apple with tender golden skin and a sweet, spiced flavor." (S28). WSU flavour field: "aromatic, balanced, slightly juicy, crisp texture" (S21). |
| 2023–2026 news (Canada) | Ontario acreage −6% year on year, −18% over five years (S2). No other variety-specific item found. |
| Retail price | Voilà 29 Sep 2026: "Delicious Apple Golden 1 Count" $1.39; "Compliments Apples Golden Delicious 1.36 kg" $6.99 ("$5.14 per kilogram" — the cheapest bagged apple line seen); 3-count "$7.69 per kilogram" (S51). |
| **One-line reading** | **Canadian-grown and in season from October (Ontario "October to May"; Quebec mid-October; BC from late September), small and shrinking acreage but imports are also small (7.4M lbs) — a plausibly Canadian October apple; public variety (S1, S2, S6, S13).** |

### 2.5 Pink Lady® (Cripps Pink)

| Item | Finding (with source) |
|---|---|
| Breeder / origin | **Western Australia; cultivar 'Cripps Pink'; brand owned by Apple and Pear Australia Limited.** WSU (U.S.): origin "Australia", "Golden Delicious x Lady Williams", harvest "Late" (S21). WSAC: "Australia … crossing Golden Delicious and Lady Williams" (S28). APAL's own site (S31): "PINK LADY® IS A BRAND, NOT A VARIETY"; "Over 30 years, Pink Lady® has evolved from the world's first branded apple"; "The Pink Lady® trademark is owned by Apple and Pear Australia Limited (APAL), and managed globally by Twenty Degrees"; "sold in over 120 countries". **Breeder John Cripps, the 1973 cross at the WA Department of Agriculture, and the 1998 trademark assignment to APAL's predecessor could not be opened at tier (a)** (APAL history page behind a bot-check; WA government page 404) — UNVERIFIED item 4. |
| Grown in Canada? | **Yes in BC; marginal in Ontario.** BC: BCTFPG lists "Cripps Pink (Pink Lady®)", "Late October to early November", storage "Good with promising CA potential and long shelf life" (S6); BC Ministry: "Pink Lady" 229 acres (5th-largest BC apple by area; Similkameen 145, South Okanagan 74), 5,164 tons (S7), plus "Cripps Pink" named among the seven "all others" varieties (S7). Ontario: "Cripps Pink" only in OAG "Other Cultivars" footnote (S2); OMAFRA 2012: "Cripp's Pink require higher heat accumulations" (S5). Quebec, NS: not listed. |
| Import sources | No separate import line ("Unspecified"). Washington (U.S.) markets Cripps Pink "November through July" (S28). Chile: Pink Lady 89,011 t + Cripps Pink 27,249 t forecast exports 2026 (B9). APAL: "Only licensed exporters can supply Pink Lady® apples to licensed importers" (S31). |
| Season in Canadian stores | BC harvest late Oct–early Nov (S6). Washington window Nov–Jul (S28, U.S.); Chile "late January through November" (S48). |
| Storage life | BC: "Good with promising CA potential and long shelf life" (S6). Not in Ontario CA table (S4). **U.S. context:** WSU "10 Months" (S21). |
| Licensing | Cultivar 'Cripps Pink' has **no CFIA PBR record** in the apple list (S15); the **brand** Pink Lady® is a licensed trademark of APAL, managed by Twenty Degrees; "reserved for the exclusive use of licensees" (S31). Who holds the Canadian licence was not established (UNVERIFIED item 5). |
| Own descriptions (attributed, verbatim) | APAL (S31): "From the audible crunch and burst of juice to the fizz sensation on your tongue, Pink Lady® apples invigorate your tastebuds"; "Early to blossom and late to harvest, Pink Lady® apples are sun ripened longer, delivering a perfectly balanced flavour wrapped in a rosy-blush colour."; "At Harvest time, Pink Lady® apples are hand-picked in several 'waves'". WSAC (U.S.): "A sweet-tart in pink"; "Sweet-tart flavor with a firm, crisp bite"; "high sugar, high acid with an effervescent finish" (S28 — tasting words only). |
| 2023–2026 news (Canada) | B4 (28 Apr 2025) names Pink Lady among varieties discussed in the retailer "one or two U.S. varieties" context. S7 (Jul 2026): BC Pink Lady 229 acres / 5,164 tons. |
| Retail price | Voilà 29 Sep 2026: "Apple Pink Lady Large 1 Count" $1.39; 3-count "$8.80 per kilogram" (S51). |
| **One-line reading** | **A branded (licensed-trademark) apple that IS grown in BC (229 acres, harvest late Oct–early Nov) but not meaningfully in Ontario/Quebec — in early October it is imported storage fruit (Washington/Chile); by November BC fruit exists — read the label (S6, S7, S28, S31).** |

### 2.6 Cosmic Crisp® (WA 38)

| Item | Finding (with source) |
|---|---|
| Breeder / origin | **Washington State University; cross 1997 by Bruce Barritt; Enterprise × Honeycrisp; launched December 2019.** WSU Foundation (S25, 1 Dec 2019): Barritt "took pollen from the Honeycrisp and placed it on the stigma of the Enterprise" in 1997; Kate Evans took over the program on Barritt's retirement in 2008; "boxes of the WA 38 hybrid apple will arrive in grocery stores nationwide this December" (2019). U.S. patent PP24,210 (S26): granted 4 Feb 2014; inventor Bruce H. Barritt; assignee Washington State University; cross of 'Enterprise' × 'Honeycrisp'; evaluation orchard Douglas County, WA. PVM (S27): "Seeds were germinated in 1998 at Washington State University's Tree Fruit Research and Extension Center in Wenatchee", evaluation 2001–2008. WSAC: "Place of origin: Washington State. Parents: Honeycrisp and Enterprise" (S28). CFIA record lists breeder as "Bruce H. Barritt, Okanagan Centre, British Columbia" (S15 — breeder's stated address at application). |
| Grown in Canada? | **No.** PVM (S27): "Cosmic Crisp is exclusively available in Washington State for all of North America"; WSU FAQ (S24): "Trees are available to all Washington State growers from 2018 forward"; "Washington State grown fruit can be marketed anywhere in the world"; tree royalty "$1.00 USD", production royalty "4.75% of total box sale price if the price is at or above $20.00 USD". B1 (Capital Press, 29 Apr 2026): WSU extended Washington exclusivity for WA 38 "through May 26, 2032" (from 2027); WSU's Jeremy Tamsen: "WSU recognizes that the Washington apple industry's involvement in 'WA 38' continues well beyond its initial commercialization"; "22 million Cosmic Crisp trees" in Washington; ">$8 million in royalties" FY2025. B2 (FreshFruitPortal, 18 May 2026): exclusivity "grants exclusive North American cultivation rights to Washington state". Not on any Canadian association list (S1, S6, S10, S14). |
| Import sources | **Washington, U.S. only** for North America (S27). PVM: importers "must obtain a valid Import License from the commercialization company", "valid for the term of a crop year and must be renewed annually" (S27). B2: Canada listed among markets with "particularly strong traction"; volumes 3.7M 40-lb boxes (2021) → ~8M (2023) → ~11.7M (2024); "Licensed in 12 markets outside North America". No separate Canadian import line (Unspecified). |
| Season in Canadian stores | Washington calendar: all 12 months (S28, U.S.). Harvest is "late" (CFIA description: "late fruit harvest and eating maturity", S15). |
| Storage life | **U.S. context only:** patent "loses little of its crispness, sugar, and acid following five months of storage" (S26); WSU Foundation "In controlled-atmosphere storage, its crunch lasts into the next harvest", "can stay fresh for a year in storage" (S25); WSU storage study at "33°F", "1% carbon dioxide, and 2% oxygen", fruit held ~5–6 months (S23); PVM: "flavor and texture is maintained even through storage" (S27). Not in Ontario/BC tables. |
| Licensing | **Club/managed; Washington-only production.** CFIA PBR 'WA 38' cert. 6655, granted 2022-09-08, terminates 2047-09-08, holder WSU (S15) — i.e., the cultivar is protected in Canada even though no Canadian grower is licensed. |
| Own descriptions (attributed, verbatim) | PVM (licensor): "The Cosmic Crisp® apple is remarkably crisp, sweet, sharp, tangy, and unbelievably juicy." (S27). WSAC (U.S.): "Out of this world crunch"; "A large, juicy red apple that's explosively crunchy and bursting with plenty of sweetness"; "dark red skin scattered with bright lenticels like stars" (S28). |
| 2023–2026 news (Canada) | B1 (29 Apr 2026) and B2 (18 May 2026): exclusivity to 2032; Canada a strong export market. B4 (28 Apr 2025): Cosmic Crisp named in The Grower's discussion of Washington's push into Canada amid retailers cutting to "one or two U.S. varieties". S18/S19: no counter-tariff on apples (Sept 2026 list has no chapter 08). |
| Retail price | Voilà 29 Sep 2026: "Apple Cosmic Crisp 3 Count" "$6.20 typically", "$10.34 per kilogram" — the highest $/kg of any apple in the searches (S51). |
| **One-line reading** | **Never Canadian: a WSU club apple that only Washington growers may plant in North America until at least 26 May 2032, so every Cosmic Crisp on a Canadian shelf is a U.S. import — and it was the priciest apple line seen ($10.34/kg at Voilà) (S15, S24, S27, B1, S51).** |

### 2.7 Envy™ (Scilate)

| Item | Finding (with source) |
|---|---|
| Breeder / origin | **New Zealand; cross made 1985 at Havelock North; Royal Gala × Braeburn; launched 2008.** U.S. patent PP20,477 (S32): inventor Allan G. White; "The cross was made in 1985 on trees located on land at Havelock North, Hawkes Bay, New Zealand"; granted 10 Nov 2009. CFIA (S15): breeder "Allan White, Havelock North, New Zealand"; holder The New Zealand Institute for Plant and Food Research Ltd. T&G (S38, 19 Nov 2025): "Developed in 1985 by scientists at the Bioeconomy Science Institute" (the successor body), cross of 'Royal Gala' and 'Braeburn'; "ENVY™ (Scilate): Launched in 2008"; 8,000 seeds, ~7,000 planted, 211 trees selected for advanced testing. T&G (S43): "The first ENVY™ apple was sold in 2009". |
| Grown in Canada? | **No Canadian growing found.** T&G: "grown in 13 locations across both hemispheres and sold in more than 55 countries" (S43); "grown in over 13 countries" (S38) — countries not named. Not on any Canadian association list. B6 (RNZ/Farmers Weekly, 8 Jul 2025): Envy and Jazz "sold in Canada were imported from southern hemisphere locations and Washington State". |
| Import sources | **Washington State (U.S.) and Southern Hemisphere (NZ; Chile per T&G's "Americas" share).** B6: T&G's Shane Kingston: "around 31 percent of T&G's apple supply is sourced from Aotearoa New Zealand, 37 percent from the Americas, 23 percent from Europe and the United Kingdom, and 9 percent from other markets." Canadian 2.27 kg (5 lb) bags carried "prominent 'product of USA' labelling" (B6). No separate Canadian import line (Unspecified). |
| Season in Canadian stores | Washington window "October through March" (S28, U.S.); NZ new-season Envy shipments begin in April (S41, 2 Apr 2025). Together these imply near-year-round presence, U.S. fruit in autumn–winter and NZ fruit from spring. |
| Storage life | Patent (NZ breeder, U.S. record): "Stores well in air cool storage (0±0.5° C.) for at least 3 months" (S32). No Canadian or WSU duration found. WSAC notes it is "slow to brown" when sliced (S28, U.S.). |
| Licensing | **Club.** T&G "the exclusive global licence holder of both varieties" (S38); "ENVY™ is a registered trademark of the T&G Global Group of Companies" (S45). CFIA PBR 'Scilate' cert. 5843, granted 2018-11-16, terminates 2043-11-16 (S15). |
| Own descriptions (attributed, verbatim) | ENVY™ site (T&G): "Beautifully balanced sweetness, Delightfully satisfying crunch, Uplifting fresh aroma, Naturally radiant – slices stay white longer" (S45). T&G 2020: "a large apple with bright red skin, a fantastic crisp crunch, and a perfectly balanced sweet flavour" (S44). CFIA description: "The fruit size of 'Scilate' is large to very large … The time of maturity of the fruit for eating is very late" (S15). WSAC (U.S.): "a very sweet, low acid apple that's slow to brown" (S28). |
| 2023–2026 news (Canada) | B6 (8 Jul 2025): Canadian consumer backlash against U.S.-labelled goods caught Envy/Jazz bags labelled "product of USA"; Kingston said "sales met expectations". S39 (15 Apr 2026): Envy "surpassed NZD $1 billion in global retail sales"; U.S. household penetration "from 7.2% in 2023 to 12.4% in 2025" (no Canada figure). S40 (16 Dec 2025): Envy grower returns +$0.70/TCE; context of "U.S. import tariffs". S47 (Oppy, 29 Mar 2022, pre-window context): Envy "not widely distributed in Quebec". |
| Retail price | Voilà 29 Sep 2026: "Envy Apples 908 g" $6.99 ($0.77/100 g = $7.70/kg); "Envy Apple 3 Count" "$9.90 per kilogram" (S51). |
| **One-line reading** | **Not Canadian: a T&G club apple with no Canadian growers found, sold here as Washington fruit in autumn–winter and NZ/Southern-Hemisphere fruit from spring (bagged "product of USA" in 2025), at $7.70–$9.90/kg; Canadian PBR protects the cultivar to 2043 (S15, S38, S41, S45, B6, S51).** |

### 2.8 JAZZ™ (Scifresh)

| Item | Finding (with source) |
|---|---|
| Breeder / origin | **New Zealand; same 1985 Royal Gala × Braeburn cross family at Havelock North; launched 2004.** T&G (S38): "JAZZ™ (Scifresh): Launched in 2004"; breeder programme at Plant & Food Research (now Bioeconomy Science Institute). CFIA (S15): breeder "Allan White, Havelock North, New Zealand"; holder Plant and Food Research. T&G (S42): "First exported from New Zealand in 2004". T&G (S43): "First launched in 2004"; "Over 120,000 tonnes of JAZZ™ apples are harvested each year"; "grown in 13 locations across both hemispheres and sold in more than 45 countries". (U.S. patent PP13,888 page could not be opened — UNVERIFIED item 6.) |
| Grown in Canada? | **No commercial Canadian growing found.** CFIA record contains BC trial observations ("In the Okanagan valley, 'Scifresh' matures more than one week before 'Braeburn' and more than two weeks before 'Fuji BC2'") — evidence of DUS testing in BC, not of commercial production (S15). Not on any Canadian association list. B6: Jazz sold in Canada came from "southern hemisphere locations and Washington State". |
| Import sources | Washington (U.S.) and Southern Hemisphere (B6). NZ: ~2 million cartons/36,000 t exported in the 2022 NZ season to "Europe, the United Kingdom, North America and Asia" (S42). No Canadian import line. |
| Season in Canadian stores | Washington window "September through July" (S28, U.S.); NZ season shipments from late March (S42, 2022). |
| Storage life | No published duration obtained (Ontario/BC tables silent; WSU page not opened; patent unavailable). CFIA: "mid to late season" maturity (S15). |
| Licensing | **Club (trademark), but the Canadian PBR on the cultivar has run out:** CFIA 'Scifresh' (trade name Jazz) cert. 2990, granted 2007-11-16, **Grant of Rights Termination Date 2025-11-16** (S15). JAZZ™ site: "grown under license from ENZA"; "registered trademark of the T&G Group of Companies" (S46). |
| Own descriptions (attributed, verbatim) | JAZZ™ site (T&G): "Bursting with crisp, tangy-sweet flavour"; "Perfectly sized and just right for on the go" (S46). T&G (S42): "distinctive tangy-sweet flavour and refreshing taste profile". Oppy/T&G's Susanne Bertolas (S47): "The deliciously crisp and tangy-sweet flavor of JAZZ™, combined with its size, makes it the perfect snack". CFIA description: overcolour "bright orange red" (S15). WSAC (U.S.): "this apple has a strong bite and an almost floral aromatic flavor" (S28). |
| 2023–2026 news (Canada) | B6 (8 Jul 2025): "product of USA" labelling backlash. S40 (16 Dec 2025): Jazz grower returns "up $4.24 per average TCE"; sales volumes +32%; "number one imported apple brand in Japan". S15: Canadian PBR expired 16 Nov 2025. |
| Retail price | Voilà 29 Sep 2026: "Jazz Apple 3 Count" "$4.61 typically", "$7.69 per kilogram" (S51). |
| **One-line reading** | **Not Canadian: a T&G-licensed New Zealand-bred apple with no Canadian growers, imported from Washington (autumn–winter) and the Southern Hemisphere (spring–summer); its Canadian Plant Breeders' Right expired 16 Nov 2025, but the JAZZ™ trademark remains T&G's (S15, S42, S46, B6).** |

### 2.9 SweeTango® (Minneiska)

| Item | Finding (with source) |
|---|---|
| Breeder / origin | **University of Minnesota; controlled cross 1988, Honeycrisp × Minnewashta (Zestar!); seedling selected 1999; patented 2008; sold from 2009.** U.S. patent PP18,812 (S33): inventors David S. Bedford and James J. Luby; "controlled cross made in 1988 between 'Honeycrisp' (female parent) and 'Minnewashta' (male parent)"; seedling found in 1999 as "Tree 46 in Row 23 of Block 86 at Excelsior, Minn."; granted 13 May 2008. UMN (S34): "in 2009, a limited supply of SweeTango® Minneiska apples became available to consumers"; "SweeTango® (Minneiska variety) is a registered trademark owned by the Regents of the University of Minnesota"; "typically ripens in early September". CFIA (S15): breeders David Scott Bedford and James J. Luby; holder Regents of the University of Minnesota. |
| Grown in Canada? | **Yes — Nova Scotia (confirmed); Quebec (government statement).** NSFGA lists "SweeTango®" among Nova Scotia apples (S14). SweeTango's own grower directory lists "Scotian Gold Cooperative Ltd.", Annapolis Valley, Nova Scotia — "55 member growers managing over 2,500 acres" with 22 named member farms (S36); SweeTango is "available during apple season across the United States and Canada" (S35). Quebec government (S10, 5 Mar 2026): consumers increasingly seek "la Gala, la Honeycrisp, la SweeTango et l'Ambrosia", which "sont généralement importées" but which "les producteurs d'ici ajoutent maintenant à leurs vergers". Ontario: not on OAG list; not in "Other Cultivars" footnote (S1, S2). BC: not listed (S6, S7) — but the CFIA agent in Canada is Summerland Varieties Corp., B.C. (S15). |
| Import sources | Washington (U.S.) markets SweeTango "August through November" (S28); NBT growers span "from Nova Scotia to Washington state" (search-result paraphrase — see UNVERIFIED item 7 for the un-opened page). No Canadian import line. |
| Season in Canadian stores | Early-season apple: UMN "typically ripens in early September" (S34, Minnesota); WSAC Aug–Nov (S28, U.S.); "a seasonal apple harvested in early fall" (S35). No Canadian association date found for NS/QC harvest (UNVERIFIED item 7). |
| Storage life | Patent (U.S. breeder): "storage life of 90 to 120 days in common storage (34 to 37° F.)" — "an unusually long storage life for an early ripening variety" (S33). Not in Canadian tables. |
| Licensing | **Club/managed.** UMN: "introduced as a 'managed variety'"; growers outside Minnesota must be "licensed members of the growers cooperative, Next Big Thing (NBT)" (S34). SweeTango (S35): NBT is "A group of the best apple growers selected to cultivate SweeTango. Only these select members can grow SweeTangos". CFIA PBR 'Minneiska' cert. 4979, granted 2015-01-12, terminates 2033-01-12; agent in Canada Summerland Varieties Corp. (S15). |
| Own descriptions (attributed, verbatim) | NSFGA (Nova Scotia growers): "sweet and tart flavour combination" (S14). UMN (breeder): "juicy and sweet with hints of fall spices", "crisp texture", "vibrant acidity" (S34). SweeTango site: "The harmony of Honeycrisp and Zestar! varieties blossoms into SweeTango's distinguished flavor—and exceptional texture to match." (S37). WSAC (U.S.): "One part sweet, one part tangy … a fun loud crunch and big flavor" (S28). |
| 2023–2026 news (Canada) | Quebec government page (5 Mar 2026) on SweeTango entering Quebec orchards (S10). No bylined 2023–26 Canadian SweeTango news item found. |
| Retail price | Voilà 29 Sep 2026: "SweeTango Apple 3 Count" "$4.61 typically", "$7.69 per kilogram" (S51). |
| **One-line reading** | **Canadian-grown under licence (Nova Scotia's Scotian Gold co-op is a Next Big Thing grower; Quebec growers adding it) and an early-season apple (early-September ripening; U.S. window Aug–Nov), so early October is inside its short 90–120-day window — a club apple whose Canadian PBR runs to 2033 (S14, S15, S33, S34, S36).** |

|---|---|---|---|---|
