## UNVERIFIED / DO-NOT-USE

Everything below could not be confirmed at tier (a)/(b)/(d) on 29 Sep 2026. Do not use as fact; pointers are given for follow-up.

1. **Fuji breeding dates and station (cross 1939; Tohoku Research Station, Fujisaki, Aomori; named/registered 1962).** Found only in Wikipedia/aggregator snippets (tier c). NARO's English NIFTS pages (https://www.naro.go.jp/english/laboratory/nifts/apple/index.html → 404; https://www.naro.go.jp/english/laboratory/nifts/index.html opened but has no Fuji history) do not carry it. Tier-(a) support that exists: WSU and WSAC (Japan; Red Delicious × Ralls Janet; "Named for Fujisaki, Japan"). Pointer: NARO Japanese-language cultivar page for 'ふじ', or Aomori Prefectural Industrial Technology Research Center apple pages.
2. **Red Delicious first-seedling year (c. 1870s–1880) and the "rejected by nurserymen" detail.** Stark Bro's page gives only the 1893–94 fair story; the earlier date is Wikipedia/aggregator only. Use S29's 1893/1894 dates and "Hawkeye" name only.
3. **Golden Delicious discovery detail (A.H. Mullins, Odessa/Clay County, seedling c. 1905–1912) and any West Virginia state-fruit statute.** Stark Bro's page confirms only the 1914 introduction; https://code.wvlegislature.gov/2-2-12/ opened but that section is unrelated. PPQ says "Introduite en 1916" (S13) vs Stark's 1914 — do not reconcile; cite each as that body's statement. Pointer: West Virginia Legislature code search for "state fruit"; Stark Bro's "Golden Delicious" product/history pages.
4. **Pink Lady / Cripps Pink breeding history (John Cripps; 1973 cross at the WA Department of Agriculture; 1998 trademark assignment from DAFWA to the Australian Apple and Pear Growers Association).** APAL's history page https://apal.org.au/birth-apals-pink-lady-business/ and https://apal.org.au/industry-info/pink-lady/ returned Cloudflare "Just a moment…" (403); WA government page https://www.agric.wa.gov.au/pome-fruit/pink-lady-apple → 404; Austrade article fetched a different page; Wayback is not reachable from this environment. Tier-(a) support that exists: APAL's brand site (trademark owner; "30 years"; Twenty Degrees management) and WSU/WSAC (Australia; Golden Delicious × Lady Williams). Pointer: reopen APAL in a browser; DPIRD WA "Pink Lady story".
5. **Who licenses Pink Lady® for Canada (Pink Lady America LLC or another licensee) and whether Canadian-grown Cripps Pink is sold under the brand.** https://www.pinkladyamerica.org/ returned an empty JS shell. Pointer: pinkladyapples.com "Pink Lady® Near You" (JS map) and Pink Lady America's site in a browser.
6. **U.S. Plant Patent PP13,888 'Scifresh' (Jazz) text and any storage statement.** Google Patents returned 503/404 on three URL forms; USPTO PDF endpoint returned 403. The CFIA record (S15) is the primary record used instead. Pointer: https://patents.google.com/patent/USPP13888P2/en in a browser.
7. **SweeTango Canadian season dates ("Canadian SweeTango season beginning October 1st and running through Halloween"), the "60-member cooperative … from Nova Scotia to Washington state" description, and Quebec growers by name.** Search snippets attributed these to sweetango.com press pages and a 2010 CBC story; the press URL https://sweetango.com/about/for-the-media/sweet-spot-apple-season-stretches-nationwide/ and https://www.sweetango.com/about/our-growers/ now redirect to a game page; no Quebec grower appears in the current sweetango.com grower sitemap (only Scotian Gold is Canadian). Quebec growing rests on the Quebec government sentence (S10). Pointer: Scotian Gold (https://www.scotiangold.com/ returned an HTTP 202 JS shell); PPQ; sweetango.com "meet a grower" posts.
8. **Whether Envy or Jazz is grown anywhere in Canada.** T&G names "13 locations" without listing countries; no Canadian association lists either; the CFIA 'Scifresh' record shows Okanagan trial data only. Treat as "no Canadian commercial growing found", not "never grown". Pointer: T&G Global (VentureFruit) licensing pages; BC Tree Fruits successor packers.
9. **Variety-level import volumes for Fuji, Pink Lady, Cosmic Crisp, Envy, Jazz, SweeTango, and any month-by-month or country-by-variety split.** Statistics Canada's classification used by OAG/OMAFA has lines only for Honeycrisp, Gala, Golden Delicious, Granny Smith, Idared, McIntosh, Red Delicious and "Unspecified" (S2, S3); CIMT monthly data (HS 0808.10) were not retrieved. Pointer: Statistics Canada CIMT web application (HS 080810, by country and month); AAFC InfoHort storage holdings.
10. **Chile → Canada and New Zealand → Canada apple volumes by variety.** Fruits from Chile gives only "North America 11.0%" (S48); NZAPI gives ">80 countries" (S49); FreshFruitPortal 23 Sep 2026 (no byline, F1) has no Canada line. Country totals exist only in the U.S. government report (S20). Pointer: Frutas de Chile/ASOEX season-end reports; NZAPI "export statistics"; StatCan CIMT.
11. **Washington Apple Commission Canada activity and the Produce News item "Washington Apple Commission focusing exports on Canada and Mexico".** https://theproducenews.com/washington-apple-commission-focusing-exports-canada-and-mexico → 403/Cloudflare; https://www.washingtonapples.ca/ did not resolve. Pointer: waapple.org "International" pages.
12. **Envy 2025 "domestic season" launch across the U.S. and Canada, first New York-grown Envy (AndNowUKnow, "ANUK staff", 2025).** https://www.andnowuknow.com/… → 403. T&G's own news index (https://tandg.global/news/) lists no Canada-specific Envy release for 2025–26. Pointer: T&G North America / Oppy news pages.
13. **Retail prices at Metro, Loblaws/Real Canadian Superstore, Food Basics, Super C, Walmart, Save-On-Foods, Longo's.** All returned 403 / human-verification pages (Metro group: "please confirm that you are human"; Loblaws/Longo's: Akamai "Access Denied"; Walmart: "press and hold"; Save-On-Foods: Cloudflare). A search snippet gave Metro Fuji "$6.59 per kg ($2.99 per lb)" — **not opened; flag only**. Voilà prices (S51) were obtained but without a selected delivery area and without origin. Pointer: open each retailer's flyer in a browser and record date, banner, region, $/lb, $/kg and origin as displayed.
14. **Ontario/OMAFRA month-figure storage-life table by variety (regular cold vs CA).** Only the CA-regime conditions table (S4) exists at tier (a); https://www.ontario.ca/page/apples-storage-recommendations → 404. Pointer: DeEll, J.R. 2020 (book chapter cited in S4); OMAFA ONfruit postharvest posts.
15. **BC Tree Fruits closure coverage from CBC (https://www.cbc.ca/lite/story/1.7277309 and the full URL) → 403.** Country Life in BC (B7) is the bylined source used. Do not cite CBC.
16. **Cosmic Crisp consumer-facing site text (https://cosmiccrisp.com/ and /orchards/, /about/) — JS-rendered, returned empty (HTTP 202).** PVM (S27), WSU (S24, S25) and WSAC (S28) are used instead.
17. **T&G "Our brands" statement that "The first ENVY™ apple was sold in 2009" vs T&G's 40-year release "Launched in 2008".** Both are T&G's own pages (S43 vs S38); cite whichever you use as T&G's statement and note the discrepancy rather than choosing.
18. **BC report Table 8 heading year.** S7 labels the production table "2026" while the report's own methodology text says "The last year of data available is 2026 for acreage and 2025 for production." Quote the figures with the table title as printed and this caveat.
19. **Tier-(c) pages encountered and NOT used as sources (flag only):** Wikipedia (Fuji, Granny Smith, Red Delicious, Golden Delicious, Cripps Pink, Cosmic Crisp, Envy, Jazz, SweeTango), orangepippin.com, specialtyproduce.com, pomiferous.com, grokipedia, adamapples blog, goodfruitguide.co.uk, yoursourceone.com, FreshPlaza items without bylines, FreshFruitPortal items without bylines (F1, F2), flyer-aggregator sites, nursery catalogue pages, "The Plain Truth"/Lee Reich blogs, NewsBreak.

# Canadian retail apple prices by variety — retailers' own channels, 27–29 Sep 2026

Compiled 29 Sep 2026 (all pages opened 29 Sep 2026 between 01:24 and 01:38 UTC, i.e. the evening of 28 Sep 2026 Eastern). Every price below was read from a retailer's own website, app-backend or same-day storefront. No Flipp, PriceListo, Reddit, blog or aggregator data is used. Unit prices marked "computed" are price ÷ package weight; 1 lb = 0.45359237 kg. Prices are CAD. Nothing in this file is a statement about health, nutrition, taste, texture or food safety.

Scope note (hard rule 5): the only banners whose own channels could actually be opened were Voilà by Sobeys, Loblaws / Real Canadian Superstore / No Frills (via Loblaw's PC Express product API, since the loblaws.ca web pages return 403), Walmart Canada (mobile user-agent only), Giant Tiger, and Costco Same-Day. Metro, Sobeys/Safeway/Foodland/FreshCo/IGA/Thrifty flyers, Save-On-Foods, Longo's, Farm Boy, Food Basics, Super C, Maxi, Provigo, Calgary Co-op and costco.ca warehouse pricing could not be opened and are in the UNVERIFIED section.

### 2.2 Loblaws (loblaws.ca / PC Express) — 33 listings

Source: Loblaw's PC Express product-search API (https://api.pcexpress.ca/pcx-bff/api/v1/products/search), which is the backend that loblaws.ca, realcanadiansuperstore.ca and nofrills.ca call; the public web pages returned 403 to every direct request. Store IDs were resolved to addresses via the same API's pickup-locations endpoint. "Farmer's Market", "PC Organics", "President's Choice" and "No Name" are Loblaw own brands as returned in the brand field. Loose apples are "SOLD_BY_EACH_PRICED_BY_WEIGHT": the API returns a per-apple estimate and a $/kg and $/lb comparison price; the table uses the $/kg figure the retailer shows. Deal expiry dates are as returned (2026-09-30 = the current flyer week).

### 3.3 Benchmark — Statistics Canada average retail price, apples per kilogram (Table 18-10-0245-01)

Source opened: https://www150.statcan.gc.ca/n1/tbl/csv/18100245-eng.zip (full table CSV, 2026-09-29 01:27 UTC) and the table page https://www150.statcan.gc.ca/t1/tbl1/en/tv.action?pid=1810024501 (release date shown: 2026-09-02). Table title: "Monthly average retail prices for selected products". Product line: "Apples, per kilogram". Latest reference month available: July 2026 (end reference period 2026-07-01 in the metadata).

| Geography | Jul 2025 ($/kg) | Sep 2025 ($/kg) | Jul 2026 ($/kg) |
|---|---|---|---|
| Canada | 6.18 | 4.68 | 6.45 |
| Newfoundland and Labrador | 7.09 | 6.61 | 7.58 |
| Prince Edward Island | 6.82 | 6.11 | 7.19 |
| Nova Scotia | 6.73 | 6.14 | 7.33 |
| New Brunswick | 6.72 | 6.17 | 7.23 |
| Quebec | 5.75 | 4.63 | 6.01 |
| Ontario | 6.23 | 4.41 | 6.33 |
| Manitoba | 6.24 | 4.87 | 6.80 |
| Saskatchewan | 6.29 | 4.88 | 6.94 |
| Alberta | 6.33 | 5.11 | 6.97 |
| British Columbia | 6.32 | 4.99 | 6.87 |

Canada monthly series, "Apples, per kilogram" (same file): 2025-05 6.06; 2025-06 6.19; 2025-07 6.18; 2025-08 6.17; 2025-09 4.68; 2025-10 4.40; 2025-11 4.81; 2025-12 5.34; 2026-01 5.55; 2026-02 5.65; 2026-03 5.62; 2026-04 5.76; 2026-05 6.17; 2026-06 6.33; 2026-07 6.45.

StatCan note 1, verbatim from the metadata file: "Average price estimates for these products are derived using transaction data from Canadian grocery retailers. The composition of a given product category may change over time due to evolving consumer purchasing patterns and product availability. Therefore, users should exercise caution when comparing average prices over time as average prices may not be fully comparable from one month to another and should not be used as a representative measure of pure price change through time. Note: Data are subject to revision."

Reading the benchmark against section 2: the July 2026 Canada figure ($6.45/kg) is a blended average across all varieties and pack types. In the listings captured on 28–29 Sep 2026 (the start of the domestic harvest, when StatCan's series has historically dropped, e.g. Sep 2025 $4.68), bagged Ontario/Quebec-season varieties (McIntosh, Paula Red, Cortland, Empire, Gala 4–5 lb bags) sit mostly at $2.2–$4.5/kg, while loose Honeycrisp, Cosmic Crisp, Granny Smith and Pink Lady sit at $6.6–$8.8/kg. This is a description of the captured listings only, not a StatCan finding.

The only origin text displayed anywhere in the captured channels: (a) Loblaw banners, product description for "Ambrosia Apples" (Farmer's Market, 1.814 kg) and "Honeycrisp Apples, 4 lb Bag" (Farmer's Market, 1.81 kg): "Hand-picked and grown in Quebec" — counted as Canada at each store where the item was listed; (b) Costco Same-Day, "Organic Gala Apple Bag" 3 lb, features text "Made in Canada." Voilà defines a "Country Of Origin" product field but its value did not render server-side; Walmart, Giant Tiger and the Loblaw API expose no origin field. No listing displayed U.S., Chile, New Zealand or South Africa origin. Consequently the origin split the video wants cannot be supported from these channels as of 29 Sep 2026 — see UNVERIFIED.

## UNVERIFIED / DO-NOT-USE

Everything below could not be confirmed at tier (a)/(b)/(d) on 29 Sep 2026. Do not use in script or on screen without re-checking.

1. Metro online prices and Metro flyer apple items — metro.ca online grocery returned 403 on every attempt (desktop, mobile, WebFetch); the flyer page is a client-side embed. No Metro price exists in this file. Pointer: https://www.metro.ca/en/online-grocery/search?filter=apples ; https://www.metro.ca/en/flyer
2. Sobeys, Safeway, Foodland, FreshCo, IGA, Thrifty Foods weekly flyers — all 403. Voilà prices (section 2.1) are the only Empire/Sobeys data. Pointer: https://www.sobeys.com/en/flyer/ and sibling URLs in the access log.
3. Save-On-Foods — 403 on search, category, flyer and home page. No Western-Canada Pattison price exists in this file. Pointer: https://www.saveonfoods.com/sm/pickup/rsid/1982/results?q=apples
4. Longo's — 403 on every URL. Pointer: https://www.longos.com/search?text=apples
5. Farm Boy prices and weekly flyer — the flyer URLs redirect to a JavaScript challenge; the site search lists no fresh produce. Only the retailer blog post opened (no prices). Pointer: https://www.farmboy.ca/weekly-flyer-specials/
6. Food Basics, Super C (Metro discount banners) and Maxi, Provigo (Loblaw Quebec banners) — 403. No Quebec-banner price other than Costco Same-Day (Montréal default) exists in this file.
7. Costco warehouse (in-club) apple prices — costco.ca search renders client-side and returned no fresh apples; only Costco Same-Day (Instacart-operated delivery pricing, which Costco states may differ from warehouse pricing) was captured. Also: the Same-Day pages' LD-JSON markup declares priceCurrency "USD" while the storefront is Canadian and shows "$"; treated as CAD but flag before quoting. Pointer: https://sameday.costco.ca/store/costco-canada/products/21090933-honeycrisp-apple-5-5-lbs
8. Loblaws / Superstore / No Frills web pages — every public page returned 403. Prices came from Loblaw's own PC Express product API (api.pcexpress.ca), the backend those sites use, with the site's public key and explicit store IDs. It is the retailer's own channel, but an editor should confirm the prices in the app or store before broadcast, and note that "deal" prices carry expiry 2026-09-30.
9. Giant Tiger — records are tagged in_store_only:true and the product page says "Online prices may vary from in-store prices."; province tags come from product data, not from a displayed region selector. Pointer: https://www.gianttiger.com/products/granny-smith-apples-3-lb-6
10. Walmart Canada — prices are for store 1061 (Mississauga ON), Walmart's default for an anonymous visitor, and were only obtainable with a mobile user-agent after the desktop page served a bot check. No other Walmart store was checked. Pointer: https://www.walmart.ca/en/search?q=apples
11. Voilà region — the catalogue served carried no postal code/region; the Voilà regionId in the page is an opaque identifier. Treat Voilà prices as "Voilà default catalogue, region not shown".
12. Voilà country of origin — the product template defines a "Country Of Origin" field but the value is loaded client-side and did not render; no Voilà listing has a verified origin. Pointer: https://voila.ca/products/apples-royal-gala-bag-1-36-kg/72449EA
13. Voilà "Lil Snappers Organic Apples Honeycrisp 1.36 kg" — the title says 1.36 kg but the size field shows 907 g and the unit price shown ($1.21/100 g) matches 907 g; the $/kg in this file uses 907 g. Confirm pack weight before quoting.
14. Origin split Canada vs U.S. vs Chile vs New Zealand vs South Africa — NOT SUPPORTABLE from these channels: only 10 of the captured listings display any origin (all Canada/Quebec). Any statement about where a variety on Canadian shelves "comes from" needs shelf photos, CFIA-compliant labels or StatCan/Global Affairs import data (not pulled here).
15. Brand/packer attributions (BC Tree Fruits, Algoma, Martin's, Pommes Qualité Québec, Scotian Gold, Stemilt, Rainier, Washington) — none of the retailer listings captured displays a packer name except "Lil Snappers" (Stemilt brand, per Stemilt's own page) and "Envy" (T&G Global, per its own page). Do not attribute any listing to a packer.
16. Cosmic Crisp breeder/licensor statement, Ambrosia origin statement, Pink Lady ownership statement, Scotian Gold and Pommes Qualité Québec pages — sites did not open (challenge pages / connection failures). Pointers: https://www.cosmiccrisp.com/ ; https://ambrosiaapples.ca/ ; https://www.pinkladyapples.com/ ; https://www.scotiangold.com/ ; https://www.qualitepommesquebec.com/
17. Ontario Apple Growers body text — curl returned the page skeleton and headlines only; the WebFetch reader hit a verification screen. Only the headline "Crisp, sweet and local: apple season arrives in Ontario" is confirmed as present on the page (and it is a headline, not a factual claim to reuse).
18. University of Minnesota Honeycrisp release year — not stated on the page opened; do not quote a year.
19. StatCan release date 2026-09-02 — read from the table page metadata; the CSV files are dated 2 Sep 2026. Next release (August 2026 reference month) had not appeared as of 29 Sep 2026.
20. Any Voilà/Walmart "1 Count" or "Sold in singles" listing $/kg — for Walmart the $/kg is the retailer's own ¢/100 g figure; for Voilà "1 Count" items no weight is shown, so no $/kg is given. Do not compute one from an assumed apple weight.
21. Retailer product descriptions (e.g. "crisp", "sweet", "tart") appearing in Loblaw and Walmart data — these are retailer marketing text and are NOT reproduced in this file except the origin phrase "Hand-picked and grown in Quebec"; hard rule 8 applies.

# BC Tree Fruits Cooperative collapse and the BC apple industry, 2023–2026

Research file for the "8 Apple Varieties Sold in Canada To NEVER EAT Again" episode. Business / legal / statistical dossier only.

**House-rule compliance note.** This file contains no health, nutrition, antioxidant, sugar, glycemic, pesticide-residue, vitamin or food-safety-*quality* claims. (The only "food safety" references below are administrative: which *certificate* a grower must hold to ship to a packer; nothing is said about the fruit itself.) Nothing from the February 2026 nutrition video is reused. No taste or texture is asserted as fact; where a breeder, licensor, packer or association describes a variety, it is quoted and attributed. Every legal item is labelled (order / application / allegation / finding / ruling / reported). No wrongdoing is stated or implied by this file.

**Date all URLs were opened: 29 September 2026** unless a row says "could not open" (those items are repeated in the mandatory UNVERIFIED / DO-NOT-USE section at the end).

**Tier key.** (a) primary = government statistics, regulator/court records, the company's / association's / breeder's / licensor's own page or release. (b) named outlet with byline and date. (c) aggregator / Wikipedia / review / Reddit / SEO = flag only, never a source. (d) named consumer survey with publisher, year, methodology (none used in this file).

---

## 2. The cooperative and the wind-down announcement (July 2024)

| Fact | Verbatim / detail | Source (tier) | URL opened |
|---|---|---|---|
| Founding & structure | "The organization was established in 1936 to promote British Columbia's tree fruits, beginning with apples…" (co-op site, now under new ownership) | (a) BC Tree Fruits "About Us" | https://www.bctreefruits.com/about/about-us/ |
| Membership 2023 | "BC Tree Fruits Cooperative comprises over 330 local families producing apples, cherries, pears, peaches, nectarines, apricots, prunes, plums, and table grapes." (The Grower, 6 Jul 2023, Karen Davidson) | (b) | https://thegrower.org/news/bc-tree-fruits-cooperative-invests-oliver-packing-house |
| Board decision | On July 25, 2024 the Board determined that "due to extremely low estimated fruit volumes, weather effects, and difficult market and financial conditions, the cooperative will not be able to effectively operate the business moving forward." Co-op statement: "This is a very unfortunate situation, and we are working with our lenders and advisors as we embark on this process. We respectfully ask for your patience as we navigate through the process." Contact named: Laurel Van Dam. (FreshPlaza, 5 Aug 2024, no byline) | (b) | https://www.freshplaza.com/north-america/article/9646877/bc-tree-fruits-will-be-shutting-down-operations/ |
| Letter to members | "It is with profound sadness that we must inform you that BC Tree Fruits Collective [sic] will not be receiving fruit beginning today, Friday, July 26, 2024." (Global News, dpotenteau, 26–27 Jul 2024) | (b) | https://globalnews.ca/news/10646108/farmers-stunned-as-bc-tree-fruits-announces-surprise-closure |
| Letter to members | Co-op advised growers to "immediately search for another alternative to market your fruit for the balance of the 2024 season." 330 member growers; ~40 packing houses in the province, BCTF the largest; packed 70 million lb of apples the prior year; "up to 30 million pounds of apples" lacked an outlet. (Country Life in BC, Peter Mitham & Tom Walker, 31 Jul 2024) | (b) | https://www.countrylifeinbc.com/bc-tree-fruits-shuts-down/ |
| Co-op VP quote | Laurel Van Dam, VP grower relations: "This is unfortunate and unchartered waters for us all. We are working with our advisors, banks and legal experts as we embark on this process." (Penticton Herald, Susan McIver, 31 Jul 2024) | (b) | https://www.pentictonherald.ca/news/article_1b447324-4f5f-11ef-b8ef-b758341501a1.html |
| Volume collapse (grower/ex-director) | Mike Mitchell: "The projected volume was only in the 30,000-bin range when they needed to be upward of 70,000 plus." (Penticton Herald) ; "There's probably 300 growers looking for a place to find a home for their fruit… we haven't settled up from last year's crop." (Global News) | (b) | as above |
| Reaction, Oliver | Mayor Martin Johansen: "It came out of nowhere. Nobody I know had any word that anything was going on there." (Times Chronicle, Roy Wood, 1 Aug 2024) | (b) | https://www.timeschronicle.ca/surprise-co-op-closure-surrounded-by-mystery/ |
| 2024 crop (from petition, as reported) | "85-90% decline in cherries, zero production in other stone fruits, and approximately 50% reduction in projected apple volumes" (FreshPlaza, 13 Aug 2024, summarising the CCAA filing) | (b) | https://www.freshplaza.com/north-america/article/9649074/bc-tree-fruits-files-for-creditor-protection/ |

Note on 2022–2023 background (context only): Country Life in BC reported on 7 Sep 2022 (Peter Mitham) that the co-op held engagement sessions on "consolidating packing operations in Oliver and selling assets in Kelowna and Lake Country" — https://www.countrylifeinbc.com/co-op-meets-with-growers/ (b). The 1 Oct 2020 Country Life piece (Tom Walker) reported the co-op "sells over $100 million worth of apples annually" and had closed its Osoyoos plant (2017) and downtown Kelowna offices — https://www.countrylifeinbc.com/bc-tree-fruits-prepares-to-sell-assets-apples/ (b).

(representative Anthony Joseph Tillman, 925 West Georgia St, Suite 902, Vancouver). **Date of Initial Order:** 2024-08-13. Debtor head office: 3335 Sexsmith Road, Kelowna. Status on the OSB page: monitor discharge date shown as "—".

Quoted by Summerland Review (Brennan Phillips, 13 Feb 2026): "burdensome, harsh, and wrongful"; "a visible departure from the standards of fair dealing and an abuse of power"; "there is no explanation for why the current members are seeking to pass the resolution excluding former members…beyond that the current members believe that they are entitled to the whole." Penticton Herald (Ron Seymour, 20 Feb 2026): "Simply put, (former members) have a claim for a share of the surplus funds under the current Rule 125 that the current members, by special resolution, seek to remove." Order: bar the meeting; declare resolution unfairly prejudicial; receiver to distribute per Rule 125. Surplus estimated $12–15M; 178 current / 180 former members; 68% / 32%; former members' share ≈ $4M (Herald: current ≈ $8.5M). Distribution keyed to tonnage shipped in final six years. Herald notes current members "may appeal to BC Court of Appeal" (no appeal located). **This is a civil ruling on co-op rules; it is not a finding of wrongdoing against any person.** | (b) ×5 | https://summerlandreview.com/2026/02/13/attempt-to-cut-out-ex-bc-tree-fruits-members-abuse-of-power-court-rules/ ; https://www.pentictonherald.ca/business_news/article_728d907b-6f2a-4cec-9f13-48df78a7f70a.html ; https://www.kelownadailycourier.ca/news/article_eaa9f27b-d4ab-462d-a7ea-c629763406b0.html ; https://www.freshplaza.com/north-america/article/9812070/british-columbian-court-orders-payout-to-former-b-c-tree-fruits-members/ |

Judge's first name: The Grower, Penticton Herald, Kelowna Daily Courier, FreshPlaza and Global News write "Miriam Gropper"; Summerland Review writes "Mariam". Use "Justice Gropper". No neutral citation (e.g., 2026 BCSC ___) was located on any opened page — see UNVERIFIED.

## 8. Orchard removals / acreage and production — official statistics

### 8a. Statistics Canada, Table 32-10-0364-01 "Area, production and farm gate value of marketed fruits" — British Columbia, fresh apples
Downloaded full-table CSV (a): https://www150.statcan.gc.ca/n1/tbl/csv/32100364-eng.zip (table landing page: https://www150.statcan.gc.ca/t1/tbl1/en/tv.action?pid=3210036401 ; metadata end reference period 2025; The Daily release for 2025 data dated 16 Feb 2026: https://www150.statcan.gc.ca/n1/daily-quotidien/260216/dq260216c-eng.htm ). Farm gate value scalar = thousands of dollars.

| Year | Cultivated area (acres) | Bearing area (acres) | Marketed production (tonnes) | Farm gate value ($000) |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 2019 | 9,500 | 8,760 | 104,018 | 61,955 |
| 2020 | 9,443 | 8,157 | 91,423 | 58,783 |
| 2021 | 9,210 | 7,736 | 83,344 | 57,395 |
| 2022 | 8,914 | 7,454 | 75,426 | 56,380 |
| 2023 | 8,713 | 7,227 | 67,936 | x (suppressed) |
| 2024 | 8,549 | 7,029 | 60,759 | 52,139 |
| 2025 | 8,213 | 6,324 | 52,411 | 47,823 |

Computed from the table: cultivated area 2019→2025 −13.5%; bearing area −27.8%; marketed production −49.6%. The Daily (16 Feb 2026) national line: "Production declined for lowbush blueberries (-12.6%) and apples (-9.7%)." and "Fruit producers in British Columbia reported a strong rebound in 2025, following the severe cold snap that affected the Okanagan and Similkameen valleys in 2024." (the rebound sentence refers to fruit overall; BC sweet cherries "+319.1%… to a record high 26 million kilograms").

### 8b. BC Ministry of Agriculture and Food, *2025 Tree Fruit Acreage and Production Report* (July 2026) — production-insurance data (covers "approximately 85-90% of tree fruit acres")
(a) https://www2.gov.bc.ca/assets/gov/farming-natural-resources-and-industry/agriculture-and-seafood/animal-and-crops/crop-production/2025_tree_fruit_acreage_and_production_report.pdf

Verbatim executive summary points:
- "The insured area devoted to tree fruits in British Columbia has increased around 1,600 acres in the past decade, reaching 14,250 acres in 2026."
- "Over that time, B.C. tree fruit acreage has shifted decisively from apples toward cherries, with cherries becoming the largest tree fruit planted by about 2023."
- "The industry is consolidating around a smaller number of commercially successful varieties, particularly Staccato and Sentennial cherries, Ambrosia and Gala apples."
- "Despite production losses in 2024 due to extreme weather, 2025 shows a strong recovery, especially for cherries."
- "As noted, apple acreage has seen a steady decline over the past decade, though production trends remain far more stable than for cherries."
- Farmgate receipts for tree fruits: "$163 million in 2025".

| Insured apple producers | 2022 | 2023 | 2024 | 2025 | 2026 |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Count (Table 1) | 438 | 425 | 417 | 415 | 412 |

Insured apple acreage by region, 2026 (Table 3): Central Okanagan 2,447; Creston 25; North Okanagan 522; Rest of province 45; South Okanagan 1,118; Similkameen 1,525; **Total 5,682 acres**. Insured apple production 2025 (Table 4): **95,422 tons** (Central Ok. 41,518; Similkameen 26,522; South Ok. 17,647; North Ok. 9,292).

Regional notes (verbatim): North Okanagan "reached a high of 1,100 insured acres in 2021… since experienced a steady decline to around 520 acres in 2026, led by large decreases in Gala and Ambrosia." Similkameen "climbed steadily to around 1,600 acres in 2023; despite decreasing 5% since then, it continues to be an important production region." South Okanagan: "the decline of apple acreage in general, the decline of Ambrosia and Gala in particular."

Top insured apple varieties 2026 (Table 7, acres): Ambrosia 2,274; Gala 1,631; Honeycrisp 599; Mac 325; Pink Lady 229; **Spartan 183**; Granny Smith 112; Red Del 61; **Sunrise 57**; Fuji 55; Gold Del 25; **Salish 18**; All others 110 (report says the remainder comes mostly from "Cripps Pink, Gingergold, Cider, Jonagold, **Nicola**, Orin and **Aurora**"). Production 2025 (Table 8, tons): Ambrosia 41,649; Gala 24,095; Honeycrisp 10,650; Pink Lady 5,164; Mac 4,636; Spartan 3,143; Sunrise 1,042; Salish 198.

Note: StatCan "cultivated area" (8,213 ac, 2025) and the insured-acreage series (5,682 ac, 2026) are different measures (all farms vs insured farms; different definitions) — do not splice them.

### 8c. Other acreage / land-value data points
- FCC *Review of 2025 Fruit land values* (13 May 2026, Judith Francoeur): "we noted an average decrease in land values of 6.6% across the Okanagan region" (orchards, 2025); vineyards "declined by 20% in 2025". (a-ish: Crown corporation analysis) https://www.fcc-fac.ca/en/knowledge/economics/2025-fruit-land-price-trends
- Enhanced Replant: ~809 ha (2,000 ac) of all perennial crops removed/replanted in 2024 (all crops, not apples only) — §6.
- Ministry "Tree fruits" page: "About 15,000 acres cultivated"; "Approximately 400 commercial growers" (all tree fruits). §6.
- **U.S. context only:** USDA FAS *Fresh Deciduous Fruit Annual* (Canada) — page could not be opened (403); search snippet says BC apple acreage "will likely continue to decline… but is projected to remain flat for MY 2024/25". UNVERIFIED.

## 11. Prices (rule 5 filter)

No opened page supplies a retailer's own dated shelf price with banner, region, unit and origin. Grower-attributed retail figures exist only as quotes and must be presented as such, never as price facts:
- Deep Brar (Vancouver Sun via Yahoo, 31 Aug 2026): "They're selling our apples for $4.99 a pound, and I'm getting 30 cents" — no store, date or origin label given.
- iNFOnews (28 Aug 2025): growers "receiving approximately 50 cents per pound while retailers charge $2.50–$3.50" — no store or date.
- Country Life (1 Jun 2022): Deep Brar received "18 cents per pound for Galas" vs production cost >30¢/lb — grower return, 2022.
All three → UNVERIFIED as prices; usable only as attributed grower statements.

# Apples and produce origin in Canada, 2023–2026: the documented consumer, regulatory and media record

Compiled 29 Sep 2026. Every URL below was opened on 29 Sep 2026 unless stated otherwise. Tier labels: **(a)** primary (government statistics, regulator pages, company/association's own site or release); **(b)** named outlet with byline and date; **(c)** aggregator/SEO/trade-aggregator/review/social — flag only, never a source; **(d)** named consumer survey with publisher, date and methodology. Legal/regulatory items carry an explicit label (allegation / regulator finding / penalty / warning / company statement). Nothing here concerns health, nutrition, residues or food safety, and nothing from the February 2026 video is reused.

Search-engine access was exhausted early in this session; everything below was reached by opening URLs directly (WebFetch or curl). Pages that could not be opened are listed in the final section.

---

## 1. CFIA: country-of-origin and "Product of Canada" / maple-leaf enforcement, 2025–2026

### 1.1 The rules as CFIA states them (tier a)

| Item | Verbatim / fact | Source (opened 29 Sep 2026) |
|---|---|---|
| "Product of Canada" definition | "all or virtually all major ingredients, processing, and labour used to make the food product are Canadian"; non-Canadian content generally under 2% (spices, additives, vitamins, etc.) | CFIA, Origin claims on food labels, Date modified 2023-12-06 — https://inspection.canada.ca/en/food-labels/labelling/industry/origin-claims |
| "Made in Canada" | used when "the last substantial transformation of the product occurred in Canada, even if some ingredients are from other countries"; requires a qualifier such as "Made in Canada from domestic and imported ingredients" | same page |
| Maple leaf | "depending on how a maple leaf is used, it could imply a 'Product of Canada' claim"; the stylized 11-point maple leaf requires Canadian Heritage permission; an accompanying domestic-content statement near the leaf is recommended | same page |
| 14 Mar 2025 notice to industry (updated 30 Jul 2025) | "We have seen an increase in complaints related to origin claims on bulk produce, on food labels and in advertisements." / "If a food business chooses to use the maple leaf on packaging, retail shelves or online, the CFIA recommends that an accompanying domestic content statement…be placed in close proximity to the maple leaf" / "The CFIA is reinforcing its commitment to a transparent and trustworthy market and will take the appropriate enforcement action to protect Canadians from misleading claims if non-compliance is found." | CFIA, Notice to industry – The importance of accurate use of Product of Canada, Made in Canada and other origin claims — https://inspection.canada.ca/en/food-labels/labelling/notice-industry-2025-03-14 |

### 1.2 CFIA complaint and non-compliance counts (chronological)

| Period / date | Figure | Label | Source |
|---|---|---|---|
| Nov 2024–Jan 2025 vs Feb 2025 | "After a total of five complaints made between November and January, 23 complaints were made in February alone." CFIA also said it had seen "an increase in complaints related to country of origin claims on food labels or in advertisements" | CFIA statement to CBC | CBC News, Jenna Benchetrit, 12 Mar 2025 — https://www.cbc.ca/lite/story/1.7475346 |
| 5 Mar 2025 | CFIA "says it's seen an increase in complaints about items mislabelled as products of Canada"; "too early" to tell if there has been non-compliance; no complaints received about "made in Canada" or "100 per cent Canadian" misuse at that date | CFIA statement to CP | The Canadian Press, Tara Deschamps, first published 5 Mar 2025 (carried by St. Albert Gazette) — https://www.stalbertgazette.com/quebec-news/complaints-about-food-mislabelled-as-products-of-canada-rising-cfia-10328851 |
| Feb–Mar 2025 | "The CFIA saw a significant increase in complaints about origin claims starting in February 2025, from 1 or 2 complaints per month, to 25 complaints in February and 33 in March." / "In February 2025, the 'Buy Canadian' movement gained popularity. This led to interest in Canadian content claims on food labels and advertisements (such as Product of Canada and Made in Canada)." / 2024-25 overall: "over 380 consumer or industry complaints identifying concerns related to food misrepresentation or product advertising" | CFIA annual report (a) | CFIA, Food Fraud Annual Report 2024 to 2025, Date modified 2026-07-20 — https://inspection.canada.ca/en/about-cfia/science-and-research-cfia/our-research-and-publications/food-fraud-report-2025 |
| Nov 2024–mid-Jul 2025 | 97 complaints related to country-of-origin claims; of 91 investigated, violations found in 29 (32%). "Most involved bulk produce sold in stores, and in each case the problem was fixed, according to the agency." | CFIA data to CBC; regulator findings, corrected, no penalties | CBC News, Sophia Harris, 24 Jul 2025 — https://www.cbc.ca/lite/story/1.7590956 |
| Feb–May 2025 complaints | 12 cases of "maple washing" identified; "all but one involved national grocery chains"; no fines; 27 violations detailed from complaints to end of May; "In seven of the cases, in-store signage advertised produce as a 'Product of Canada' — a claim that didn't match the fine print. In another five cases, a maple leaf was used on in-store labelling to promote imported products like condiments and coffee." / 2025 to date: 160 complaints, 41% filed mid-July–August; 40 cases identified as violations | CFIA data to CBC; regulator findings, no fines | CBC News, Sophia Harris, 1 Sep 2025 (updated 2 Sep 2025) — https://www.cbc.ca/lite/story/1.7621843 |
| 1 Nov 2024–25 Feb 2026 | "it identified 78 violations related to country-of-origin claims on food labels or in advertisements at retail stores." CFIA: "Grocery retailers have had sufficient time to ensure correct signage." | CFIA statement to CBC | CBC News, Sophia Harris, 17 Mar 2026 — https://www.cbc.ca/news/business/loblaw-fine-canadian-9.7130933 |
| 1 Apr 2025–16 Mar 2026 | 104 country-of-origin labelling non-compliances identified; five stores penalized totalling $47,000; remaining cases addressed through letters, licence suspensions or cancellations | CFIA statement | CP24, Alex Arsenych, 20 Mar 2026 — https://www.cp24.com/local/toronto/2026/03/20/2-grocery-stores-in-ontario-fined-10k-each-for-mislabelling-some-food-items-as-canadian/ |
| Since 1 Apr 2025 (as of 16 Mar 2026) | "$47,000 in fines since last April over 'misleading' Product of Canada labels." CFIA: "Canadians have been clear that they want to support Canadian businesses and buy Canadian products. Consumers deserve origin labels they can trust so they can make informed choices." | CFIA statement | Global News, Aaron D'Andrea, 20 Mar 2026 — https://globalnews.ca/news/11739168/maple-washing-canada/ |
| 2025 calendar year | "CFIA identified 75 instances of non-compliance in 2025"; complaints "spiked more than eight-fold in the first three months of 2025"; "$47,000 in fines on five businesses" since spring 2025 | Globe reporting of CFIA data | The Globe and Mail, Kate Helmore and Susan Krashinsky Robertson, 18 Mar 2026 — https://www.theglobeandmail.com/business/economy/article-fines-for-false-made-in-canada-claims-could-chill-investment-food/ |
| Since start of 2025 (as of 14 May 2026) | "the CFIA has identified 127 cases where retailers promoted imported food as Canadian. But so far, the agency has issued only two fines" | CFIA data to CBC | CBC News Investigates, Sophia Harris, 14 May 2026 — https://www.cbc.ca/news/investigates/sobeys-loblaw-maple-washing-9.7196767 |

### 1.3 Enforcement actions on record (CFIA Administrative Monetary Penalties list, tier a)

CFIA AMP list, Date modified 2026-09-28 — https://inspection.canada.ca/en/inspection-and-enforcement/actions-taken/administrative-monetary-penalties. All entries below are under the Safe Food for Canadians Act (SFCA) s. 6(1). The list itself gives no product description; descriptions come from CFIA statements to media (cited).

| Date served | Company (as listed) | Region | Type | Amount | What CFIA told media (label: regulator statement) |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 2025-04-17 | The Real Canadian Superstore Ltd. | West | Warning | $0 | No media description located |
| 2026-01-15 | Real Canadian Superstore (store #1033) | Ontario | Penalty | $10,000 | Toronto (Gerry Fitzgerald Dr.) store displayed President's Choice broccoli slaw with "maple leaf advertising decals" and a "Product of Canada" statement on a shelf tag; packaging said "Product of USA." CFIA: "This created a product advertisement that is misleading to consumers regarding the origin of the product." (CBC, Sophia Harris, 20 Feb 2026 — https://www.cbc.ca/news/business/superstore-imported-canadian-food-fine-9.7099827 ; CBC, Sophia Harris, 26 Feb 2026 — https://www.cbc.ca/news/business/loblaw-superstore-fine-buy-canadian-imported-food-broccoli-slaw-9.7106279 ; Global News, Ariel Rabinovitch, 19 Feb 2026 — https://globalnews.ca/news/11672603/real-canadian-superstore-fined-product-of-canada/ ) |
| 2026-01-15 | 1000717809 Ontario Limited (Fortinos store #7920) | Ontario | Penalty | $10,000 | Fortinos, Queens Plate Drive, Toronto: Président-brand Rondelé cheese spread (made in France) displayed with an 11-point maple leaf on the shelf tag. CFIA: "The product display created an impression that the product was made in Canada." (CBC, 17 Mar 2026, URL above) |
| 2026-02-20 | Loblaw Companies Limited (two entries) | Atlantic | Warning | $0 | Per CBC 14 May 2026: a Superstore in Nova Scotia promoted Mexican bell peppers with signage "grown in Canada from your farmers" and California green onions as domestic; a Dominion store in Newfoundland and Labrador displayed U.S. President's Choice cocktail tomatoes with a maple leaf and "product of Canada" claim (CBC, 14 May 2026, URL above) |
| 2026-03-11 | Metro Ontario Inc. | Ontario | Warning | $0 | No media description located |
| 2026-03-19 | Sobeys Capital Incorporated (IGA CO-OP) | Atlantic | Warning | $0 | No media description located |

Other CFIA statements on enforcement posture (regulator statements): fines are chosen "based on a range of considerations" including risk factors, compliance history and "responsiveness to resolving the issue" (CBC, 1 Sep 2025); maximum AMP under SFCA is $15,000 per violation; $10,000 is classified as "very serious" (CBC, 17 Mar 2026; Global News, 20 Mar 2026). CFIA on the Sobeys head-office advertising investigation (May 2026): "The probe resulted in no fines because 'corrective actions' were taken, the CFIA said in an email." (CBC, 14 May 2026) — label: regulator investigation closed without penalty, per CFIA.

## 2. Competition Bureau, 2023–2026 (tier a unless noted)

| Date | Item | Verbatim / facts | Source |
|---|---|---|---|
| 27 Jun 2023 | Retail Grocery Market Study report "Canada Needs More Grocery Competition" | Four recommendations: "Canada needs a Grocery Innovation Strategy…"; "Federal, provincial, and territorial support … should encourage the growth of independent grocers and the entry of international grocers…"; "Provincial and territorial governments should consider introducing accessible and harmonized unit pricing requirements."; "…take measures to limit property controls in the grocery industry, which could include banning their use." Gross margins of the largest grocers "generally increase by a modest yet meaningful amount over the last five years" (about $1–2 per $100). Commitment: "Revisit the findings of this study in three years to assess progress on recommendations made to government." | https://competition-bureau.canada.ca/en/how-we-foster-competition/education-and-outreach/canada-needs-more-grocery-competition |
| 5 Mar 2025 | Bureau on Made-in-Canada complaints (non-food) | Marianne Blondin: "As the Bureau is required by law to conduct its work confidentially, I am unable to provide information regarding complaint volumes or trends related to Made in Canada or Product of Canada claims, specifically." | CP, Tara Deschamps, 5 Mar 2025 (URL in 1.2) |
| 14 May 2026 | Bureau on maple-washing (food) | spokesperson said the bureau "can" become involved; noted not all its work is public; encouraged complaints | CBC, 14 May 2026 (b) |
| 16 Jun 2026 | News release: "Competition Bureau to examine competition across Canada's food supply chain" | Interim Commissioner Jeanne Pratt: "The cost of food matters to all Canadians, and strong competition can help keep prices in check. Our examination builds on our earlier work in the retail grocery sector and will look at all parts of the food supply chain." Three focus areas: production and processing; transportation and distribution; retail pricing practices "including loyalty programs, pricing algorithms, shrinkflation and skimpflation." Not a formal market study; not a law-enforcement investigation. Public input deadline 4 Sep 2026; final report spring 2027. | https://www.canada.ca/en/competition-bureau/news/2026/06/competition-bureau-to-examine-competition-across-canadas-food-supply-chain.html |
| 16 Jun 2026 → | Examination page "Behind the price tag: An examination of the food supply chain" | Scope includes "harvesting, production, processing, logistics and transportation"; stakeholder roundtables and market calls "Fall 2026 to Spring 2027"; report "Spring 2027"; page references a call for information on minimum advertised pricing policies in grocery dated 28 Sep 2026 (that separate notice not opened). Produce/fresh fruit not named specifically. | https://competition-bureau.canada.ca/en/how-we-foster-competition/behind-price-tag-examination-food-supply-chain |
| — | Notice page | "The period to provide submissions through our online form ended on September 4, 2026."; timeline June–July 2026 outreach; Fall 2026–Spring 2027 roundtables; Spring 2027 report | https://competition-bureau.canada.ca/en/how-we-foster-competition/notice-behind-price-tag-examining-food-supply-chain |
| 16 Jun 2026 | Globe coverage (b) | Pratt: "The price at checkout starts a long time before the product gets on the shelves." Kim Furlong (Retail Council of Canada): "80 per cent of the cost of a typical item being sold at retail is built in before it hits store shelves." Marie-Claude Bacon (Metro): "Food affordability extends well beyond the retail sector and requires a comprehensive look." Critics: Joshua Krane (MLT Aikins): "This is about as low-impact an approach as you can do."; Mike von Massow: "This is so unfocused." | The Globe and Mail, Kate Helmore and Susan Krashinsky Robertson, 16 Jun 2026 — https://www.theglobeandmail.com/business/economy/article-competition-bureau-launches-study-to-examine-how-food-supply-chain/ |

## 3. Grocery Code of Conduct (Canada Grocery Code / OGSCC) — status and relevance to produce suppliers

| Date | Fact | Source |
|---|---|---|
| 1 Jan 2026 | Code fully implemented; Dispute Resolution Management Process (DRMP) operational; annual dues collection begins. Signatories: "Costco Wholesale Canada Ltd., Empire Company Limited, Loblaw Inc., Metro Inc., and Wal‑Mart Canada Corp"; over 150 additional retailers and suppliers registered. Karen Proud (President and Adjudicator): "This level of participation is a significant advancement for the voluntary Code." | OGSCC release, 5 Jan 2026 (a) — https://canadacode.org/news/the-canada-grocery-code-now-fully-implemented/ |
| 1 Jan 2026 | CBC: code covers "grocers, suppliers, wholesalers and primary producers"; Proud: "It would be great if we had a [silver bullet] that could fix food inflation. It's really not the purpose of the code … The code is aimed at improving the relationships between suppliers and retailers." | CBC News (files from Paula Duhatschek and Jenna Benchetrit), 1 Jan 2026 (b) — https://www.cbc.ca/news/business/canada-grocery-code-of-conduct-jan-1-9.7031468 |
| 15–16 Dec 2025 | Global News: Per Bank (Loblaw CEO): "The code now is fair, and it will not lead to higher prices"; von Massow: "This is going to be invisible to consumers" | Global News, Uday Rana, 15 Dec 2025 (b) — https://globalnews.ca/news/11578652/canada-grocery-code-of-conduct-2026/ |
| FAQ | Code applies to "all entities involved in the commercial sale of grocery products in Canada", including primary producers; tiered dues from $50 (under $50K sales) to $35,000 (over $1B) | https://canadacode.org/resources/faqs/ (a) |
| 16 Jun 2026 | OGSCC Spring Report (1 Jan–31 May 2026). Membership by class as of 31 May 2026: "Class A – Primary Producers: 14 members; Class B – SME Manufacturers (≤ $350M Annual Sales): 91 members; Class C – Large Manufacturers (> $350M Annual Sales): 36 members; Class D – Non-Affiliated Wholesalers/Distributors: 17 members; Class E – Independent Retailers (≤ $1B Annual Sales): 35 members; Class F – Large Retailers (> $1B Annual Sales): 7 members." "Class A (Primary Producers) participation remains an important area of focus for future recruitment efforts." Produce-specific: "In April 2026, the OGSCC established the Fruits and Vegetables Primary Producer Liaison Group in recognition of the unique characteristics and commercial realities of this sector", composed of "fruit and vegetable grower associations across Canada, including national and provincial associations as well as Class A fruit and vegetable growers", to "develop a list of systemic and recurring Code-related issues faced by produce growers across Canada in their commercial dealings with retailers". Nine concerns submitted via the Issue Centre in the first five months; the Office notes the Issue Centre is an intelligence tool, not a bilateral dispute process; a webinar (14 Apr 2026) covered how the OGSCC and the Fruit and Vegetable Dispute Resolution Corporation (DRC) work together. | OGSCC Spring Report 2026 PDF (a) — https://canadacode.org/wp-content/uploads/2026/06/OGSCC-Spring-Report-2026-Final-Web.pdf ; release — https://canadacode.org/news/ogscc-releases-spring-report-on-early-implementation-of-the-canada-grocery-code/ |
| 30 Jun 2026 | Independent Chair appointed: Cathy Loblaw (release states she has no affiliation with Loblaw Companies Limited) | https://canadacode.org/news/office-of-the-grocery-sector-code-of-conduct-announces-appointment-of-independent-chair/ (a) |
| 24 Sep 2026 | Association Portal launched | https://canadacode.org/ (a) |

## 4. Grower associations: Ontario, Québec, B.C. and national — public statements 2023–2026

### 4.1 Joint letter to the Prime Minister (Feb 2025)

Reported by The Grower, Karen Davidson, 16 Feb 2025 (b) — https://thegrower.org/news/canadian-apple-growers-support-tariffs-us-grown-imports . Signatories: Éric Rochon (Producteurs de pommes du Québec), Peter Simonsen (President, BC Fruit Growers' Association), Chris Hedges (Chair, Ontario Apple Growers), Jeffrey Walsh (Vice-Chair, Nova Scotia Fruit Growers' Association), Apple Growers of New Brunswick. Verbatim, as reproduced by The Grower:
- "We support strategic action to defend Canadian industries when necessary."
- "Canadian apples are grown commercially in five provinces and have a farm gate value of $265 million. Our growers produce high-quality fruit to supply the Canadian and global markets, with the capacity to displace U.S. imports."
- "A tariff on apples would have a significant economic and political impact in the United States. While the majority of American apples are grown in just six states, apples are produced commercially in at least 30 states."
- "53 per cent of all U.S. apple exports are shipped to Canada and Mexico."
The associations say tariffs are "not productive for either country" but support including apples in retaliatory measures.

Seasonal pattern (Canada): the series dips each autumn at harvest (Oct 2024 $4.13/kg; Oct 2025 $4.40/kg) and peaks in early summer (Jun–Jul). Food Price Data Hub confirms "$6.45" per kilogram for July 2026 — https://www.statcan.gc.ca/en/topics-start/food-price . Table: https://www150.statcan.gc.ca/t1/tbl1/en/tv.action?pid=1810024501

Retailer-displayed prices with origin: the only retailer-site/flyer price meeting rule (5) that could be opened is none; the "$4.99 a pound" figure is a grower's spoken claim (Deep Brar, Vancouver Sun, 31 Aug 2026; banner not named) and must be attributed as such, not as a verified shelf price.

## 8. Media reporting on apple storage age, wax and produce pricing

Searched for CBC Marketplace, Globe and Mail, Canadian Press and Financial Post reporting specifically on apple storage age or wax: none could be opened or located in this session (see UNVERIFIED). Storage-related facts that are on the record from primary/tier-b sources:
- USDA FAS (U.S. gov): Canadian apple storage holdings by variety are tracked monthly Dec–Jun (Figure 2, AAFC Infohort data); B.C. "Access to controlled atmosphere storage continues to be limited in supply" (2 Dec 2025, URL in section 7).
- Chris Hedges (OAG chair): growers would "come into this 2025-2026 crop with what I would say is no inventory" (FreshPlaza, 30 Jun 2025).
- Vancouver Sun (31 Aug 2026): "B.C.'s apple harvest is only beginning: last year's crop sold out months ago, and this year's crop is still a couple of weeks away from store shelves."; the co-op "had packed, stored and sold fruit for about half of the Okanagan's growers."
- PPQ: link "Techniques d'entreposage" exists on the PPQ site (https://producteursdepommesduquebec.ca/outils/techniques-dentreposage) — not opened.

Grocery produce pricing reporting opened: The Globe and Mail, 16 Jun 2026 and 18 Mar 2026 (section 1–2); CBC, 26 Feb 2026 (Loblaw quarterly retail revenue "$16.38 billion" cited in the context of fine size); Vancouver Sun, 31 Aug 2026 (grower-return claim).
